| No global error handler | Stack traces leaked to client, inconsistent error format | `@RestControllerAdvice` + RFC 7807 `ApiError` |
| Breaking change in same version | Existing clients crash (missing field, renamed field) | Version with `/v1/` → `/v2/` or make additive changes only |
| No pagination on list endpoints | OOM on `GET /orders` returning 1M rows | `Pageable` + `Page<T>` / `Slice<T>` with `max-page-size` |
| Missing `Location` header on `POST` | Client can't find created resource URL | Return `201` + `Location: /api/v1/orders/{id}` |
| OpenAPI spec out of sync with code | Docs lie, clients build wrong assumptions | `springdoc` auto-generates from code — annotate controllers, don't maintain hand-written YAML |

### Summary

- REST resources use nouns (`/orders`), HTTP verbs (`GET/POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE`), and **status codes** (200/201/204/400/404/409) — **never return raw JPA entities**; use request/response DTOs.
- **OpenAPI/Swagger** (`springdoc-openapi`) auto-generates machine-readable specs from `@Operation` / `@ApiResponse` annotations → interactive docs + client codegen.
- **Versioning**: URL path (`/api/v1/orders`) is simplest and most explicit; deprecate with `Sunset` header, remove with `410 Gone`.
- **API Gateway** (Spring Cloud Gateway) centralizes auth, rate limiting, circuit breaking, routing, and response caching at the edge.

---

## 217. How do you handle file uploads and downloads in Spring Boot? Explain `MultipartFile`, streaming downloads, S3 storage, and large-file handling.

File upload/download is a common requirement — profile images, CSV imports, PDF generation. Spring Boot provides `MultipartFile` for multipart uploads, `StreamingResponseBody` for streaming large downloads without buffering the entire file in memory, and easy integration with S3/cloud storage for persistence beyond local disk.

---

### 1. Multipart Upload — `MultipartFile`

```yaml
spring:
  servlet:
    multipart:
      enabled: true
      max-file-size: 50MB
      max-request-size: 100MB    # total for all files in one request
      file-size-threshold: 2KB   # files > 2KB written to temp dir (not RAM)
```

```java
@RestController @RequestMapping("/api/v1/files")
class FileUploadController {

    @PostMapping(consumes = MediaType.MULTIPART_FORM_DATA_VALUE)
    ResponseEntity<FileMetadata> upload(@RequestParam("file") MultipartFile file) throws IOException {
        // Validate
        if (file.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalArgumentException("File is empty");
        if (!List.of("image/png","image/jpeg","application/pdf").contains(file.getContentType()))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Unsupported content type");

        // Store (local disk example)
        String filename = UUID.randomUUID() + "_" + file.getOriginalFilename();
        Path dest = Path.of("/data/uploads", filename);
        file.transferTo(dest.toFile());

        return ResponseEntity.ok(new FileMetadata(filename, file.getSize(), file.getContentType()));
    }

    // Multiple files:
    @PostMapping("/batch")
    ResponseEntity<List<FileMetadata>> uploadBatch(@RequestParam("files") List<MultipartFile> files) {
        return ResponseEntity.ok(files.stream().map(this::store).toList());
    }
}

record FileMetadata(String filename, long size, String contentType) {}
```

```html
<!-- Client — form with multipart: -->
<form action="/api/v1/files" method="POST" enctype="multipart/form-data">
  <input type="file" name="file" accept=".png,.jpg,.pdf" />
  <button type="submit">Upload</button>
</form>
```

```java
// Testing with MockMvc:
mockMvc.perform(multipart("/api/v1/files").file(mockMultipart))
       .andExpect(status().isOk());
```

---

### 2. Streaming Download — Avoid Buffering Entire File in Memory

```java
@GetMapping("/{filename}")
void download(@PathVariable String filename, HttpServletResponse response) throws IOException {
    Path file = Path.of("/data/uploads", filename);
    if (!Files.exists(file)) throw new ResourceNotFoundException(filename);

    response.setContentType(Files.probeContentType(file));
    response.setContentLengthLong(Files.size(file));
    response.setHeader("Content-Disposition", "attachment; filename=\"" + filename + "\"");

    // Stream directly from disk to response — no buffering entire file in heap:
    try (OutputStream out = response.getOutputStream()) {
        Files.copy(file, out);
    }
}
```

```java
// For large files (>1GB) — use StreamingResponseBody to stream on a separate thread:
@GetMapping("/stream/{filename}")
 ResponseEntity<StreamingResponseBody> streamDownload(@PathVariable String filename) {
    Path file = Path.of("/data/uploads", filename);
    StreamingResponseBody stream = out -> Files.copy(file, out);
    return ResponseEntity.ok()
        .contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_OCTET_STREAM)
        .header("Content-Disposition", "attachment; filename=\"" + filename + "\"")
        .body(stream);
}
```

`StreamingResponseBody` writes to the output stream on a separate thread — the main request thread is not blocked during the I/O.

---

### 3. S3 / Cloud Storage — Don't Store on Local Disk

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>software.amazon.awssdk</groupId>
    <artifactId>s3-transfer-manager</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```java
@Service
class S3FileService {
    private final S3Client s3;
    private final String bucket = "order-service-uploads";

    S3FileService(S3Client s3) { this.s3 = s3; }

    String upload(MultipartFile file) throws IOException {
        String key = "uploads/" + UUID.randomUUID() + "_" + file.getOriginalFilename();
        s3.putObject(PutObjectRequest.builder().bucket(bucket).key(key)
                .contentType(file.getContentType())
                .build(),
            RequestBody.fromInputStream(file.getInputStream(), file.getSize()));
        return key;
    }

    void download(String key, OutputStream out) {
        s3.getObject(GetObjectRequest.builder().bucket(bucket).key(key).build(), out);
    }

    void delete(String key) {
        s3.deleteObject(DeleteObjectRequest.builder().bucket(bucket).key(key).build());
    }

    // Pre-signed URL — client uploads/downloads directly (no server round-trip):
    String presignedUploadUrl(String key, Duration expiry) {
        return s3.utilities().presign(PresignObjectPresignRequest.builder()
            .signatureDuration(expiry)
            .putObjectRequest(PutObjectRequest.builder().bucket(bucket).key(key).build())
            .build()).url().toString();
    }
}
```

```java
// Stream directly from S3 to HTTP response:
@GetMapping("/s3/{key}")
void downloadFromS3(@PathVariable String key, HttpServletResponse response) throws IOException {
    response.setContentType("application/octet-stream");
    response.setHeader("Content-Disposition", "attachment; filename=\"" + key + "\"");
    s3.getObject(GetObjectRequest.builder().bucket(bucket).key(key).build(), response.getOutputStream());
}
```

**Pre-signed URL flow:**
```
Client → POST /api/v1/files/presign { key: "import.csv", expiry: 300 }
Server → returns presigned S3 PUT URL
Client → PUT directly to S3 (bypasses server — no memory/bandwidth used)
Client → confirms upload → POST /api/v1/files/confirm { key: "import.csv" }
```

---

### 4. CSV Import — Streaming Large Files with Spring Batch / OpenCSV

```java
@PostMapping("/import/csv")
 ResponseEntity<ImportResult> importCsv(@RequestParam MultipartFile file) throws IOException {
    try (CSVReader reader = new CSVReader(new InputStreamReader(file.getInputStream()))) {
        List<String[]> rows = reader.readAll(); // OK for small files (<100K rows)
        // For large files — stream row-by-row with spring-batch or open-csv iterator
    }
}

// Streaming for millions of rows — use Spring Batch (Q???):
// FlatFileItemReader → chunk-oriented step → JPA repository
// Or manually:
try (BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(file.getInputStream()))) {
    String line;
    int count = 0;
    while ((line = br.readLine()) != null) {
        Order order = parseCsvLine(line);
        orderService.save(order);
        if (++count % 1000 == 0) entityManager.flush(); // batch flush
    }
}
```

---

### 5. Large File Handling — Chunked Upload & Resumable Uploads

```yaml
# For files >100MB — chunked upload (e.g., tus protocol or manual chunking):
spring:
  servlet:
    multipart:
      max-file-size: 2GB        # large limit
      max-request-size: 2GB
      file-size-threshold: 0     # always write to disk
```

```java
// Server-side chunked upload handler:
@PostMapping("/upload/chunk")
 ResponseEntity<Void> uploadChunk(@RequestParam String uploadId,
                                  @RequestParam int chunkNumber,
                                  @RequestParam int totalChunks,
                                  @RequestParam MultipartFile chunk) throws IOException {
    Path chunkDir = Path.of("/data/uploads/chunks", uploadId);
    Files.createDirectories(chunkDir);
    Files.copy(chunk.getInputStream(), chunkDir.resolve("chunk_" + chunkNumber));

    if (chunkNumber == totalChunks) {
        assembleChunks(uploadId, totalChunks); // concatenate all chunks → final file
    }
    return ResponseEntity.ok().build();
}

void assembleChunks(String uploadId, int totalChunks) throws IOException {
    Path finalFile = Path.of("/data/uploads", uploadId);
    try (OutputStream out = new BufferedOutputStream(Files.newOutputStream(finalFile))) {
        for (int i = 1; i <= totalChunks; i++) {
            Files.copy(Path.of("/data/uploads/chunks", uploadId, "chunk_" + i), out);
        }
    }
    // cleanup chunks directory
}
```

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `MultipartFile.getBytes()` / `getBytes()` on large file | `OutOfMemoryError` — entire file in heap | Use `transferTo()` or `getInputStream()` streaming |
| No file type validation | Malicious `.exe` uploaded as image | Validate `contentType` + magic bytes, not just extension |
| Storing files on local disk in Kubernetes | Lost on pod restart, not shared across replicas | Use S3 / GCS / Azure Blob (presigned URLs or direct upload) |
| Missing `Content-Disposition: attachment` | Browser displays file inline (XSS risk for HTML) | Always set `attachment` + safe filename |
| Buffering entire download in memory | OOM on 1GB download | Use `StreamingResponseBody` or `Files.copy()` streaming |
| No upload rate limiting | Storage exhaustion / DDoS | Rate limit at gateway (Q216) or use S3 pre-signed URLs with short expiry |

### Summary

- `MultipartFile` handles multipart uploads with `spring.servlet.multipart.max-file-size`; validate content type + magic bytes, and use `transferTo()` to avoid loading the entire file in memory.
- For **downloads**, use `Files.copy(file, response.getOutputStream())` or `StreamingResponseBody` for streaming without buffering — critical for large files.
- Store files in **S3/cloud storage** (not local disk in Kubernetes); use **pre-signed URLs** for direct client↔S3 upload/download (no server bandwidth/memory used).
- Large files: **chunked upload** (split → upload chunks → assemble on server) for >100MB; use **Spring Batch** for CSV import of millions of rows with streaming row-by-row processing.

---

## 218. How do you manage externalized configuration in Spring Boot microservices? Explain profiles, Config Server, Spring Cloud Vault, and 12-factor principles.

Spring Boot externalizes all configuration into properties/YAML files, system environment variables, and command-line args — following the **12-Factor App** principle of strict separation of config from code. In a microservices fleet you need centralized, versioned, and secrets-safe configuration — solved by **Spring Cloud Config Server** (Git-backed) and **Spring Cloud Vault** (dynamic secrets).

---

### 1. Spring Boot Configuration Sources (Priority Order)

```
Lowest → Highest priority:
  1. application.yml (default profile)
  2. application-{profile}.yml (active profile overrides)
  3. application-local.yml / application-prod.yml
  4. System properties (-Dserver.port=8081)
  5. Environment variables (SERVER_PORT=8081)
  6. Config Server (Q218 §3)
  7. Vault (Q218 §4)
```

```yaml
# application.yml — defaults:
server:
  port: 8080
spring:
  profiles:
    active: ${SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE:dev}   # env var overrides

---
# application-prod.yml:
spring:
  config:
    activate:
      on-profile: prod
server:
  port: 8443
logging:
  level:
    root: WARN
    com.example: INFO
```

```bash
# Override via env var (12-factor — works in containers):
export SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
export DATABASE_URL=jdbc:postgresql://db:5432/orders
java -jar app.jar
```

**`@ConfigurationProperties` — type-safe binding:**

```java
@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "app.kafka")
record KafkaConfig(String bootstrapServers, String topic, int retries) {}

// application.yml:
app:
  kafka:
    bootstrap-servers: kafka:9092
    topic: orders
    retries: 3
```

---

### 2. Profiles — Environment-Specific Configuration

| Profile | When active | Use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `dev` | Local development | H2 in-memory DB, DEBUG logging, localhost Kafka |
| `test` | Test suite | Testcontainers, embedded Kafka |
| `staging` | Pre-production | Real infra, 1 replica, WARN logging |
| `prod` | Production | Full replicas, metrics/tracing, ERROR logging |

```yaml
# Profile-specific DB config:
---
spring.config.activate.on-profile: dev
spring.datasource:
  url: jdbc:h2:mem:testdb
  driver-class-name: org.h2.Driver

---
spring.config.activate.on-profile: prod
spring.datasource:
  url: jdbc:postgresql://${DB_HOST}:5432/orders
  hikari:
    maximum-pool-size: 20
    minimum-idle: 5
```

```java
// Conditional bean per profile:
@Configuration
class CacheConfig {
    @Bean @Profile("dev") CacheManager devCacheManager() { return new ConcurrentMapCacheManager(); }
    @Bean @Profile("prod") CacheManager prodCacheManager(RedisConnectionFactory cf) {
        return RedisCacheManager.builder(cf).build();
    }
}
```

---

### 3. Spring Cloud Config Server — Centralized Git-Backed Configuration

```
Git repo (application.yml per service)
      ↓ push
Config Server (Spring Cloud) — reads Git, serves via REST
      ↓ GET /{application}/{profile}
Service instances — pull config at startup, refresh on demand
```

```xml
<!-- Config Server pom.xml -->
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-cloud-config-server</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```java
@SpringBootApplication
@EnableConfigServer
class ConfigServerApp {
    public static void main(String[] args) { SpringApplication.run(ConfigServerApp.class, args); }
}
```

```yaml
# Config Server application.yml:
server:
  port: 8888
spring:
  cloud:
    config:
      server:
        git:
          uri: https://github.com/company/config-repo
          default-label: main
          search-paths: '{application}'
          # repo layout:
          #   config-repo/
          #     order-service.yml
          #     payment-service.yml
          #     application-prod.yml    (shared across all services)
```

```yaml
# Client service — bootstrap.yml (older) or application.yml (Spring Cloud 2021+):
spring:
  application:
    name: order-service
  config:
    import: optional:config-server:http://config:8888  # Spring Boot 2.4+ import
  profiles:
    active: prod
```

```bash
# API:
GET http://config:8888/order-service/prod
→ { "name":"order-service", "profiles":["prod"], "propertySources":[...] }
```

**Dynamic refresh (without restart):**

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-bus-amqp</artifactId>  # or spring-cloud-bus-kafka
</dependency>
```

```java
@RefreshScope  // re-reads properties from Config Server on refresh event
class FeatureFlags {
    @Value("${app.features.new-checkout:false}") boolean newCheckout;
}
```

```bash
# Trigger refresh across all instances via actuator endpoint:
POST /actuator/busrefresh   # RabbitMQ/Kafka bus notifies all instances
```

---

### 4. Spring Cloud Vault — Dynamic Secrets and Rotating Credentials

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-vault-config</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```yaml
# bootstrap.yml:
spring:
  application:
    name: order-service
  cloud:
    vault:
      uri: https://vault:8200
      authentication: TOKEN
      token: ${VAULT_TOKEN}
      kv:
        enabled: true
        default-context: application
        backend: kv
        # Secrets at: vault kv put secret/order-service/db.username=app db.password=secret123
```

```java
// Vault-backed secrets become regular Spring properties:
@Autowired
DataSource dataSource; // spring.datasource.password from Vault — never in Git

// Dynamic database credentials (Vault generates ephemeral user/pass):
spring.cloud.vault:
  database:
    enabled: true
    role: order-service
    backend: database
    # Vault generates: username=v-app-role-abc123 password=XYZ (TTL: 1 hour, auto-rotated)
```

```bash
# Vault setup:
vault secrets enable database
vault write database/config/postgres \
    plugin_name=postgresql-database-plugin \
    connection_url="postgresql://{{username}}:{{password}}@db:5432/orders" \
    allowed_roles=order-service \
    username=superadmin password=supersecret
vault write database/roles/order-service \
    db_name=postgres \
    default_ttl="1h" \
    max_ttl="24h"
```

| Vault feature | Benefit |
| :--- | :--- |
| **KV v2** | Versioned secret store; rollback on bad deploy |
| **Dynamic DB credentials** | Ephemeral user/pass per instance, auto-rotated — no shared password |
| **Transit engine** | Encryption-as-a-service (encrypt/decrypt without managing keys) |
| **AppRole auth** | CI/CD gets short-lived token → wraps to AppRole → no long-lived token in Git |

---

### 5. 12-Factor Configuration Principles

| Factor | Rule | Spring Boot implementation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **III. Config** | Store config in environment, not code | `application.yml` + env vars + Config Server |
| **VI. Processes** | Stateless, share-nothing | Session → Redis (Q210), no local file state |
| **IX. Disposability** | Fast startup, graceful shutdown | `server.shutdown=graceful`, `spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase` |
| **X. Dev/prod parity** | Same infra, different config | Profiles, Config Server, Vault — same binary across environments |

```yaml
# Graceful shutdown (Q211, Q212):
server:
  shutdown: graceful
spring:
  lifecycle:
    timeout-per-shutdown-phase: 30s   # drain in-flight requests before stopping
```

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Secrets in Git (YAML, Dockerfile `ENV`) | Credential leak — Git history is permanent | Use Vault or environment variables; `git-secrets` pre-commit hook |
| Config Server down at service startup | `IllegalStateException: Cannot import config` | Use `optional:config-server:http://...` + retry backoff + fallback to local YAML |
| No profile on `application-prod.yml` | Config loaded unconditionally | Use `spring.config.activate.on-profile: prod` (not filename-only detection) |
| `@RefreshScope` on `@Configuration` class | Beans not recreated, stale config | `@RefreshScope` works on `@Component`/`@Controller`; for `@Configuration`, use `@Bean` method |
| Vault token hardcoded in `bootstrap.yml` | Same token across envs — token compromise | Use AppRole, Kubernetes auth, or env var injection — never commit tokens |
| `spring.config.import` missing `optional:` prefix | Service fails to start if Config Server is unavailable | Always use `optional:config-server:http://...` for resilience |

### Summary

- Spring Boot externalizes config via `application.yml` + env vars + command-line args (12-Factor App); `@ConfigurationProperties` gives **type-safe binding** and IDE autocompletion.
- **Profiles** (`spring.config.activate.on-profile: prod`) split per-environment config; combine with `@Profile` beans and conditional auto-configuration (Q205).
- **Spring Cloud Config Server** centralizes config in a Git repo — services import via `spring.config.import: optional:config-server:http://...`; **bus-refresh** triggers dynamic property reload without restart.
- **Spring Cloud Vault** provides dynamic database credentials (ephemeral, auto-rotated) and KV secrets — never store secrets in Git; use AppRole/Kubernetes auth, not long-lived tokens.

---

## 219. How does Spring Data JPA work? Explain repository abstractions, derived queries, `@Query`, auditing, projections, and Specification.

Spring Data JPA eliminates boilerplate DAO code by auto-generating implementations at runtime from interface methods — you declare `findByStatus(OrderStatus status)` and Spring creates the SQL. Auditing tracks `@CreatedDate` / `@LastModifiedBy`; projections return partial entities or interfaces; Specifications compose dynamic queries without string concatenation.

---

### 1. Repository Hierarchy

```
Repository                           (marker interface — no methods)
  └─ CrudRepository<ID, T>          (save, findById, findAll, delete, count)
      └─ PagingAndSortingRepository (findAll(Sort), findAll(Pageable))
          └─ JpaRepository<T, ID>   (flush, saveAndFlush, deleteInBatch, getOne, JPQL specifics)
              └─ YourRepository      (custom methods — derived queries or @Query)
```

```java
public interface OrderRepository extends JpaRepository<Order, Long> {
    // Spring Data generates the implementation at runtime via a JDK Proxy
}

@Service
class OrderService {
    private final OrderRepository orders;

    OrderService(OrderRepository orders) { this.orders = orders; }

    void place(Order order) {
        orders.save(order);                    // INSERT
    }

    Order findByIdOrThrow(Long id) {
        return orders.findById(id)
                     .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("Order " + id));
    }

    Page<OrderDTO> listByStatus(OrderStatus status, Pageable pageable) {
        return orders.findByStatus(status, pageable);  // derived query
    }
}
```

---

### 2. Derived Query Methods — Name-Based DSL

Spring parses the method name and generates JPQL/SQL automatically:

```java
public interface OrderRepository extends JpaRepository<Order, Long> {

    // Simple property
    List<Order> findByStatus(OrderStatus status);

    // AND / OR
    List<Order> findByStatusAndCustomerId(OrderStatus status, Long customerId);

    // Comparison
    List<Order> findByTotalGreaterThan(BigDecimal amount);
    List<Order> findByCreatedAtBetween(Instant start, Instant end);

    // Pattern matching
    List<Order> findByCustomerNameContainingIgnoreCase(String name);

    // Ordering
    List<Order> findByStatusOrderByCreatedAtDesc(OrderStatus status);

    // Pagination + Sort
    Page<Order> findByStatus(OrderStatus status, Pageable pageable);

    // Exists / Count / Delete
    boolean existsByCustomerId(Long customerId);
    long countByStatus(OrderStatus status);
    void deleteByStatus(OrderStatus status);

    // Projections — return only needed fields (see §5)
    List<OrderSummary> findByStatus(OrderStatus status);  // interface projection
}
```

| Keyword | Meaning | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `findBy` | Query root | `findByStatus` → `WHERE status = ?` |
| `And` / `Or` | Logical | `findByStatusAndCustomerId` |
| `GreaterThan` / `LessThan` | `>`, `<` | `findByTotalGreaterThan(100)` |
| `Between` | `BETWEEN ? AND ?` | `findByCreatedAtBetween(start, end)` |
| `Like` / `Containing` / `StartingWith` | Pattern | `findByCustomerNameContaining("john")` |
| `OrderBy` | Sort | `findByStatusOrderByCreatedAtDesc` |
| `Is` / `Equals` | Explicit equality | `findByStatusIs(status)` |
| `In` | `IN (?)` | `findByStatusIn(List<Status>)` |
| `Not` | Negation | `findByStatusNot(status)` |

---

### 3. `@Query` — JPQL / Native SQL

```java
public interface OrderRepository extends JpaRepository<Order, Long> {

    // JPQL — works across databases, uses entity names/fields
    @Query("SELECT o FROM Order o WHERE o.status = :status AND o.total > :minTotal")
    List<Order> findHighValueByStatus(@Param("status") OrderStatus status,
                                       @Param("minTotal") BigDecimal minTotal);

    // Native SQL — when JPQL can't express it (window functions, CTEs)
    @Query(value = """
        SELECT o.*, RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY total DESC) as rnk
        FROM orders o WHERE o.status = 'PAID'
        """, nativeQuery = true)
    List<Order> findTopOrdersPerCustomer();

    // Paginated native query:
    @Query(value = "SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = :status",
           countQuery = "SELECT count(*) FROM orders WHERE status = :status",
           nativeQuery = true)
    Page<Order> findByStatusNative(@Param("status") String status, Pageable pageable);

    // Update — modify and return affected rows
    @Modifying
    @Query("UPDATE Order o SET o.status = :newStatus WHERE o.id = :id")
    int updateStatus(@Param("id") Long id, @Param("newStatus") OrderStatus newStatus);

    // Delete
    @Modifying
    @Query("DELETE FROM Order o WHERE o.createdAt < :cutoff")
    int deleteOldOrders(@Param("cutoff") Instant cutoff);
}
```

**`@Modifying` note:** clears the persistence context after execution (`clearAutomatically = true` by default) — prevents stale entity state. Set `clearAutomatically = false` if you want to keep the persistence context.

---

### 4. Auditing — Track `@CreatedDate`, `@LastModifiedBy`

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-data-jpa</artifactId>  <!-- already included -->
</dependency>
```

```java
@EntityListeners(AuditingEntityListener.class)
@Entity
class Order {
    @Id @GeneratedValue Long id;

    @CreatedDate Instant createdAt;
    @LastModifiedDate Instant updatedAt;

    @CreatedBy Long createdBy;     // from SecurityContext (Q207)
    @LastModifiedBy Long modifiedBy;

    // fields...
}

@EnableJpaAuditing  // on a @Configuration class
class JpaConfig {}

// Spring Security integration — @CreatedBy reads from current principal:
@Bean AuditorAware<Long> auditorAware() {
    return () -> SecurityContextHolder.getContext().getAuthentication()
               .map(auth -> Long.parseLong(auth.getName()))
               .or(() -> Optional.of(0L));  // system user for non-HTTP contexts
}
```

| Annotation | Set by | Value |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `@CreatedDate` | `INSERT` — entity listener | `Instant.now()` at persist time |
| `@LastModifiedDate` | `UPDATE` — entity listener | `Instant.now()` at every update |
| `@CreatedBy` | `INSERT` — from `AuditorAware` | Current principal (JWT subject, username) |
| `@LastModifiedBy` | `UPDATE` — from `AuditorAware` | Current principal at last update |

**Audit log table pattern:**

```java
@Entity
class AuditLog {
    @Id @GeneratedValue Long id;
    String entityType;   // "Order"
    Long entityId;       // order.id
    String action;       // "CREATE", "UPDATE", "DELETE"
    String changedBy;
    Instant changedAt;
    @Column(columnDefinition = "TEXT")
    String changesJson;  // {"status":{"old":"PENDING","new":"PAID"}}
}
```

---

### 5. Projections — Return Only What You Need

```java
// Interface projection — Spring proxies the interface, maps entity fields:
public interface OrderSummary {
    Long getId();
    String getCustomerName();
    BigDecimal getTotal();
    OrderStatus getStatus();
}

// Usage — derived query method returns projection interface:
List<OrderSummary> findByStatus(OrderStatus status);

// Value class projection:
record OrderListDTO(Long id, String customerName, BigDecimal total) {}

// Dynamic projection — choose at runtime:
public interface OrderRepository extends JpaRepository<Order, Long> {
    <T> List<T> findByStatus(OrderStatus status, Class<T> type);
}

// Callers:
List<OrderSummary> summaries = repo.findByStatus(PAID, OrderSummary.class);
List<OrderListDTO> dtos      = repo.findByStatus(PAID, OrderListDTO.class);
List<Order> full             = repo.findByStatus(PAID, Order.class);
```

| Projection type | When | Trade-off |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Interface** | Simple read-only DTO | Spring creates proxy — no custom logic |
| **Value class / Record** | Typed DTO, immutable | Constructor-based — cleaner, testable |
| **Dynamic (Class\<T\>)** | Same query, different shapes | Flexible, type-safe |

---

### 6. Specification — Dynamic Criteria Queries

```java
// JPA Specification — composable, testable, type-safe predicate builder:
public class OrderSpecs {

    public static Specification<Order> hasStatus(OrderStatus status) {
        return (root, query, cb) -> status == null ? null : cb.equal(root.get("status"), status);
    }

    public static Specification<Order> totalGreaterThan(BigDecimal amount) {
        return (root, query, cb) -> amount == null ? null : cb.greaterThan(root.get("total"), amount);
    }

    public static Specification<Order> createdBetween(Instant from, Instant to) {
        return (root, query, cb) -> {
            if (from == null && to == null) return null;
            if (from != null && to != null) return cb.between(root.get("createdAt"), from, to);
            if (from != null) return cb.greaterThanOrEqualTo(root.get("createdAt"), from);
            return cb.lessThanOrEqualTo(root.get("createdAt"), to);
        };
    }

    public static Specification<Order> belongingToCustomer(Long customerId) {
        return (root, query, cb) -> customerId == null ? null
            : cb.equal(root.get("customer").get("id"), customerId);
    }
}

// Repository:
public interface OrderRepository extends JpaRepository<Order, Long>, JpaSpecificationExecutor<Order> {}

// Usage — compose specs dynamically:
Specification<Order> spec = Specification
    .where(OrderSpecs.hasStatus(PAID))
    .and(OrderSpecs.totalGreaterThan(new BigDecimal("100")))
    .and(OrderSpecs.createdBetween(start, end));

Page<Order> results = orders.findAll(spec, PageRequest.of(0, 20, Sort.by("createdAt").descending()));
```

```java
// Controller — build spec from query params:
@GetMapping
Page<OrderDTO> list(@RequestParam(required = false) OrderStatus status,
                    @RequestParam(required = false) BigDecimal minTotal,
                    @RequestParam(defaultValue = "0") int page,
                    @RequestParam(defaultValue = "20") int size) {
    Specification<Order> spec = Specification
        .where(OrderSpecs.hasStatus(status))
        .and(OrderSpecs.totalGreaterThan(minTotal));
    return orders.findAll(spec, PageRequest.of(page, size)).map(OrderDTO::from);
}
```

---

### 7. `JpaSpecificationExecutor` + `Querydsl` — Advanced Criteria

```java
// Querydsl — type-safe criteria (generated Q-classes):
public interface OrderRepository extends JpaRepository<Order, Long>,
                                        QuerydslPredicateExecutor<Order> {}

// Usage:
QOrder o = QOrder.order;
Predicate predicate = o.status.eq(PAID)
    .and(o.total.goe(new BigDecimal("100")))
    .and(o.createdAt.between(start, end));
Page<Order> results = orders.findAll(predicate, PageRequest.of(0, 20));

// Specification vs Querydsl:
// Specification: no code-gen, good for simple predicates
// Querydsl: generated Q-classes, full type-safety, complex joins, subqueries
```

---

### 8. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| N+1 on derived queries | Lazy-loaded collections cause N+1 selects | Use `@EntityGraph`, `JOIN FETCH` in `@Query`, or `Specification` with joins |
| `@Query` returns entities but you need DTOs | Unnecessary columns fetched, lazy init issues | Use interface/value projections or `@Query` with constructor expression (`SELECT new OrderDTO(...)`) |
| `@Modifying` without `@Transactional` | `TransactionRequiredException` | Wrap in `@Transactional` or use service-layer `@Transactional` |
| `Specification` with null params returns all | Unfiltered query on empty search form | Return `null` from spec (ignored by `Specification.where()` chaining) |
| Projections return `null` for missing fields | `NullPointerException` in DTO | Interface projections: Spring returns null for unmapped; value projections: require all fields |
| Auditing not working | `createdAt`/`createdBy` stay null | `@EnableJpaAuditing` on a `@Configuration` class + `@EntityListeners(AuditingEntityListener.class)` on entity |

### Summary

- Spring Data JPA generates repository implementations from interface method names — **derived queries** (`findByStatus`, `totalGreaterThan`) create JPQL without writing code; use `@Query` for JPQL or native SQL when names aren't expressive enough.
- **Auditing** (`@CreatedDate`, `@LastModifiedBy`, `AuditorAware`) auto-tracks who changed what and when — integrates with Spring Security for user context.
- **Projections** (interface / record / dynamic) return only the fields the client needs — avoids fetching full entities; `Specification` composes dynamic criteria without string concatenation.
- `JpaSpecificationExecutor` + **Specification** pattern (or **Querydsl** for generated Q-classes) handles search/filter/sort across multiple optional parameters — the standard for API list endpoints.

---

## 220. How does Spring Batch work? Explain chunk-oriented processing, `ItemReader`/`ItemProcessor`/`ItemWriter`, jobs/steps, partitioning, and retry/skip.

Spring Batch is the framework for **large-scale batch processing** — ETL jobs, data migrations, payroll runs, report generation, and multi-million-row CSV/DB imports. It provides a chunk-oriented execution model (read → process → write), built-in retry/skip/restart semantics, and scaling via partitioning or remote chunking. It's the standard answer to "how would you process 10 million rows without OOM?"

---

### 1. Core Concepts — Job, Step, Chunk

```
Job (e.g., import-customers)
  └─ Step 1: read-and-write (chunk-oriented)
  │      └─ Chunk = read N items → process N items → write N items, then commit TX
  │      └─ (repeat until reader returns null = exhausted)
  └─ Step 2: check-report (tasklet — single operation)
         └─ generate summary file
```

```java
@Configuration
@EnableBatchProcessing
class CustomerImportConfig {

    @Bean Job customerImportJob(JobRepository repo, Step importStep, Step reportStep) {
        return new JobBuilder("customerImportJob", repo)
            .start(importStep)
            .next(reportStep)
            .incrementer(new RunIdIncrementer())   // new JobInstance each run
            .build();
    }

    @Bean Step importStep(JobRepository repo, PlatformTransactionManager txm, ItemReader<Customer> reader,
                          ItemProcessor<Customer, Customer> processor, ItemWriter<Customer> writer) {
        return new StepBuilder("importStep", repo)
            .<Customer, Customer>chunk(1000, txm)   // commit every 1000 items
            .reader(reader)
            .processor(processor)
            .writer(writer)
            .faultTolerant()
            .retryLimit(3).retry(DeadlockLoserDataAccessException.class)
            .skipLimit(100).skip(InvalidCustomerException.class)
            .noSkip(FileNotFoundException.class)
            .build();
    }
}
```

| Concept | Role |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Job** | Top-level batch — a sequence of steps |
| **Step** | Chunk-oriented (read/process/write) **or** tasklet (single action) |
| **Chunk** | `commitInterval` items read+processed+written under **one transaction** |
| **JobInstance** | Logical run (by identifying parameters) — restartable |
| **JobExecution / StepExecution** | One physical attempt — recorded in BATCH_JOB_* tables |
| **JobLauncher** | Triggers the job (REST endpoint, `@Scheduled`, or CLI) |

---

### 2. Chunk-Oriented Processing — `ItemReader` / `ItemProcessor` / `ItemWriter`

```java
// Reader — reads from CSV, DB, REST, etc. Returns null when exhausted:
public class CustomerCsvReader implements ItemReader<Customer> {
    private final CsvReader delegate;

    @Override
    public Customer read() throws Exception {
        // read next line; return null when no more records
        String[] row = delegate.readNext();
        return row == null ? null : parseRow(row);
    }
}

// Processor — transform / validate / enrich (optional — can be identity):
public class CustomerProcessor implements ItemProcessor<Customer, Customer> {
    @Override
    public Customer process(Customer c) {
        if (c.getName().isBlank()) throw new InvalidCustomerException(c); // skipped
        c.setNormalized(c.getName().trim().toUpperCase());
        return c; // null → item is filtered out, not written
    }
}

// Writer — writes in a chunk (one TX per chunk):
public class CustomerJdbcWriter implements ItemWriter<Customer> {
    private final JdbcTemplate jdbc;

    @Override
    public void write(Chunk<? extends Customer> chunk) {
        jdbc.batchUpdate("INSERT INTO customers(name, email) VALUES (?, ?)",
            chunk.getItems().stream()
                .map(c -> new Object[]{c.getName(), c.getEmail()})
                .toList());
    }
}
```

**Why chunking prevents OOM:** the chunk buffer holds only `commitInterval` (e.g., 1000) items at a time — no need to load 10M rows into memory. The DB/Hibernate-side equivalent is `setFetchSize` + `scrollable result set` (Q208).

---

### 3. Built-In Readers / Writers

```java
// FlatFileItemReader — CSV/TSV/text:
@Bean ItemReader<Customer> csvReader(@Value("file:${csv.path}") Resource input) {
    return new FlatFileItemReaderBuilder<Customer>()
        .name("customerCsvReader")
        .resource(input)
        .delimited()
        .names("id", "name", "email")
        .fieldSetMapper(fs -> new Customer(fs.readLong("id"), fs.readString("name"), fs.readString("email")))
        .build();
}

// JdbcCursorItemReader — streaming DB read (fetchSize pages through result set):
@Bean ItemReader<Customer> dbReader(DataSource ds) {
    return new JdbcCursorItemReaderBuilder<Customer>()
        .name("customerDbReader")
        .dataSource(ds)
        .sql("SELECT id, name, email FROM customers")
        .fetchSize(500)                                // cursor paging — not all rows at once
        .rowMapper((rs, i) -> new Customer(rs.getLong(1), rs.getString(2), rs.getString(3)))
        .build();
}

// JpaPagingItemReader — pagination alternative (page-based, safer with DB cursor limits):
@Bean ItemReader<Customer> jpaReader(EntityManagerFactory emf) {
    return new JpaPagingItemReaderBuilder<Customer>()
        .name("customerJpaReader")
        .entityManagerFactory(emf)
        .queryString("SELECT c FROM Customer c")
        .pageSize(1000)
        .build();
}

// Multiple writers:
@Bean CompositeItemWriter<Customer> compositeWriter(...) {
    return new CompositeItemWriterBuilder<Customer>()
        .delegates(List.of(jdbcWriter(), jpaWriter()))
        .build();
}
```

`JdbcCursorItemReader` keeps a DB cursor and pulls `fetchSize` rows at a time; `JpaPagingItemReader` uses SQL `LIMIT/OFFSET` pages — both scale to millions of rows with bounded memory.

---

### 4. Restart, Retry, and Skip

```java
@Bean Step importStep(JobRepository repo, PlatformTransactionManager txm, ...) {
    return new StepBuilder("importStep", repo)
        .<Customer, Customer>chunk(1000, txm)
        .reader(reader)
        .processor(processor)
        .writer(writer)
        .faultTolerant()
        // RETRY — transient errors retried within the chunk:
        .retryLimit(3)
        .retry(DeadlockLoserDataAccessException.class)     // connection timeout, deadlock
        .retry(TransientDataAccessException.class)
        // SKIP — permanent errors skipped, chunk can still commit:
        .skipLimit(100)                                   // max 100 skipable items per step
        .skip(InvalidCustomerException.class)             // bad record → skip
        .noSkip(FileNotFoundException.class)              // fatal — don't skip this
        .listener(new ItemErrorListener())                // log each skipped item
        .build();
}

// Listeners — log/inspect each phase:
class ItemErrorListener implements ItemProcessListener<Customer, Customer> {
    @Override public void onProcessError(Customer item, Exception e) {
        log.warn("Skipped item {}: {}", item.getId(), e.getMessage());
    }
}
```

**Why retry + skip together:** DB deadlocks are transient (retry helps); malformed CSV rows are permanent (skip allows the chunk to commit, then restart ignores already-committed chunks). Spring records skip/retry counts in `BATCH_STEP_EXECUTION` and **restart resumes from the last failed chunk**, not from the beginning.

```java
// Manual restart — same identifying parameters, JobLauncher resumes:
jobLauncher.run(job, new JobParametersBuilder()
    .addString("inputFile", csvPath)   // same value → resume
    .toJobParameters());
```

---

### 5. Scaling — Partitioning and Remote Chunking

```
Partitioning (horizontal — splits the READ):
  Step
    ├─ Partition 0: rows 0–1,000,000  → worker thread/process
    ├─ Partition 1: rows 1M–2M        → worker
    ├─ Partition 2: rows 2M–3M        → worker
    └─ Partition 3: rows 3M–4M        → worker
  Each partition owns a StepExecution — independent retry/commit, then aggregate.

Remote Chunking (moves the WRITE to workers via messaging):
  Master reads → sends chunks over RabbitMQ/Kafka (Q212/Q224)
  Workers process+write → ACK back
```

```java
// Partitioning with a Partitioner:
@Bean Partitioner customerPartitioner() {
    return new Partitioner() {
        @Override
        public Map<String, ExecutionContext> partition(int gridSize) {
            Map<String, ExecutionContext> map = new HashMap<>();
            long total = jdbc.queryForObject("SELECT count(*) FROM customers", Long.class);
            long size = total / gridSize;
            for (int i = 0; i < gridSize; i++) {
                ExecutionContext ctx = new ExecutionContext();
                ctx.putLong("minId", i * size + 1);
                ctx.putLong("maxId", i == gridSize - 1 ? total : (i + 1) * size);
                map.put("partition" + i, ctx);   // worker reads only its id range
            }
            return map;
        }
    };
}

@Bean Step masterStep(JobRepository repo, PlatformTransactionManager txm, Partitioner partitioner, Step worker) {
    return new StepBuilder("masterStep", repo)
        .partitioner(worker.getName(), partitioner)
        .step(worker)
        .gridSize(4)                       // 4 partitions
        .taskExecutor(new SimpleAsyncTaskExecutor())  // or TaskExecutor of your choice
        .build();
}
```

Partitioning parallelizes CPU/DB-bound batch by **id-range or file-split**; remote chunking scales I/O-bound writes by delegating chunks to a farm of workers over a message broker.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| No `fetchSize` / using `readAll()` | OOM on million-row input | `JdbcCursorItemReader` with `fetchSize`; `JpaPagingItemReader` |
| Missing `faultTolerant()` | One bad row aborts the whole chunk | Add `faultTolerant()` + `skipLimit`/`skip`; use `noSkip` for fatal classes |
| Reader returns `null` in middle | Unwanted stop — `null` signals EOF | Stream-find EOF properly; return `null` **only** when exhausted |
| No `RunIdIncrementer` | Same identifying params refuse new runs ("A job instance already exists") | Add `.incrementer(new RunIdIncrementer())` or use unique params |
| Processor returns `null` silently | Items "missing" from output | Use a `PassThroughItemProcessor` or return a valid item; `null` means filtered-out by design |
| Chunk too large | Long TX, lock contention, slow rollback | 100–1000 typical; benchmark; use smaller commit interval for large rows |

### Summary

- Spring Batch's **chunk-oriented model** (`read N → process N → write N` under one TX) processes millions of rows with bounded memory — never load the whole dataset at once.
- Readers (`FlatFileItemReader`, `JdbcCursorItemReader` with `fetchSize`, `JpaPagingItemReader`) stream input; processors transform/filter; **composite writers** fan out to multiple destinations.
- **Fault tolerance** = retry (transient) + skip (permanent, with `skipLimit`) + commit-interval — restart resumes from the last failed chunk because metadata lives in `BATCH_JOB_*` tables.
- **Partitioning** splits the read across workers by id-range; **remote chunking** distributes the write via a broker; scale with `gridSize` + `TaskExecutor`.

---

## 221. Why is WebFlux reactive and how does it differ from Spring MVC? Explain Mono/Flux, reactive streams backpressure, and non-blocking threading.

Spring WebFlux is the **reactive** web stack — it doesn't block a thread per request. Where Spring MVC uses one thread per request plus thread pools, WebFlux uses **non-blocking I/O** with an **event loop** (Netty), serving thousands of concurrent requests on a couple of threads. It's the right tool for high-concurrency, I/O-heavy services that call many downstream APIs and need bounded resource usage; it's a poor fit for CPU-bound work or blocking legacy libraries.

---

### 1. One-Thread-Per-Request vs Event Loop

```java
// Spring MVC — thread-per-request (Q206):
//  Tomcat: 200 worker threads → 200 concurrent requests max
//  Slow DB call → thread blocked in wait → thread pool exhausts → 503s under load

// Spring WebFlux — event loop (Netty):
//  Few threads (≈ #cores) — each runs an event loop
//  I/O is non-blocking ("register interest, get callback when data ready")
//  Thread is never blocked — it multiplexes thousands of in-flight operations
```

```
MVC:                         WebFlux:
Request ──► Thread ── wait ─► done    Request ──► EventLoop ─registers interest─┐
Request ──► Thread ── wait ─► done    Request ──► EventLoop ─registers interest─┤
Request ──► Thread ── wait ─► done    Request ──► EventLoop ─registers interest─┤
(4 requests = 4 threads, blocked)     (4 requests = 1 thread, none blocked)    │
                                       data ready ◄──────────────────────────────┘
```

| | Spring MVC | Spring WebFlux |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Model | One thread per request | Event loop + non-blocking callbacks |
| I/O | Blocking (`Thread.sleep`, `restTemplate` blocks) | Non-blocking (`WebClient`, reactive drivers) |
| Threads | Tomcat pool (~200) | Netty event loops (~#cores) |
| Throughput | Limited by pool size | High — ~1k–10k req/s sustained |
| Latency | Higher under load (queuing) | Lower under load (no thread contention) |
| CPU-bound work | Runs on worker thread natively | **Blocks the event loop** — offload to `Schedulers.boundedElastic()` |

---

### 2. Mono and Flux — The Reactive Types

```java
// Reactive Streams contract: Publisher<T>
//   Mono<T>  — 0 or 1 item   (e.g., one order, one request/response)
//   Flux<T>  — 0..N items    (e.g., list of orders, event stream)

Flux<String> stream = Flux.just("a", "b", "c")
    .delayElements(Duration.ofMillis(100))
    .map(String::toUpperCase)
    .onErrorResume(e -> Flux.just("fallback"));

Mono<Order> order = orderRepository.findById(id)        // returns Mono<Order>
    .switchIfEmpty(Mono.error(new NotFoundException()))
    .map(orderService::enrich);

// Operators are lazy — nothing runs until you subscribe:
//   map, flatMap, filter, zip, merge, concat, retry, timeout, cache, ...
stream.subscribe(System.out::println);  // NOW it runs
```

**Common operators:**

| Operator | Effect |
| :--- | :--- |
| `.map(f)` | Synchronous 1:1 transform |
| `.flatMap(f)` | Async 1:M — returns inner `Publisher`, **non-blocking** composition |
| `.zipWith(...)` | Pair two sources — join order + customer |
| `.parallel()` | Fan out to parallel rails |
| `.retry(3)` / `.retryWhen(...)` | Re-subscribe after error (Q213 — resilience) |
| `.timeout(Duration)` | Fail after a deadline |
| `.onErrorResume/Return/Map` | Reactive error handling — no try/catch across threads |
| `Schedulers.parallel()` | CPU-bound work on a fixed pool (≈cores) |
| `Schedulers.boundedElastic()` | **Blocking work** (JDBC, legacy libs) on a pool — DON'T block the event loop |

```java
// Example: reactive endpoint
@RestController
class OrderController {

    @GetMapping("/orders/{id}")
    Mono<OrderDTO> get(@PathVariable Long id) {
        return orderService.findById(id)          // Mono<Order>
            .map(OrderDTO::from)
            .switchIfEmpty(Mono.error(new ResourceNotFoundException()));
    }

    @GetMapping("/orders")
    Flux<OrderDTO> list(@RequestParam String customerId) {
        return orderService.findByCustomer(customerId)  // Flux<Order>
            .map(OrderDTO::from);
    }

    // Stream infinite events — Server-Sent Events:
    @GetMapping(value = "/events", produces = MediaType.TEXT_EVENT_STREAM_VALUE)
    Flux<ServerSentEvent<Order>> events() {
        return Flux.interval(Duration.ofSeconds(1))
            .map(i -> ServerSentEvent.<Order>builder().data(orderService.latest()).build());
    }
}
```

---

### 3. Reactive Streams Backpressure

**Backpressure** = the consumer tells the producer "slow down" via `request(n)`. A `Flux.interval` publishing 1000 msg/s must not overwhelm a slow consumer:

```java
// No backpressure → unbounded buffer = OOM
Flux.interval(Duration.ofMillis(1))                    // 1000/s
    .subscribe(d -> blockAndProcess(d));               // slow consumer — buffer explodes

// With backpressure — bounded buffer + drop policy:
Flux.interval(Duration.ofMillis(1))
    .onBackpressureBuffer(100, dropped -> log.warn("dropped {}", dropped))
    .subscribe(d -> blockAndProcess(d));

// Or drop / latest / error policies:
.onBackpressureDrop()          // skip items when overloaded
.onBackpressureLatest()        // keep only the latest
.onBackpressureError()         // fail fast with Exceptions.overflow()
```

```
Publisher ──onNext──► Subscription.request(n) ──► Publisher sends at most n
Publisher ◄──onNext x n── Consumer (each request replenishes the demand)
downstream backpressure propagates upstream (Netty channel buffers, DB cursors)
```

Reactive drivers (R2DBC for relational DB, reactive MongoDB/Kafka clients) honor backpressure at the I/O level — fetch/pull windows align with `request(n)`.

---

### 4. JDBC vs R2DBC — The Blocking Elephant

```java
// Blocking JDBC — cannot be made truly reactive (ThreadLocal TX, blocking drivers):
//   Mono.fromCallable(() -> jdbcTemplate.query(...))  // works, but runs on boundedElastic = still thread-per-query

// R2DBC — non-blocking database client (reactive streams driver):
@Repository
interface OrderRepository extends ReactiveCrudRepository<Order, Long> {
    Flux<Order> findByCustomerId(String customerId);
    Mono<Long> countByStatus(OrderStatus status);
}

@Service
class OrderService {
    Mono<OrderDTO> getWithStock(Long id) {
        return orderRepository.findById(id)                      // Mono<Order>
            .flatMap(order -> stockService.checkAsync(order))    // WebClient call — non-blocking
            .map(OrderDTO::from);
    }
}
```

If you must keep JDBC (most teams do), wrap blocking calls in `.subscribeOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic())` — but that reintroduces thread-per-query, so the real gain is from a **reactive end-to-end stack** (WebFlux + R2DBC + WebClient + reactive Kafka/Redis).

---

### 5. WebFlux + Resilience4j / Errors

```java
// Reactive resilience — operators replace annotations (Q213):
@Bean
CircuitBreaker cb = circuitBreakerRegistry.circuitBreaker("inventory");

public Mono<Stock> checkStock(String sku) {
    return Mono.defer(() -> webClient.get().uri("/stock/{sku}", sku).retrieve().bodyToMono(Stock.class))
        .transformDeferred(CircuitBreakerOperator.of(cb))     // circuit breaker on the reactive pipeline
        .retryWhen(Retry.backoff(3, Duration.ofMillis(200)))
        .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(2))
        .onErrorReturn(Stock.unknown(sku));                   // degraded fallback — no blocking
}

// Global error mapping to HTTP status codes:
@RestControllerAdvice
class GlobalErrorWebExceptionHandler {
    @ExceptionHandler(ResourceNotFoundException.class)
    Mono<ResponseEntity<ApiError>> handle(ResourceNotFoundException ex, ServerWebExchange exchange) {
        return Mono.just(ResponseEntity.status(404).body(new ApiError(404, ex.getMessage())));
    }
}
```

Reactive risk: `ThreadLocal`-based state (MDC, Spring Security, Spring TX) does **not** propagate across `flatMap` — use the Reactor **Context** API or the context-propagation library instead:

```java
// Context propagation — replaces ThreadLocal:
webClient.get().uri(...)
    .contextWrite(ctx -> ctx.put("requestId", requestId))
```

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Blocking call (JDBC, `restTemplate`, `Thread.sleep`) on the event loop | Event loop blocked → ALL requests on that loop stall (latency spike) | Use R2DBC, `WebClient`, or wrap in `.subscribeOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic())` |
| `.block()` / `.blockFirst()` in reactive chain | Blocks the event loop — anti-pattern, deadlock risk | Stay reactive downstream; block only at the true boundary (tests, imperative entry point) |
| Unbounded `flatMap` concurrency | Memory blowup — no backpressure | `.flatMap(fn, concurrency)` — cap parallelism (default 256) |
| `ThreadLocal` (MDC/Security/TX) access in operators | `null` context or wrong context (Q211) | Reactor **Context** / `contextWrite` / context-propagation library |
| `Flux` never completes | WebClient streaming hangs (keeps connection open) | Use `.take(n)`, `.takeUntil(pred)`, or timeouts |
| CPU-bound `.map(...)` on event loop | Event loop saturated — throughput collapses | Offload with `Schedulers.parallel()` / boundedElastic; keep reactive for I/O |

### Summary

- WebFlux = **event loop + non-blocking I/O** (Netty): a handful of threads multiplex thousands of requests — throughput scales with I/O concurrency, not thread count; MVC stays thread-per-request.
- `Mono<T>` (0/1) vs `Flux<T>` (0..N) with immutable operators (`map`, `flatMap`, `zip`, `retry`, `timeout`, `onErrorResume`) — pipelines are lazy until `subscribe()`.
- **Backpressure** (`request(n)` + `onBackpressureBuffer/Drop/Latest/Error`) lets a slow consumer stop the producer from overrunning memory; reactive drivers (R2DBC, reactive Kafka/Mongo) honor it end-to-end.
- Never **block the event loop** — no JDBC/`Thread.sleep`/`.block()` in the chain; use R2DBC/WebClient or `Schedulers.boundedElastic()`, and use Reactor **Context** instead of `ThreadLocal`.

---

## 222. How does GraphQL work and when should you prefer it over REST? Explain schema, resolvers, queries/mutations, and the N+1 problem.

GraphQL is an API query language where the **client declares exactly the shape of data it wants** in a single request — no over-fetching (REST list endpoints returning 20 fields you need 3 of) and no under-fetching (N round-trips to assemble a view). Implemented in Spring via **Spring GraphQL** (`spring-graphql`, built on WebMvc or WebFlux), it exposes a **schema**, resolves fields via **resolvers**, and solves composition with **DataLoader** batching.

---

### 1. GraphQL vs REST — The Contract Model

```
REST: server-defined resources + the client adapts to many endpoints / shapes
  GET /api/v1/users/42 → { id, name, email, phone, address, meta_1..meta_20 }
  Client over-fetches (doesn't need phone/address) — under-fetch requires 2–3 more calls.

GraphQL: client-defined query, one endpoint
  POST /graphql
  { user(id:42){ name email } }   → { user: { name: "...", email: "..." } }
  Exactly the requested fields — no over/under fetch.
```

| | REST (Q216) | GraphQL |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Endpoints | Many (`/users`, `/users/{id}/orders`) | One — `/graphql` |
| Shape | Server-fixed | Client-driven query |
| Over/under-fetch | Both common | Neither (by design) |
| Versioning | `/v1/`, `/v2/` (Q216) | Schema **evolution** — add fields, deprecate old ones |
| Caching | HTTP cache per URL easy | Harder — POST + dynamic shape; use persisted queries |
| Security | Per-endpoint auth easy | Field/query-level authorization needed |
| Use when | Simple, cacheable, public APIs, CRUD | Aggregating apps (mobile/web), complex client views, evolving schema |

---

### 2. The Schema — Types, Queries, Mutations, Subscriptions

```graphql
# schema.graphqls
type Query {
    orders(customerId: ID, status: OrderStatus): [Order!]!
    order(id: ID!): Order
}

type Mutation {
    createOrder(input: CreateOrderInput!): Order!
    cancelOrder(id: ID!): Order!
}

type Subscription {
    orderStatusChanged(orderId: ID!): Order!   # WebSocket push
}

type Order {
    id: ID!
    customer: Customer!
    lines: [OrderLine!]!
    status: OrderStatus!
    total: Float!
    createdAt: String!
}

type OrderLine {
    id: ID!
    product: Product!
    quantity: Int!
    price: Float!
}

input CreateOrderInput {
    customerId: ID!
    lines: [OrderLineInput!]!
}

enum OrderStatus { PENDING PAID SHIPPED CANCELLED }
```

- **Types** define the graph; **query** = read (GET), **mutation** = write (POST), **subscription** = realtime (WebSocket).
- `[Order!]!` = non-null list of non-null elements (every element has `id`, list is never null).
- **Input types** (`CreateOrderInput`) carry structured mutation arguments.

---

### 3. Resolvers — Wiring Schema to Java

```java
// Spring GraphQL — annotate @Controller + @QueryMapping/@MutationMapping/@SchemaMapping:

@Controller
class OrderGraphqlController {

    // Resolves: Query.orders
    @QueryMapping
    Flux<Order> orders(@Argument String customerId, @Argument OrderStatus status) {
        return orderRepository.findByCustomerAndStatus(customerId, status);
    }

    // Resolves: Query.order
    @QueryMapping
    Mono<Order> order(@Argument Long id) {
        return orderRepository.findById(id).switchIfEmpty(Mono.error(new NotFoundException()));
    }

    // Resolves: Mutation.createOrder
    @MutationMapping
    Mono<Order> createOrder(@Argument CreateOrderInput input) {
        return orderService.create(input);
    }

    // Resolves: Order.customer — field-level resolution
    @BatchMapping  // solves the N+1 — batches all customer lookups for this query in one call
    Mono<Map<Order, Customer>> customer(List<Order> orders) {
        List<Long> ids = orders.stream().map(Order::getCustomerId).toList();
        return customerRepository.findByIdIn(ids)
            .collectMap(c -> orderMapByCustomerId(c, orders));
    }
}

// Or via SchemaMapping — computed field (no DB column):
@SchemaMapping(typeName = "Order", field = "total")
double total(Order order) {
    return order.getLines().stream().mapToDouble(l -> l.getPrice() * l.getQuantity()).sum();
}
```

**Resolver resolution logic:**

```
Client asks: { order(id:5){ id status customer{name} } }
1. resolve Query.order(id:5)      → Order entity
2. resolve Order.customer         → POJO property OR @SchemaMapping/@BatchMapping
3. resolve customer.name          → property getter
Fields without a dedicated resolver fall back to JavaBean property access
```

---

### 4. The N+1 Problem and DataLoader / @BatchMapping

```
Query { orders(...){ customer { name } } }   // 10 orders

Naive resolution:
  1 SELECT orders ... LIMIT 10
  10 SELECT customers WHERE id = ?   ← N+1 (Q208) — 11 queries

@BatchMapping / DataLoader resolution:
  1 SELECT orders ... LIMIT 10
  1 SELECT customers WHERE id IN (?,?,?,?,?,?,?,?,?,?)   ← 2 queries total
```

```java
// Option 1 — @BatchMapping: Spring groups parent records and calls once:
@BatchMapping
Mono<Map<Order, Customer>> customer(List<Order> orders) { /* single IN-query */ }

// Option 2 — DataLoader (graphql-java): per-data-fetching batch:
@Configuration
class DataLoaderConfig {
    @Bean DataLoaderRegistryCustomizer<Order> customerLoader(CustomerRepository repo) {
        return r -> r.register("customerLoader",
            new MappedBatchLoader<Long, Customer>(ids -> repo.findByIdIn(ids).collectMap(Customer::getId)));
    }
}
```

The N+1 is the #1 GraphQL performance trap — every nested object resolution triggers a new query unless batched. **`@BatchMapping`/`DataLoader` turns N queries into 1 `IN` query.**

---

### 5. Handling Errors, Validation, and Authorization

```java
// GraphQLError — typed errors, no 4xx/5xx HTTP codes:
class InvalidOrderError extends GraphQLError {
    private final Long orderId;
    // GraphQLError: getMessage(), getErrorType(), getLocations(), getPath()
    // ErrorType: VALIDATION_ERROR, BAD_REQUEST, DATA_FETCHING_EXCEPTION, ...
}

// Runtime exception handler:
@ControllerAdvice
class GraphqlExceptionHandler {
    @ExceptionHandler(NotFoundException.class)
    GraphQLError handle(NotFoundException ex, DataFetchingEnvironment env) {
        return GraphQLError.newError().message("Resource not found: " + ex.getMessage())
            .path(env.getExecutionStepInfo().getPath())
            .errorType(ErrorType.NOT_FOUND)
            .build();
    }

    // jakarta-validation annotation failures are mapped to VALIDATION_ERROR
}

// Field-level security (Q207): @PreAuthorize on resolver methods, or schema directives:
//   directive @auth(role: String) on FIELD_DEFINITION | OBJECT
//   → Instrumentation checks the logged-in principal per resolved field
```

GraphQL responses are **200 OK with an `errors` array** (not HTTP status codes) — monitoring must check the `errors` field, not just the status.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| N+1 on nested resolvers | 11+ SQL queries for a trivial list | `@BatchMapping` / `DataLoader` / batching `IN` queries |
| Deeply nested client query | `{ a { b { c { d { ... }}}}}` → huge DB load | Depth/query-complexity limits (`Instrumentation`); directives; allow-list persisted queries |
| Returning `Mono`/`Flux` but blocking them | Event loop blocked (Q221) | Return reactive types unblocked — Spring GraphQL subscribes for you |
| Treating GraphQL like REST (many endpoints) | Unnecessary complexity | Use for aggregation/complex views; REST still fine for caching-critical public APIs |
| No field authz | `customer.email` leaks to any authenticated user | Field-level `@PreAuthorize` / instrumentation on sensitive fields |
| Returning full entity in the resolver | Sensitive data fetched even if unfiltered | Resolve exactly requested shape; project to DTOs; instrument `graphql.query` metrics |

### Summary

- GraphQL is a **client-driven query language** — one `/graphql` endpoint, the client selects exact fields (`Query`/`Mutation`/`Subscription`), eliminating over- and under-fetching; schema **evolution** replaces REST versioning.
- **Schema** (`*.graphqls`) defines types/inputs/enums; **resolvers** (`@QueryMapping`/`@MutationMapping`/`@SchemaMapping`/`@BatchMapping`) wire schema to service code — uncached fields fall back to POJO property access.
- The **N+1 trap**: nested object resolution issues a new query per parent — solve with `@BatchMapping`/`DataLoader` turning N queries into one `IN` query, and cap query depth/complexity.
- Errors return in the `errors` array over HTTP 200; add **field-level authorization**; choose REST when public/cache-heavy, GraphQL for aggregation-heavy client views.

---

## 223. How do you handle distributed transactions across microservices? Explain Saga, outbox pattern, idempotency, and two-phase commit's limitations.

In a distributed system, one business flow spans multiple services — a 2PC transaction doesn't scale and couples services, so the industry standard is the **Saga pattern**: a sequence of local transactions with compensating actions. Combined with the **outbox pattern** (eventual consistency without losing messages) and **idempotency** (safe retries), Sagas give pragmatic consistency without global locks or XA.

---

### 1. Why Not a Distributed Transaction (ACID across services)?

```
Option A — 2PC (Two-Phase Commit) across Order, Payment, Inventory:
  Coordinator: "prepare" → all services lock rows → "commit"
  Problems:
    - A global lock holds rows for the whole transaction — contention
    - Coordinator = single point of failure
    - Coupling — every service knows every other's transaction
    - Not supported by Kafka/S3/Redis, awkward with microservices
  Verdict: fine for one DB, wrong for service boundaries.

Option B — BASE + Saga (what teams actually run):
  Every step is a LOCAL transaction in its own service
  On failure, run COMPENSATING actions in reverse order
  No global lock — services stay autonomous
```

| Approach | Atomicity | Availability | Coupling | When to use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **2PC / XA** | Strong | Low (coordinator) | Tight | Single database, legacy, small scope |
| **Saga (choreography)** | Eventual | High | Loose (events) | Default for microservices |
| **Saga (orchestration)** | Eventual | High | Medium (orchestrator) | Complex workflows, need to see state |
| **Outbox + CDC** | Eventual | High | Loose (DB events) | Guaranteed message delivery |
| **Idempotency keys** | Per-op atomic | High | None | Retries-safe REST calls |

---

### 2. Saga Pattern — Choreography vs Orchestration

```
Order flow:
  PlaceOrder → ReserveInventory → ChargePayment → ShipOrder
  If payment fails → compensate: release inventory, mark order failed

CHOREOGRAPHY (no central brain — each service reacts to events):
  OrderService:  publishes "order-placed"
  InventoryService (listens): reserves → publishes "inventory-reserved" or "inventory-failed"
  PaymentService (listens "inventory-reserved"): charges → publishes "payment-charged"/"payment-failed"
  On "payment-failed" → InventoryService compensates (release inventory) → OrderService sets FAILED

ORCHESTRATION (a coordinator/saga service tells each step what to do):
  OrderSaga (orchestrator):
    1. call Inventory.reserve()    → step 1
    2. call Payment.charge()       → step 2
    3. call Ship.ship()            → step 3
    on Payment error → call Inventory.release()  ← compensating step; store saga state in DB
```

```java
// Orchestrated saga skeleton (coordinator as a service):
@Service
class OrderSaga {                                  // saga state machine
    private final InventoryClient inventory;
    private final PaymentClient payment;
    private final OrderRepository orders;

    @Transactional
    void placeOrder(Order order) {
        orders.save(order.withStatus(PENDING));
        try {
            inventory.reserve(order);               // each is a LOCAL TX in its service
            payment.charge(order);
            orders.save(order.withStatus(CONFIRMED));
        } catch (PaymentFailedException | InventoryFailedException e) {
            try { inventory.release(order); } catch (Exception ex) { /* log + manual/retry compensation */ }
            orders.save(order.withStatus(FAILED));
            throw new SagaCompensationFailed(e);
        }
    }
}

// Choreography — each service listens to an event and emits the next (Q212):
@Component
class InventoryListener {
    @EventListener  // or @KafkaListener (Q212)
    void onOrderPlaced(OrderPlacedEvent evt) {
        try { reserve(evt); eventPublisher.publish(new InventoryReserved(evt)); }
        catch (Exception e) { eventPublisher.publish(new InventoryFailed(evt)); }
    }
}
```

| Choreography | Orchestration |
| :--- | :--- |
| Pros: no central point of failure, decoupled | Pros: state visible in one coordinator, easier to reason about, explicit compensating steps |
| Cons: workflow distributed across N services, hard to debug/trace | Cons: coordinator as single point, must persist saga state, more code |
| Best for: few steps, simple flows | Best for: complex, long-running multi-step processes |

Sagas are **eventually consistent** — between `inventory-reserved` and `payment-charged` the inventory is transiently held. That's acceptable in BASE; make it visible in dashboards, not hidden.

---

### 3. Outbox Pattern — No Lost Messages

The problem: "write to DB **and** publish event" isn't atomic — DB commit succeeds, publish fails → event lost.

```java
// WRONG — two independent systems, not atomic:
@Transactional
void placeOrder(Order order) {
    orders.save(order); msgBus.publish("order-placed", order);  // publish fails → event lost
}

// RIGHT — outbox table committed together with the domain write:
@Transactional
void placeOrder(Order order) {
    orders.save(order);
    outboxRepo.save(new OutboxEvent("order-placed", order.json(), UUID.randomUUID()));  // SAME TX
}

// Relay reads the outbox and publishes reliably:
@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 1000)            // or Debezium CDC
@Transactional
public void relayOutbox() {
    List<OutboxEvent> events = outboxRepo.findTop100ByPublishedNullOrderById();
    for (OutboxEvent event : events) {
        try { msgBus.publish(event.getType(), event.getPayload()); }
        catch (Exception e) { continue; }          // will retry next poll — never lost
        event.setPublished(Instant.now());         // marked only after successful publish
    }
}
```

```
Order TX
   ├─ INSERT orders
   └─ INSERT outbox (type, payload, idempotency_key)     ← same DB transaction
Outbox relay / CDC agent (Debezium) publishes to Kafka (Q212)
Kafka consumers deliver → consumer applies idempotently
```

| Variant | How | Use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Transactional outbox + scheduler** | Service polls `outbox` table | Simple, app-owned |
| **Transactional outbox + CDC** (Debezium) | DB binlog → Kafka, no code in service | High throughput, no cron window |
| **Dual write** (naive) | Write DB + publish directly — **NOT safe** | Avoid — loses events |

The outbox guarantees **at-least-once** delivery — so consumers must still be **idempotent** (below).

---

### 4. Idempotency — Making Retries Safe

An idempotent operation produces the same result whether it runs once or N times — essential for retried payments, webhooks, and saga compensations that might be replayed.

```java
// Server-side idempotency (REST — client-issued key):
@Entity
class IdempotencyRecord {
    @Id String key;                 // client sends Idempotency-Key header
    Long orderId;                   // successful result — reuse on replay
    Instant createdAt;
    LocalDateTime expiresAt;        // TTL cleanup
}

@Service
class OrderService {
    @Transactional
    Order charge(Order order, UUID idempotencyKey) {
        // If same key seen before → return stored result, do NOT re-charge:
        if (idempotencyRepo.existsById(idempotencyKey)) return orderRepo.findByKey(idempotencyKey);
        Order created = paymentProvider.charge(order);
        idempotencyRepo.save(new IdempotencyRecord(idempotencyKey, created.getId(), now));
        return created;
    }
}

// Consumer-side idempotency (Q212) — dedup by business key:
@KafkaListener(topics = "order-events")
void onOrderPlaced(OrderPlacedEvent evt) {
    if (processedRepo.existsById(evt.getEventId())) return;  // already handled
    process(evt); processedRepo.save(new Processed(evt.getEventId()));
}
```

**Idempotency requirements:**
- Unique **idempotency key** per logical operation (client-generated UUID, business key, event id)
- Same-key retry returns the **same result** (stored response) — never re-executes side effects
- **TTL** on stored keys (hours–days) + a cleanup job
- Idempotency check + execution in the **same transaction** (unique constraint on the key)

---

### 5. Compensation and Saga State

Sagas must persist their state — a crashed orchestrator must resume; a failed step must compensate exactly once.

```java
@Entity
class SagaInstance {
    @Id String sagaId;
    String name;                       // OrderSaga
    String currentStep;                // "charge-payment"
    String status;                     // RUNNING / COMPENSATING / COMPLETED / FAILED
    @Lob String stepDataJson;          // snapshot for resume/retry
    Instant createdAt;
}

// Orchestrator pattern: each step advances the state machine, each failure triggers compensation
// Compensations must be idempotent too — releasing released inventory is a no-op returning OK.
```

| Failure point | Compensation |
| :--- | :--- |
| Inventory reserved, payment failed | **Release inventory** (idempotent) |
| Payment charged, ship failed | **Refund payment** |
| Order confirmed, downstream audit failed | **Mark order as failed** |

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Try to use 2PC across services | Global locks, coordinator SPOF, tight coupling | Saga + outbox + idempotency (above) |
| Outbox relay publishes only once | If publish fails after DB commit — event lost | Keep retry loop; mark `published` only after success; or CDC |
| Consumer not idempotent | Duplicate order charges on retry/rebalance (Q212) | Idempotency key + unique constraint in same TX; dedup by event ID |
| Compensation not idempotent | Double refund, double release | Make each compensation idempotent (key stored, replay returns cached result) |
| Saga state only in memory | Orchestrator restart loses the flow | Persist `SagaInstance` + step/compensation state; replay or resume |
| Eventual consistency hidden | Users see stale "inventory available" | Expose saga status in UI/API; surface transient states explicitly |

### Summary

- **Distributed ACID (2PC)** couples services, holds global locks, and adds a single point of failure — teams use **BASE + Saga** instead: a sequence of local transactions with **compensating actions** (choreography = event-driven, orchestration = central coordinator with persisted state).
- **Outbox pattern** makes "write + publish" atomic — write the event to a DB table in the same TX as the domain change, then a relay (scheduler or Debezium CDC) delivers to Kafka — guaranteeing **at-least-once** delivery, never lost.
- **Idempotency** (client key / business key, stored result, TTL, unique constraint in same TX) makes retries and replays safe — required because messaging is at-least-once.
- Compensations must also be **idempotent**, and orchestrator/saga **state must be persisted** for crash recovery; surface eventual consistency in the product rather than hiding it.

---

## 224. How does RabbitMQ work and how does it compare to Kafka? Explain AMQP, exchanges, queues, dead-letter queues, and when to use each broker.

RabbitMQ and Kafka are the two dominant message brokers — but they solve different problems. RabbitMQ (AMQP) routes **each message to a consumer once** via exchanges and queues with fine-grained routing and DLQ support; Kafka is a **distributed log** that replays events to many consumer groups (Q212). Knowing the exchange model, consumer ACKs, and dead-lettering — plus when each fits — is a staple architecture interview question.

---

### 1. RabbitMQ / AMQP Core Model

```
   Producer ──► Exchange (fanout/topic/direct/headers)
      rabbitTemplate.convertAndSend("orders.exchange", "orders.created", payload)
                     │
                     └──► Queue(s) (bindings decide which queues get the message)
                              │
                              consumer: @RabbitListener(queues="orders")
   Each message routed to ONE consumer in the queue's consumer pool (no fan-out within a queue)
```

| AMQP entity | Role |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Producer** | Sends to an **exchange** with a **routing key** |
| **Exchange** | Routing hub — `direct` (exact key), `topic` (wildcards `*`/`#`), `fanout` (broadcast), `headers` |
| **Binding** | Rule connecting exchange → queue (key pattern) |
| **Queue** | Buffer consumed by one consumer instance per message (competing consumers) |
| **Consumer** | `@RabbitListener` — ACKs after successful processing |

```bash
# Queues/exchanges/bindings in RabbitMQ:
rabbitmqadmin declare queue name=orders durable=true
rabbitmqadmin declare exchange name=orders.exchange type=topic durable=true
rabbitmqadmin declare binding source=orders.exchange destination=orders routing_key=orders.#
```

---

### 2. Spring AMQP — `RabbitTemplate` and `@RabbitListener`

```java
// Producer:
@Service
class OrderProducer {
    @Autowired RabbitTemplate rabbit;

    void publishOrderCreated(Order order) {
        rabbit.convertAndSend("orders.exchange", "orders.created", order);
        // serializes the POJO (Jackson) and routes by key "orders.created"
    }
}

// Consumer:
@Component
class OrderConsumer {
    @RabbitListener(queues = "orders", concurrency = "3")   // 3 concurrent consumer threads
    void onOrder(Order order) {
        process(order);                  // throws → message rejected → DLQ/retry (below)
    }
}

// Configuration:
@Configuration
class RabbitConfig {
    @Bean Queue ordersQueue() { return QueueBuilder.durable("orders").deadLetterExchange("dlx.exchange").build(); }
    @Bean Queue deadLetterQueue() { return QueueBuilder.durable("orders.dlq").build(); }
    @Bean TopicExchange exchange() { return new TopicExchange("orders.exchange"); }
    @Bean Binding binding() { return BindingBuilder.bind(ordersQueue()).to(exchange()).with("orders.#"); }
    @Bean Binding dlqBinding() { return BindingBuilder.bind(deadLetterQueue()).to(new DirectExchange("dlx.exchange")).with("orders.dead"); }

    // AckMode + retry:
    @Bean RabbitListenerContainerFactory<Object> rabbitFactory(ConnectionFactory cf) {
        SimpleRabbitListenerContainerFactory f = new SimpleRabbitListenerContainerFactory();
        f.setConnectionFactory(cf);
        f.setAcknowledgeMode(AcknowledgeMode.MANUAL);        // you ACK after work
        f.setAdviceChain(RetryInterceptorBuilder.stateless()
            .maxAttempts(3).backOffOptions(1000, 2.0, 60_000).build());
        return f;
    }
}
```

**Delivery flow:** `convertAndSend(exchange, key, obj)` → exchange → bound queues → one consumer via `@RabbitListener`. ACK semantics: **automatic** (after method returns), **manual** (you `channel.basicAck(deliveryTag)`), or **reject/requeue**.

---

### 3. Message Routing — Topic, Direct, Fanout, Headers

```java
// Topic exchange — routing by pattern with * (one word) and # (zero+ words):
rabbit.convertAndSend("orders.exchange", "orders.created", order);
rabbit.convertAndSend("orders.exchange", "orders.updated", order);
rabbit.convertAndSend("orders.exchange", "orders.cancelled", order);

// Bindings:
//   queue "orders"       binding orders.#      ← matches all three
//   queue "orders.topic" binding orders.*.created  ← only orders.created
//   queue "all-events"   binding #              ← everything

// Direct exchange — exact routing key (default exchange "" routes by queue name):
rabbit.convertAndSend("", "orders", order);  // → queue "orders"

// Fanout — broadcast to ALL bound queues (no routing key filtering):
rabbit.convertAndSend("audit.exchange", "", new AuditEvent(order));
```

| Exchange | Routing | Example use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `direct` | Exact `routingKey` | Command to a specific queue |
| `topic` | Pattern `orders.*` / `orders.#` | Event routing by type |
| `fanout` | Broadcast to all bound queues | Audit/log fan-out |
| `headers` | Match on message headers | Arbitrary routing criteria |

---

### 4. Dead-Letter Queues (DLQ) and Retries

```java
@Configuration
class RetryConfig {
    // Retry queue chain: orders → orders.retry (delay) → back to orders → orders.dlq
    @Bean Queue ordersQueue() {
        return QueueBuilder.durable("orders")
            .deadLetterExchange("retry.exchange").deadLetterRoutingKey("orders.retry").build();
    }
    @Bean Queue retryQueue() {
        return QueueBuilder.durable("orders.retry")
            .ttl(5000)                                             // wait 5s in retry
            .deadLetterExchange("orders.exchange").deadLetterRoutingKey("orders.created") // back to main
            .build();
    }
```
