```java
    @Bean Queue dlq() { return QueueBuilder.durable("orders.dlq").build(); }
}

// Consumer — reject messages you can't process after retries (moves to DLQ):
@RabbitListener(queues = "orders")
void onOrder(Order order, Channel channel, @Header(AmqpHeaders.DELIVERY_TAG) long tag) throws IOException {
    try { process(order); channel.basicAck(tag, false); }
    catch (PermanentFailureException e) { channel.basicReject(tag, false); }  // false = don't requeue → DLQ
    catch (TransientFailureException e) {
        if (attempt < 3) channel.basicNack(tag, false, true);   // true = requeue (retry)
        else channel.basicReject(tag, false);                    // give up → DLQ
    }
}

// Or declarative with retry interceptor: maxAttempts, backoff → then message goes to DLQ automatically
```

**Retry loop danger:** requeueing a poisoned message forever = retry storm. Always cap attempts (interceptor / TTL retry queue / DLQ with alerting).

---

### 5. RabbitMQ vs Kafka

```
RabbitMQ (message broker):      Kafka (distributed log):
  Push model (as fast as consumer)    Pull model (consumer decides read rate)
  1 message → 1 consumer in a queue   same event → N consumer groups (all groups get all)
  Memory/disk queues, TTL, DLQ, ACKs  Durable replay — retention by time/size
  Routing: exchanges/keys             Partition key → partition
  Higher per-message overhead         High throughput (millions msg/s)
  Best: task/command queues, workers  Best: event streams, fan-out, replays, audit
```

| Dimension | RabbitMQ | Kafka |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Model | Queue (competition) | Log (log + offsets) |
| Delivery | Push to consumers | Consumer **pulls** + commits offset |
| Multi-consumer same event | No (unless fanout to separate queues) | Yes — each consumer **group** gets every event |
| Replay | No (consumed → gone) | Yes — rewind offset, replay history |
| Ordering | Per-queue (single consumer) | Per-partition (key) |
| Message lifecycle | Consumed → removed | Retained by retention policy |
| Throughput | ~thousands–tens of thousands msg/s | ~millions msg/s |
| Use cases | Job queues, RPC, work distribution | Event sourcing, stream processing, audit, fan-out |

**Practical guide:**
- **Work queue / task distribution** (email send, PDF generation, image resizing) → **RabbitMQ** — each task to one worker, retries + DLQ.
- **Event stream / data pipeline** (order events → analytics, audits, multiple consumers) → **Kafka** — replay, fan-out, high volume.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Infinite requeue of poisoned message | Retry storm, queue grows, memory dies | Cap attempts; route to DLQ after N; alert on DLQ depth |
| Declaring `non-durable` queues in prod | Messages lost on broker restart | `durable=true` + `persistent` delivery mode |
| ACKing before work completes | Message loss on consumer crash mid-work | ACK **after** processing (manual ack) |
| Topic vs direct mismatch in key names | Messages silently unrouted (no matching binding) | Test routing keys; use `#` wildcard carefully; monitor unroutable count |
| Using Kafka for classic task queues | Can't ACK one task consumed by one worker cleanly | Choose RabbitMQ for competing-consumer work queues |
| No DLQ alerting | Poison messages sit forever, unnoticed | Monitor `rabbitmq_queue_messages_unacknowledged` / DLQ depth; dead letter → alert |

### Summary

- RabbitMQ (AMQP) = **exchange → queue → one consumer** per message: `direct`/`topic`/`fanout`/`headers` routing, competing consumers, manual ACK, and **DLQ with TTL-based retry** — ideal for task queues and work distribution.
- Kafka = **distributed log with consumer groups**: every group sees every event, offsets enable **replay**, per-partition ordering and massive throughput — ideal for event streams, analytics, and fan-out.
- Choose RabbitMQ for **point-to-point tasks** (retries + DLQ, per-message routing), Kafka for **event-driven streaming** (replay, fan-out, high volume); both integrate via Spring (`RabbitTemplate`/`@RabbitListener` vs `KafkaTemplate`/`@KafkaListener`, Q212).
- Watch out for retry storms, non-durable queues, ACK-before-work, and unrouted keys — and always pair at-least-once consumers with idempotency (Q223).

---

## 225. How does search with Elasticsearch work in Spring Boot? Explain inverted indexes, mapping, queries, aggregations, and Spring Data Elasticsearch.

Relational databases are poor at full-text search ("find orders containing 'wireless headphones' ranked by relevance") — they scan rows. Elasticsearch is a **distributed search and analytics engine** built on Apache Lucene: it tokenizes text into an **inverted index** (`term → document list`) and ranks results with **TF-IDF / BM25**. Spring Data Elasticsearch maps your domain objects to ES documents via `ElasticsearchRepository` and `ElasticsearchOperations`.

---

### 1. Core Concepts — Inverted Index, Mapping, and Analysis

```
Relational (scan):         "WHERE name LIKE '%wireless%'"  → full table scan, slow

Elasticsearch (inverted index):
  Doc 1: "wireless headphones"  → tokens: [wireless, headphones]
  Doc 2: "wired headphones"     → tokens: [wired, headphones]
  Inverted index:  wireless → [1], headphones → [1,2], wired → [2]
  Query "wireless" → O(1) lookup → [1]  → ranked by BM25 score
```

| Concept | Meaning |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Index** | Collection of documents (like a table) — e.g., `orders`, `products` |
| **Document** | JSON object (like a row) — `{ "id": 1, "name": "wireless headphones", "price": 99.99 }` |
| **Mapping** | Schema — field types (`text` vs `keyword`), analyzers, nested objects |
| **Analyzer** | Pipeline: char filter → tokenizer → token filters (lowercase, stemmer, synonym) |
| **Shard** | Partition of an index — distributed across nodes for scale |
| **Replica** | Copy of a shard — for HA and read scaling |

```json
// Mapping — defines how each field is stored and searched:
PUT /products
{
  "mappings": {
    "properties": {
      "id":          { "type": "keyword" },
      "name":        { "type": "text",    "analyzer": "standard" },
      "description": { "type": "text",    "analyzer": "english" },
      "price":       { "type": "double" },
      "category":    { "type": "keyword" },
      "createdAt":   { "type": "date" },
      "tags":        { "type": "keyword" }
    }
  },
  "settings": {
    "number_of_shards": 3,
    "number_of_replicas": 1,
    "analysis": {
      "analyzer": {
        "autocomplete": { "type": "custom", "tokenizer": "edge_ngram", "filter": ["lowercase"] }
      }
    }
  }
}
```

| Field type | When to use | Searched how |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `text` | Full-text search (name, description) | Analyzed → tokenized → BM25 ranked |
| `keyword` | Exact match, filter, aggregation (id, category, status) | Not analyzed — exact value |
| `double` / `long` | Numeric range | Range queries, aggregations |
| `date` | Timestamps | Date range, histogram aggregation |
| `nested` | Arrays of objects that need independent querying | Each object indexed separately |

---

### 2. Spring Data Elasticsearch — `ElasticsearchRepository`

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-data-elasticsearch</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```yaml
spring:
  elasticsearch:
    uris: http://localhost:9200
  data:
    elasticsearch:
      repositories:
        enabled: true
```

```java
@Document(indexName = "products")
class Product {
    @Id
    private String id;

    @Field(type = FieldType.Text, analyzer = "standard") String name;
    @Field(type = FieldType.Text, analyzer = "english") String description;
    @Field(type = FieldType.Double) double price;
    @Field(type = FieldType.Keyword) String category;
    @Field(type = FieldType.Date, format = DateFormat.date_time) Instant createdAt;
    @Field(type = FieldType.Keyword) List<String> tags;

    // constructors, getters, setters...
}

public interface ProductRepository extends ElasticsearchRepository<Product, String> {

    // Derived query — Spring parses the method name:
    List<Product> findByName(String name);
    List<Product> findByCategory(String category);
    Page<Product> findByPriceBetween(double min, double max, Pageable pageable);

    // Custom query — native ES JSON:
    @Query("{\"match\": {\"name\": \"?0\"}}")
    List<Product> searchByName(String name);

    // Highlight:
    @Highlight(fields = @HighlightField(name = "description"))
    List<SearchHit<Product>> searchWithHighlight(String query);
}
```

```java
@Service
class ProductService {
    private final ProductRepository repo;

    Product save(Product p) { return repo.save(p); }       // index one doc
    void saveAll(List<Product> ps) { repo.saveAll(ps); }   // bulk index
    Optional<Product> findById(String id) { return repo.findById(id); }
    void delete(String id) { repo.deleteById(id); }

    // Derived query via the interface:
    List<Product> byCategory(String cat) { return repo.findByCategory(cat); }
}
```

---

### 3. Queries — Full-Text, Bool, Filters, and Sorting

```java
// ElasticsearchOperations — programmatic queries (most flexible):
@Service
class ProductSearchService {
    private final ElasticsearchOperations ops;

    List<Product> fullTextSearch(String query) {
        Query q = new NativeQueryBuilder()
            .withQuery(QueryBuilders.match(m -> m.field("name").query(query)))
            .withSort(Sort.by(Sort.Order.desc("_score")))  // BM25 relevance first
            .withPageable(PageRequest.of(0, 20))
            .build();
        return ops.search(q, Product.class).stream().map(SearchHit::getContent).toList();
    }

    // Bool query — must / should / must_not / filter:
    List<Product> boolSearch(String name, String category, Double minPrice) {
        Query q = new NativeQueryBuilder()
            .withQuery(QueryBuilders.bool(b -> {
                if (name != null)     b.must(QueryBuilders.match(m -> m.field("name").query(name)));
                if (category != null) b.filter(QueryBuilders.term(t -> t.field("category").value(category)));
                if (minPrice != null) b.filter(QueryBuilders.range(r -> r.field("price").gte(JsonData.of(minPrice))));
                return b;
            }))
            .build();
        return ops.search(q, Product.class).stream().map(SearchHit::getContent).toList();
    }

    // Autocomplete / prefix:
    List<Product> autocomplete(String prefix) {
        Query q = new NativeQueryBuilder()
            .withQuery(QueryBuilders.prefix(p -> p.field("name").value(prefix.toLowerCase())))
            .build();
        return ops.search(q, Product.class).stream().map(SearchHit::getContent).toList();
    }
}
```

| Query type | Use |
| :--- | :--- |
| `match` | Full-text — analyzed, BM25 ranked |
| `term` | Exact `keyword` filter — not analyzed |
| `range` | Numeric/date range (`gte`, `lte`) |
| `bool` | Combine `must` (scored) + `filter` (not scored, cached) + `should` + `must_not` |
| `prefix` / `wildcard` | Autocomplete — use `edge_ngram` analyzer for production autocomplete |
| `nested` | Query inside nested arrays |

**`filter` vs `must`:** `filter` context is **not scored** and is **cached** — use it for `category`, `price range`, `status` (exact filters). `must` is scored (affects BM25 rank) — use it for the full-text part.

---

### 4. Aggregations — Analytics Without SQL

```java
// Aggregation — "count products per category" (like GROUP BY):
SearchHits<Product> hits = ops.search(
    new NativeQueryBuilder()
        .withQuery(QueryBuilders.matchAll(m -> m))
        .withAggregation("by_category", AggregationBuilders.terms(t -> t.field("category")))
        .build(), Product.class);

// Extract buckets:
ElasticsearchAggregations aggs = (ElasticsearchAggregations) hits.getAggregations();
StringTermsAggregate byCategory = aggs.get("by_category");
byCategory.buckets().forEach(b -> System.out.println(b.key() + ": " + b.docCount()));

// More aggregations:
NativeQueryBuilder()
    .withAggregation("avg_price", AggregationBuilders.avg(a -> a.field("price")))
    .withAggregation("price_histogram", AggregationBuilders.histogram(h -> h.field("price").interval(50)))
    .withAggregation("by_month", AggregationBuilders.dateHistogram(d -> d.field("createdAt").calendarInterval(CalendarInterval.MONTH)))
    .build();
```

| Aggregation | Equivalent SQL | Use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `terms` | `GROUP BY category` | Facets, counts per category |
| `avg` / `sum` / `min` / `max` | `AVG(price)` | Stats |
| `histogram` | `GROUP BY price/50` | Price distribution |
| `date_histogram` | `GROUP BY month` | Time-series counts |

Aggregations run **without fetching documents** — they scan the inverted index directly, so they're fast even on millions of docs.

---

### 5. Syncing Data — Keeping ES and the Primary DB Consistent

```
Primary DB (PostgreSQL/MySQL) ──► Elasticsearch

Option A — Application dual-write:
  @Transactional
  void create(Product p) { jpaRepo.save(p); esRepo.save(p); }
  Problem: not atomic — DB succeeds, ES fails → out of sync.

Option B — Outbox + CDC (Q223) — recommended:
  DB TX writes row + outbox event → Debezium tails the binlog → publishes to Kafka → consumer indexes into ES
  Eventually consistent, never lost, no dual-write.

Option C — Periodic full reindex:
  @Scheduled(cron = "0 2 * * * *")  // nightly
  void reindex() { List<Product> all = jpaRepo.findAll(); esRepo.saveAll(all); }
  Simple but stale between runs; good as a safety net alongside B.
```

Always treat the **primary DB as the source of truth** — Elasticsearch is a **derived, rebuildable index**. If ES is lost, reindex from the DB.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Using `text` for `category` / filter fields | `category:electronics` doesn't match (analyzed → tokens) | Use `keyword` for exact filters/aggregations; `text` only for full-text |
| No `refresh` after bulk index | Newly indexed docs not searchable immediately | `refresh=true` for tests; default (1s `refresh_interval`) is fine for prod |
| Large `from`/`size` pagination (deep pagination) | `from: 100000` is slow — ES must collect 100k docs | Use `search_after` (cursor) or `scroll` API for deep pages |
| Dual-write without outbox | DB and ES drift on failure | Outbox + CDC (Q223) or at minimum retry + reconciliation job |
| Mapping explosion (too many fields) | `Limit of total fields [1000] has been exceeded` | Limit dynamic fields; use `dynamic: strict` or `false`; flatten nested objects |
| Analyzer mismatch (query vs index) | "wireless" doesn't match "Wireless" | Same analyzer at index and search time; test with `POST /products/_analyze` |

### Summary

- Elasticsearch stores documents in an **inverted index** (`term → doc list`) analyzed by tokenizers/filters — `text` for full-text (BM25 ranked), `keyword` for exact filter/aggregation.
- **Spring Data Elasticsearch** maps `@Document` classes to indexes via `ElasticsearchRepository` (derived queries, `@Query` JSON) and `ElasticsearchOperations` (`NativeQueryBuilder` with `match`/`bool`/`range`).
- **Queries**: `match` (scored, full-text) + `filter` (cached, not scored) inside `bool`; **aggregations** (`terms`/`avg`/`histogram`/`date_histogram`) replace `GROUP BY` without fetching docs.
- Keep ES **eventually consistent** via outbox + CDC (Q223) — treat the primary DB as truth, ES as a rebuildable derived index; use `search_after` for deep pagination and `keyword` vs `text` correctly.

---

## 226. How do you build CI/CD and deploy Spring Boot microservices? Explain pipelines, deployment strategies, and feature flags.

CI/CD turns "it works on my machine" into reliable, automated delivery — every commit builds, tests, scans, and deploys without manual steps. For microservices you need **pipeline stages** (build → test → image → scan → deploy), **deployment strategies** (rolling, blue-green, canary) that reduce risk, and **feature flags** that decouple deploy from release.

---

### 1. The Pipeline — From Commit to Production

```
Commit → Build → Unit Tests → Integration Tests → Build Image → Scan → Push → Deploy → Smoke Tests
  git      mvn      JUnit         Testcontainers       Docker     Trivy   Registry   K8s/ECS    health check
  push   package   (Q209)          (Q209)              build     CVE    (ECR/GCR)  rollout    /actuator/health
```

```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml — GitHub Actions
name: CI/CD

on:
  push: { branches: [main] }
  pull_request: { branches: [main] }

jobs:
  build-and-test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with: { java-version: '21', distribution: 'temurin', cache: 'maven' }
      - run: ./mvnw -B verify                          # compiles, runs unit + integration tests
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4                # keep JAR for next job
        with: { name: app-jar, path: target/*.jar }

  docker:
    needs: build-and-test
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v5
        with:
          context: .
          push: true
          tags: ghcr.io/company/order-service:${{ github.sha }}
          cache-from: type=gha
          cache-to: type=gha,mode=max
      - run: trivy image ghcr.io/company/order-service:${{ github.sha }}

  deploy-staging:
    needs: docker
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: staging
    steps:
      - run: |
          kubectl set image deployment/order-service \
            order-service=ghcr.io/company/order-service:${{ github.sha }} -n staging
          kubectl rollout status deployment/order-service -n staging --timeout=120s
          curl -f https://order-staging.example.com/actuator/health  # smoke test
```

| Stage | Tool | What it does |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Build | Maven/Gradle | `verify` — compile + test + package |
| Test | JUnit, Testcontainers (Q209) | Unit + slice + integration |
| Image | Docker BuildKit, Jib, `spring-boot:build-image` | Layered image (Q215) + layer cache |
| Scan | Trivy, Snyk, Grype | CVE scan on base image + deps |
| Push | ECR/GCR/GHCR | Immutable tag per commit (`:sha`, not `:latest`) |
| Deploy | `kubectl`, Helm, ArgoCD | Rollout to K8s |
| Smoke | `curl /actuator/health` (Q214) | Fail fast if new pods are broken |

Tag immutably (`:abc123`, not `:latest`) — rollback is just `kubectl set image ... :previous-sha`.

---

### 2. Deployment Strategies

| Strategy | How | Downtime | Risk | Rollback |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Rolling** | Replace pods one-by-one (`maxSurge`/`maxUnavailable`) | None | Medium — mixed versions briefly | Automatic (`rollout undo`) |
| **Blue-Green** | Two full envs (blue=live, green=new) — switch Service/ingress | None | Low — instant switch + instant rollback | Flip Service back |
| **Canary** | 5–10% of traffic to new version, then gradually 100% | None | Lowest — real users validate before full rollout | Reduce canary weight to 0 |
| **Feature flag** | Same binary, flag toggles new code path per user/percent | None | Lowest — no redeploy to change behavior | Flip flag off |

```yaml
# Rolling (default in Kubernetes):
spec:
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate: { maxSurge: 25%, maxUnavailable: 0 }  # always keep old pods until new are ready

# Blue-Green — two Deployments, one Service selector flip:
#   Service initially: selector: { app: order-service, version: blue }
#   Deploy green: kubectl apply -f deployment-green.yaml
#   Switch: kubectl patch svc order-service -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"version":"green"}}}'
#   Rollback: patch back to blue

# Canary with Argo Rollouts / Flagger (progressive delivery):
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Rollout
spec:
  strategy:
    canary:
      steps:
        - setWeight: 10          # 10% to canary
        - pause: { duration: 5m } # observe metrics
        - analysis:               # auto-promote or abort based on metrics
            templates: [{ templateName: success-rate }]
            args: [{ name: service, value: order-service }]
        - setWeight: 50
        - pause: { duration: 5m }
        - setWeight: 100
```

```
Rolling:   v1 v1 v1 → v2 v1 v1 → v2 v2 v1 → v2 v2 v2   (gradual pod replacement)
Blue-Green: [blue: v1 v1 v1] + [green: v2 v2 v2] → flip Service → [green live]
Canary:    [stable: 90%] + [canary: 10%] → metrics OK? → 50/50 → 100% canary → stable replaced
```

---

### 3. Feature Flags — Decouple Deploy from Release

```java
// Flag provider — LaunchDarkly, Unleash, ConfigCat, or simple Config Server (Q218):
@Component
class FeatureFlags {
    private final LDClient ldClient;  // or UnleashClient

    boolean isNewCheckoutEnabled(String userId) {
        return ldClient.boolVariation("new-checkout", LDUser.builder(userId).build(), false);
    }
}

@RestController
class CheckoutController {
    @PostMapping("/checkout")
    ResponseEntity<Order> checkout(@RequestBody CheckoutRequest req, @AuthenticationPrincipal User user) {
        if (featureFlags.isNewCheckoutEnabled(user.getId())) {
            return ResponseEntity.ok(newCheckoutService.checkout(req));
        }
        return ResponseEntity.ok(legacyCheckoutService.checkout(req));
    }
}
```

```yaml
# Simple flag via Config Server / application.yml (Q218):
app:
  features:
    new-checkout: false   # flip to true per env without redeploy (bus-refresh)
```

| Flag type | Use |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Release flag** | Hide unfinished feature until ready — deploy anytime, release when QA passes |
| **Experiment flag** | A/B test — 50% of users see variant A, 50% variant B |
| **Ops flag** | Kill switch — disable a failing dependency instantly |
| **Permission flag** | Entitlement — premium users see extra features |

Flags need **cleanup**: remove the flag and dead code once the feature is fully rolled out — otherwise flags accumulate and become tech debt.

---

### 4. Database Migrations in CI/CD

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.flywaydb</groupId>
    <artifactId>flyway-core</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```sql
-- V1__create_orders.sql
CREATE TABLE orders (id BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY, status VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL, total NUMERIC(10,2));

-- V2__add_customer_id.sql — additive, backward-compatible:
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN customer_id BIGINT;
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_customer ON orders(customer_id);

-- V3__add_currency.sql — expand/contract pattern for renames:
-- Phase 1 (deploy): add new column, dual-write both
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN currency VARCHAR(3) DEFAULT 'USD';
-- Phase 2 (next deploy): backfill, switch reads to new column
-- Phase 3 (later): drop old column
```

| Rule | Why |
| :--- | :--- |
| Migrations are **forward-only**, never edit old `V__` files | Flyway checksum would fail; history is immutable |
| Additive changes first (add column, not rename) | Rolling deploy has old + new code running simultaneously |
| Expand/contract for renames | Old code reads old column, new code reads new — no downtime |
| `baselineOnMigrate` for existing DBs | Bootstraps Flyway on a DB that already has tables |

---

### 5. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Tagging images `:latest` | Rollback pulls wrong image; cache confusion | Immutable tags (`:${sha}` or `:${semver}-${sha}`) |
| No health check in pipeline | Broken deploy goes live, 503s | `readinessProbe` (Q215) + smoke test `curl /actuator/health` after rollout |
| Big-bang deploy (all at once) | Outage affects 100% of users | Canary or blue-green; feature flags for risky changes |
| Flag left forever | Dead code, flag debt, conditional hell | Track flags, set expiry, clean up after 100% rollout |
| DB migration breaks old pods during rolling deploy | Old code crashes on new schema | Expand/contract — additive migrations only; dual-write during transition |
| No image CVE scan | Vulnerable base image ships to prod | Trivy/Snyk in pipeline — fail the build on HIGH/CRITICAL CVEs |

### Summary

- CI/CD pipeline: **commit → build (`verify`) → tests (Q209) → layered image (Q215) → CVE scan → push (immutable `:sha` tag) → deploy (K8s/Helm/ArgoCD) → smoke test (`/actuator/health`, Q214)**.
- Deployment strategies: **rolling** (pod-by-pod, default), **blue-green** (two envs, Service flip, instant rollback), **canary** (progressive traffic shift with metric analysis via Argo/Flagger).
- **Feature flags** (LaunchDarkly/Unleash or Config Server) decouple deploy from release — release, experiment, ops, and permission flags; clean up after 100% rollout.
- DB migrations: **Flyway** forward-only `V__` scripts, **expand/contract** for zero-downtime schema changes during rolling deploys.

---

## 227. How do you tune and profile JVM / Spring Boot performance? Explain JFR, async-profiler, heap dumps, and GC tuning.

Performance work starts with measurement — guessing wastes time. The JVM ships built-in profilers (JFR, `jcmd`, `jstat`) and pairs with low-overhead native profilers (async-profiler) that reveal CPU hot spots, allocations, and lock contention via **flame graphs**. Combined with heap dump analysis and **GC tuning** (G1/ZGC flags), you can diagnose latency spikes, memory leaks, and throughput bottlenecks without restarting production.

---

### 1. Observability First — What to Measure

| Signal | Tool | What it tells you |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| CPU hot spots | async-profiler, JFR `jdk.ExecutionSample` | Which methods burn CPU |
| Allocations | JFR `jdk.ObjectAllocationInNewTLAB`, async-profiler `alloc` | Who creates the most garbage |
| Lock contention | JFR `jdk.JavaMonitorEnter`, async-profiler `lock` | Threads blocked on `synchronized` |
| Heap usage | `jstat -gc`, `/actuator/metrics/jvm.memory.*` (Q214) | Old Gen growth → leak? |
| GC pauses | GC log, JFR `jdk.GCPhasePause`, `jstat` | Pause time, frequency, promotion rate |
| Latency (p50/p99) | Micrometer `http.server.requests` (Q214) | Where the user feels it |

```yaml
# Enable JFR + GC logging at startup:
# JVM flags:
# -XX:StartFlightRecording=filename=app.jfr,duration=60s
# -Xlog:gc*:file=/var/log/gc.log:time,uptime:filecount=5,filesize=20M
```

---

### 2. Java Flight Recorder (JFR) — Built-In, Low Overhead

JFR records JVM events (method samples, allocations, I/O, GC) with <1% overhead — safe for production.

```bash
# Start a 60s recording on a running JVM:
jcmd <pid> JFR.start name=profile duration=60s filename=/tmp/app.jfr

# Dump on demand:
jcmd <pid> JFR.dump filename=/tmp/dump.jfr

# Check status:
jcmd <pid> JFR.check

# Analyze with JDK Mission Control (JMC) GUI:
jmc /tmp/app.jfr   # open the .jfr file — CPU, memory, threads, GC tabs

# Or CLI:
jfr print --events jdk.ExecutionSample /tmp/app.jfr | head
jfr summary /tmp/app.jfr
```

```java
// Continuous JFR via Spring Boot (JDK 17+):
// application.yml:
management.endpoint.jfr.enabled: true  // if using spring-boot-starter-actuator + JFR
// Or programmatically:
FlightRecorder.register(MyCustomEvent.class);

@Name("com.example.OrderCreated")
@Label("Order Created") @Category("Business")
class OrderCreatedEvent extends Event {
    @Label("Order ID") long orderId;
    @Label("Total") double total;
}
// Usage:
var e = new OrderCreatedEvent(); e.orderId = 42; e.total = 99.0;
e.begin(); /* ... work ... */ e.commit();  // appears in JFR timeline
```

JFR event categories: `jdk.ExecutionSample` (CPU), `jdk.ObjectAllocationInNewTLAB` (alloc), `jdk.JavaMonitorEnter` (locks), `jdk.ThreadPark`, `jdk.GCPhasePause`, `jdk.SocketRead/Write`.

---

### 3. async-profiler — Low-Overhead Native Profiler + Flame Graphs

async-profiler uses `perf_events` / `AsyncGetCallTrace` — no safepoint bias, <2% overhead, works on any OpenJDK.

```bash
# CPU profiling — 30s, output flame graph:
./profiler.sh -d 30 -f /tmp/cpu.html --title "CPU" <pid>

# Allocation profiling — who allocates the most:
./profiler.sh -d 30 -e alloc -f /tmp/alloc.html <pid>

# Lock contention:
./profiler.sh -d 30 -e lock -f /tmp/lock.html <pid>

# Live mode with JFR output:
./profiler.sh -d 30 -o jfr -f /tmp/profile.jfr <pid>
```

```
Flame graph — read bottom-up:
  [all threads]  ← bottom (root)
    └─ http-nio-8080-exec-5
        └─ OrderController.create
            └─ OrderService.enrich        ← 35% of samples — hot spot!
                └─ Jackson ObjectMapper.writeValue  ← 20% — serialization cost
```

Flame graph width = sample count — wide frames are hot. Look for unexpected width (e.g., `String concatenation in a loop`, `reflection`, `synchronized`).

**Spring Boot integration:**

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>io.github.jvm-profiling-tools</groupId>
    <artifactId>async-profiler-spring-boot-starter</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```bash
# Trigger via actuator:
curl -X POST http://localhost:8080/actuator/profiler/start
curl http://localhost:8080/actuator/profiler/flamegraph > flame.html
```

---

### 4. Heap Dump Analysis — Finding Memory Leaks

```bash
# Trigger a heap dump:
jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump /tmp/heap.hprof
jmap -dump:live,format=b,file=/tmp/heap.hprof <pid>   # alternative (pauses JVM briefly)

# Auto-dump on OOM:
java -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError -XX:HeapDumpPath=/tmp/heap.hprof -jar app.jar

# Analyze:
# 1. Eclipse MAT (Memory Analyzer) — open heap.hprof → Leak Suspects report
# 2. JVisualVM / JMC
# 3. CLI: jhat /tmp/heap.hprof  (then browse http://localhost:7000)
```

```
MAT — Leak Suspects:
  1. com.example.OrderCache — 450 MB (42% of heap) — 1.2M Order objects retained
     → Dominator tree: OrderCache → ConcurrentHashMap → Order[] → ...
     → Fix: add TTL/eviction (Q210) or use Caffeine with maximumSize
```

| Heap dump view | What it shows |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Histogram** | Count + shallow/retained size per class — find the biggest retainers |
| **Dominator tree** | Which objects keep others alive — trace the leak root |
| **Path to GC roots** | Why an object isn't collected — who holds the reference |
| **Leak Suspects** | MAT's automated report — top 3 likely leaks |

Common leaks: unbounded `static Map` / cache without eviction, `ThreadLocal` not cleared (Q211), unclosed `InputStream`/`Connection`, `ClassLoader` leak on hot redeploy.

---

### 5. GC Tuning — Reading Logs and Choosing Flags

```bash
# GC log (JDK 17+ unified logging):
java -Xlog:gc*:file=/var/log/gc.log:time,uptime:filecount=5,filesize=20M \
     -XX:+UseG1GC -Xms1g -Xmx1g -jar app.jar

# Live GC stats:
jstat -gc <pid> 1s        # heap regions, GC count, time
jstat -gcutil <pid> 1s    # % used per generation
jcmd <pid> GC.heap_info   # region sizes
```

```
GC log snippet (G1):
[0.234s][info][gc] GC(0) Pause Young (Normal) (G1 Evacuation Pause) 512M->48M(1024M) 12.3ms
[1.102s][info][gc] GC(1) Pause Young (Normal) (G1 Evacuation Pause) 560M->52M(1024M) 14.1ms
[5.891s][info][gc] GC(5) Pause Full (G1 Compaction Pause) 900M->120M(1024M) 145ms  ← Full GC — investigate
```

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Frequent Young GC (<100ms interval) | Allocation rate too high | Reduce allocations (reuse buffers, avoid `String` concat in loops) |
| Frequent Full GC | Old Gen fills — leak or promotion failure | Heap dump (above); increase `-Xmx` or fix leak; tune `InitiatingHeapOccupancyPercent` |
| Long pauses (>100ms p99) | G1 not keeping up; humongous objects | Switch to ZGC/Shenandoah (Q199) for sub-ms pauses; fix large allocations |
| Promotion failure | Survivor/S Old Gen too small | Increase heap or tune `G1ReservePercent`, `MaxGCPauseMillis` |

```bash
# G1 tuning (throughput + bounded pauses):
java -XX:+UseG1GC -Xms2g -Xmx2g \
     -XX:MaxGCPauseMillis=100 \
     -XX:G1HeapRegionSize=8m \
     -XX:InitiatingHeapOccupancyPercent=45 \
     -jar app.jar

# ZGC — sub-ms pauses, large heaps (Q199):
java -XX:+UseZGC -Xms4g -Xmx4g -XX:+ZGenerational -jar app.jar

# Container-aware (Q215):
java -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0 -XX:+UseG1GC -jar app.jar
```

Heap sizing rule: `Xmx` ≈ 50–75% of container `limits.memory` — leave headroom for off-heap (direct buffers, metaspace, thread stacks).

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Profiling only in dev, not prod | Prod-only hot spots missed (real data shape) | JFR + async-profiler are prod-safe (<2% overhead) — profile in prod |
| `jmap -histo:live` triggers Full GC | Latency spike during profiling | Use `jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump` (less intrusive) or JFR allocation events |
| No GC log enabled | Blind to pause causes | Always enable `-Xlog:gc*` — cheap, invaluable for post-mortem |
| Tuning GC before fixing allocations | Endless flag tweaking, no real gain | Flame graph first — fix hot allocations, then tune GC |
| Ignoring `ThreadLocal` leaks | Heap grows after each request | `MDC.clear()` in filter (Q214); `TaskDecorator` for async (Q211) |
| `Xmx` = container limit | OOMKilled by K8s (off-heap exceeds limit) | `MaxRAMPercentage=75%` — leave 25% for non-heap |

### Summary

- Start with **metrics** (Q214 — `jvm.memory.*`, `http.server.requests` p99) then profile — **JFR** (`jcmd JFR.start`, <1% overhead, `jdk.ExecutionSample`/`Allocation`/`MonitorEnter`) and **async-profiler** (CPU/alloc/lock flame graphs, no safepoint bias) pinpoint hot spots.
- **Heap dumps** (`jcmd GC.heap_dump` + Eclipse MAT) find leaks via histogram/dominator tree/path-to-GC-roots — common culprits: unbounded caches, `ThreadLocal`, unclosed resources.
- **GC logs** (`-Xlog:gc*`, `jstat -gc`) reveal pause frequency and promotion failures; tune G1 (`MaxGCPauseMillis`, `InitiatingHeapOccupancyPercent`) or switch to ZGC for sub-ms pauses — always leave heap headroom (`MaxRAMPercentage=75%`).
- Never tune GC before fixing allocations — flame graph first, flags second; JFR + async-profiler are safe for production.

---

## 228. How do you handle real-time communication in Spring Boot? Explain WebSockets, STOMP, Server-Sent Events (SSE), and when to use each.

HTTP is request/response — the client asks, the server answers, then the connection closes. Real-time features (chat, live notifications, collaborative editing, order status updates) need the server to **push** data without the client polling. Spring Boot provides **WebSockets** (full-duplex), **STOMP over WebSocket** (message routing), and **Server-Sent Events** (one-way streaming) — each suited to a different real-time pattern.

---

### 1. Polling vs Long Polling vs SSE vs WebSocket

```
Polling:        Client ──GET /orders/5──► Server (every 2s) — wasteful, stale between polls
Long polling:   Client ──GET /orders/5──► Server holds until new data or timeout — better, but still one-shot
SSE:            Client ──GET /events──► Server ──► event ──► event ──► event (one-way stream, auto-reconnect)
WebSocket:      Client ◄──ws://──► Server (full-duplex — either side pushes anytime, one TCP connection)
```

| Approach | Direction | Overhead | Reconnect | Use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Polling** | Client → Server | High (many empty responses) | N/A | Simple, low-frequency checks |
| **Long polling** | Client → Server (held) | Medium | Manual | Legacy fallback |
| **SSE** | Server → Client (one-way) | Low (single HTTP connection) | Auto (browser `EventSource`) | Live feeds, notifications, progress |
| **WebSocket** | Both ways (full-duplex) | Lowest per message (no HTTP headers) | Manual | Chat, collaborative editing, gaming |

---

### 2. Server-Sent Events (SSE) — One-Way Server Push

```java
// SSE with Spring MVC — SseEmitter (blocking, one thread per connection):
@RestController
class NotificationController {
    private final CopyOnWriteArrayList<SseEmitter> emitters = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>();

    @GetMapping("/notifications/stream")
    SseEmitter stream() {
        SseEmitter emitter = new SseEmitter(60_000L);  // 60s timeout
        emitters.add(emitter);
        emitter.onCompletion(() -> emitters.remove(emitter));
        emitter.onTimeout(() -> emitters.remove(emitter));
        return emitter;
    }

    void pushToAll(String message) {
        for (SseEmitter emitter : emitters) {
            try { emitter.send(SseEmitter.event().name("notification").data(message)); }
            catch (IOException e) { emitters.remove(emitter); }
        }
    }
}

// SSE with WebFlux — Flux<ServerSentEvent> (non-blocking, Q221):
@RestController
class ReactiveNotificationController {
    private final Sinks.Many<String> sink = Sinks.many().multicast().onBackpressureBuffer();

    @GetMapping(value = "/notifications/stream", produces = MediaType.TEXT_EVENT_STREAM_VALUE)
    Flux<ServerSentEvent<String>> stream() {
        return sink.asFlux().map(msg -> ServerSentEvent.<String>builder().event("notification").data(msg).build());
    }

    void push(String message) { sink.tryEmitNext(message); }
}
```

```javascript
// Client — EventSource (auto-reconnects on drop):
const es = new EventSource('/notifications/stream');
es.addEventListener('notification', e => console.log(e.data));
es.onerror = () => console.log('reconnecting...');
```

SSE is **plain HTTP** — works through proxies/firewalls, needs no special protocol upgrade. The browser's `EventSource` handles reconnection and `Last-Event-ID` automatically.

---

### 3. WebSocket — Full-Duplex Bidirectional Channel

```java
// Raw WebSocket — low-level, no routing:
@Component
class OrderWebSocketHandler extends TextWebSocketHandler {
    private final Set<WebSocketSession> sessions = ConcurrentHashMap.newKeySet();

    @Override public void afterConnectionEstablished(WebSocketSession session) { sessions.add(session); }
    @Override public void afterConnectionClosed(WebSocketSession session, CloseStatus status) { sessions.remove(session); }

    @Override protected void handleTextMessage(WebSocketSession session, TextMessage message) throws IOException {
        // Echo or broadcast:
        for (WebSocketSession s : sessions) s.sendMessage(new TextMessage("Echo: " + message.getPayload()));
    }
}

@Configuration @EnableWebSocket
class WebSocketConfig implements WebSocketConfigurer {
    @Override public void registerWebSocketHandlers(WebSocketHandlerRegistry registry) {
        registry.addHandler(orderHandler(), "/ws/orders").setAllowedOrigins("*");
    }
    @Bean OrderWebSocketHandler orderHandler() { return new OrderWebSocketHandler(); }
}
```

Raw WebSocket gives you **bytes/frames** — you handle routing, serialization, and heartbeats yourself. For anything beyond echo, use **STOMP** (below).

---

### 4. STOMP over WebSocket — Message Routing with a Broker

STOMP (Simple Text Oriented Messaging Protocol) adds **destinations** (`/topic`, `/queue`, `/app`) and a **message broker** on top of WebSocket — so you can `@SendTo` topics and let the broker fan out.

```java
@Configuration @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker
class StompConfig implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {
    @Override public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
        registry.enableSimpleBroker("/topic", "/queue");  // in-memory broker (or RabbitMQ/Redis)
        registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app"); // client sends to /app/...
    }
    @Override public void registerStompEndpoints(StompEndpointRegistry registry) {
        registry.addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOriginPatterns("*").withSockJS(); // SockJS fallback
    }
}

@Controller
class ChatController {
    // Client sends to /app/chat → this method handles it → broadcasts to /topic/messages:
    @MessageMapping("/chat")
    @SendTo("/topic/messages")
    ChatMessage handle(ChatMessage msg, Principal principal) {
        return new ChatMessage(principal.getName(), msg.text(), Instant.now());
    }

    // Server-initiated push (no client trigger):
    @Autowired SimpMessagingTemplate broker;

    void notifyOrderUpdate(Order order) {
        broker.convertAndSend("/topic/orders/" + order.getCustomerId(), order);
    }

    // User-specific queue:
    void notifyUser(String username, String message) {
        broker.convertAndSendToUser(username, "/queue/notifications", message);
    }
}
```

```
Client → STOMP SEND /app/chat {text:"hello"}  → @MessageMapping("/chat") → /topic/messages → all subscribers
Server → broker.convertAndSend("/topic/orders/42", order) → subscribers to /topic/orders/42
Server → convertAndSendToUser("alice", "/queue/notifications", msg) → only alice's /user/queue/notifications
```

| Destination prefix | Meaning |
| :--- | :--- |
| `/app/...` | Routed to `@MessageMapping` controller method |
| `/topic/...` | Broadcast to **all** subscribers |
| `/queue/...` | Point-to-point — one subscriber gets it |
| `/user/queue/...` | User-specific — only that user's session |

**External broker (RabbitMQ/Redis) for scale:**

```java
@Override public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
    registry.enableStompBrokerRelay("/topic", "/queue")
        .setRelayHost("rabbitmq").setRelayPort(61613)
        .setClientLogin("guest").setClientPasscode("guest");
}
```

The simple in-memory broker lives in one JVM — for multi-instance deployments, relay through RabbitMQ (Q224) so all instances share the same topic subscriptions.

---

### 5. Scaling Real-Time Connections

```
Single instance:  10k WebSocket connections on one JVM — thread per connection (MVC) or event loop (WebFlux)
Multi-instance:   Sticky sessions + external broker (RabbitMQ/Redis) so any instance can push to any client
                  or: stateless — client reconnects to any instance, broker relays

Load balancer:    Must support WebSocket upgrade (nginx: proxy_http_version 1.1, Upgrade headers)
                  SSE: plain HTTP — no special LB config needed
```

```nginx
# nginx — WebSocket upgrade:
location /ws {
    proxy_pass http://backend;
    proxy_http_version 1.1;
    proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;
    proxy_set_header Connection "upgrade";
}
```

For **massive scale** (100k+ connections): use a dedicated push service (e.g., Centrifugo, Pusher) or WebFlux with Reactor Netty — one event loop handles thousands of WebSocket sessions without thread-per-connection.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| No heartbeat / ping | Idle WebSocket killed by proxy/LB after 60s | Enable STOMP heartbeats (`registry.enableSimpleBroker(...).setHeartbeatValue(new long[]{10000, 10000})`) |
| In-memory broker with multiple instances | Message sent on instance A never reaches client on instance B | Use external broker relay (RabbitMQ/Redis) |
| `SseEmitter` timeout too short | Stream drops every 30s, client reconnects constantly | Increase timeout or use `SseEmitter(Long.MAX_VALUE)` + manual cleanup |
| No `Last-Event-ID` handling on SSE | Missed events during reconnect gap | Send `id` with each SSE event; server replays from that id on `Last-Event-ID` header |
| Blocking work inside `TextWebSocketHandler` | Event loop / WebSocket thread blocked | Offload to `Schedulers.boundedElastic()` (Q221) or use reactive `WebSocketHandler` |
| No auth on `/ws` endpoint | Anyone can connect | Same Spring Security filter chain (Q207) — validate JWT during WebSocket handshake |

### Summary

- **SSE** (`SseEmitter` / `Flux<ServerSentEvent>`) is one-way server push over plain HTTP with auto-reconnect — ideal for live feeds, notifications, and progress streams.
- **WebSocket** is full-duplex over a single TCP connection — raw `TextWebSocketHandler` for bytes/frames, **STOMP** (`@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker`, `/app`→`@MessageMapping`→`/topic`/`/queue`) for routed messaging with broker fan-out.
- Scale with an **external broker relay** (RabbitMQ, Q224) so multi-instance deployments share subscriptions; configure LB for WebSocket upgrade headers; use heartbeats to keep idle connections alive.
- Choose SSE for one-way push (simpler, HTTP-native), WebSocket/STOMP for bidirectional (chat, collaborative editing); always secure the handshake and handle reconnection.

---

## 229. How do you implement caching in a Spring Boot microservice? Explain Caffeine, Redis, distributed cache patterns, and cache invalidation strategies.

Caching is the single highest-leverage performance optimization — a cache hit avoids a DB query, an external API call, or a computation. Spring Boot provides `CacheManager` abstraction over **Caffeine** (local, in-process) and **Redis** (distributed, shared). Choosing the right strategy — where to cache, when to invalidate, how to handle stampeding — determines whether caching helps or hurts.

---

### 1. Cache Fundamentals — Hit, Miss, and Where to Cache

```
Request arrives
  ├─ Cache HIT  → return cached value (μs, no DB)
  └─ Cache MISS → query DB (ms) → store in cache → return
```

| Cache type | Location | Shared? | Use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Local (Caffeine)** | In-process heap | No (per JVM) | Read-heavy, low-cardinality, per-instance config |
| **Distributed (Redis)** | External server | Yes (all instances) | Shared data, session cache, multi-instance consistency |
| **HTTP response** | Browser/CDN | Client-side | Static assets, API responses (`Cache-Control`) |
| **ORM/Hibernate L1/L2** | EntityManager / second-level | Per-Session / per-JVM | Entity objects (Q208 L2 cache) |

**When to cache:** read-heavy (>10:1 reads:writes), data changes infrequently, stale-tolerant.
**When NOT to cache:** highly volatile data, per-user sensitive data, write-heavy.

---

### 2. Caffeine — Local In-Process Cache

Caffeine is the successor to Guava cache — high-performance, window-tiny-lfu eviction, automatic refresh.

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>com.github.ben-manes.caffeine</groupId>
    <artifactId>caffeine</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```yaml
spring:
  cache:
    type: caffeine
    caffeine:
      spec: maximumSize=500,expireAfterWrite=10m,refreshAfterWrite=1m
```

```java
@Configuration
class CacheConfig {
    @Bean
    CaffeineCacheManager cacheManager() {
        CaffeineCacheManager m = new CaffeineCacheManager("products", "categories", "orders");
        m.setCaffeine(Caffeine.newBuilder()
            .maximumSize(1000)
            .expireAfterWrite(10, TimeUnit.MINUTES)
            .expireAfterAccess(5, TimeUnit.MINUTES)
            .recordStats());        // enable hit/miss stats
        return m;
    }
}

@Service
class ProductService {
    @Cacheable(value = "products", key = "#id")       // cache miss → load, then cache
    public ProductDTO getById(Long id) { return repo.findById(id).map(ProductDTO::from).orElse(null); }

    @Cacheable(value = "products", key = "#name", condition = "#name.length() > 3")
    public ProductDTO getByName(String name) { ... }

    @CacheEvict(value = "products", key = "#product.id")       // remove on update
    public Product update(Product product) { return repo.save(product); }

    @CachePut(value = "products", key = "#product.id")         // update cache AFTER method runs
    public ProductDTO updateAndCache(Product product) { return ProductDTO.from(repo.save(product)); }

    @CacheEvict(allEntries = true)                              // clear all at once
    public void reindex() { }
}
```

| Annotation | What it does |
| :--- | :--- |
| `@Cacheable` | On miss → call method → cache result. Supports `condition`, `unless`, `key` |
| `@CacheEvict` | Remove entry(s) from cache — `allEntries=true` clears the entire cache |
| `@CachePut` | Always call method, update cache with result (useful for updates without eviction race) |
| `@Caching` | Combine multiple evict/put/cacheable annotations on one method |

**Caffeine eviction policy (window-tiny-lfu):** keeps hot items in a "main" window and evicts cold items — far better than LRU/LFU for mixed workloads. `maximumSize` caps memory; `expireAfterWrite` / `expireAfterAccess` bound lifetime.

---

### 3. Redis — Distributed Shared Cache

Redis is an in-memory key-value store shared across all application instances — the standard distributed cache for microservices.

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-data-redis</artifactId>
</dependency>
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-cache</artifactId>
</dependency>
```

```yaml
spring:
  cache:
    type: redis
  redis:
    host: redis
    port: 6379
    timeout: 2000ms
    lettuce:
      pool:
        max-active: 20
        max-idle: 10
        min-idle: 5
    # Optional: use a Redis namespace prefix for multi-app clusters
    # cache:
    #   redis:
    #     cache-names: products,categories,orders
```

```java
@Configuration
@EnableCaching
class RedisCacheConfig {
    @Bean
    RedisCacheManager cacheManager(RedisConnectionFactory factory) {
        RedisCacheConfiguration config = RedisCacheConfiguration.defaultCacheConfig()
            .entryTtl(Duration.ofMinutes(10))       // default TTL per cache
            .disableCachingNullValues()             // don't cache nulls (avoid cache penetration)
            .serializeValuesWith(RedisSerializationContext.SerializationPair
                .from(new GenericJackson2JsonRedisSerializer())); // JSON (not Java serialized)

        return RedisCacheManager.builder(factory)
            .cacheDefaults(config)
            .withInitialCacheConfigurations(Map.of(
                "products", config.entryTtl(Duration.ofMinutes(30)),
                "categories", config.entryTtl(Duration.ofHours(1))
            ))
            .build();
    }
}
```

**Why `GenericJackson2JsonRedisSerializer` over Java serialization:**
- Java serialization leaks class names + is fragile across version changes
- JSON is language-agnostic, portable, human-readable, version-tolerant

**Redis data structures for caching:**

| Structure | Use |
| :--- | :--- |
| `String` (JSON) | Simple key-value cache |
| `Hash` | Object fields — partial updates |
| `Sorted Set` | Leaderboards, time-series |
| `Set` | Dedup, intersection |
| `List` | Simple queue |

---

### 4. Cache-Aside (Lazy Loading) vs Read-Through vs Write-Through

```
Cache-Aside (Spring's default — @Cacheable):
  1. Try cache → MISS
  2. Load from DB
  3. Write to cache
  4. Return
  Problem: first request after deploy misses → DB hit

Read-Through (Spring Cache + CacheManager):
  1. Try cache → MISS → CacheManager loads (e.g., Caffeine loads via CacheLoader)
  2. Cache is populated automatically
  Cleaner, but less flexible

Write-Through:
  Write to cache AND DB synchronously (in the same TX)
  Always consistent, but slower writes

Write-Behind (async):
  Write to cache, async flush to DB
  Fastest, but risk of data loss if cache crashes
```

```java
// Read-through with a CacheLoader:
@Bean
CacheLoader<String, ProductDTO> productLoader() {
    return id -> repo.findById(Long.valueOf(id)).map(ProductDTO::from).orElse(null);
}
// @Cacheable(value="products", key="#id") then Caffeine auto-loads via CacheLoader
```

| Strategy | Consistency | Write latency | Complexity |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Cache-Aside** | Eventual | Fast | Low (Spring default) |
| **Read-Through** | Eventual | Fast | Low (CacheLoader) |
| **Write-Through** | Strong | Slower (2 writes) | Medium |
| **Write-Behind** | Weak (async) | Fastest | High (recovery logic) |

---

### 5. Cache Invalidation — The Hard Part

Invalidation is the #1 cache challenge — stale data served after the underlying data changes.

```java
@CacheEvict(value = "product", key = "#product.id")
public Product updateProduct(Product product) { ... }   // evict on update

@CacheEvict(value = "product", key = "#id")
public void deleteProduct(Long id) { ... }               // evict on delete

// When a product's category changes, invalidate BOTH product AND category caches:
@Caching(evict = {
    @CacheEvict(value = "product", key = "#product.id"),
    @CacheEvict(value = "category", key = "#product.categoryId")
})
public Product moveToCategory(Product product, Long newCategoryId) { ... }
```

```
Stale data causes:
  User updates profile → cache still shows old name
  Fix: @CacheEvict on update/delete (immediate)
       or shorter TTL (eventual consistency)
```

**Cache stampede (thundering herd):** when a hot key expires, 1000 concurrent requests all miss → all hit DB simultaneously → DB overload.

```java
// Solution — lock-based single-flight:
@Cacheable(value = "products", key = "#id", unless = "#result == null")
public ProductDTO getById(Long id) { ... }

// Caffeine built-in refresh (prevents stampedes):
//   refreshAfterWrite=1m → background thread refreshes on next read; other reads get stale until refresh done
// Redis: use SETNX / Redisson lock per key during refresh

// Alternative — never cache nulls (prevents cache penetration):
//   .disableCachingNullValues()  (Redis CacheManager)
```

**Cache penetration** (request for non-existent data):
- Cache nulls with short TTL (`nullValue()` in Caffeine, `.disableCachingNullValues()` + explicit null entry in Redis)
- Bloom filter to reject impossible keys before DB

**Cache warming:** pre-populate cache at startup (or on deploy) — `@EventListener(ContextRefreshedEvent)` loads hot data.

---

### 6. Distributed Cache Challenges — Consistency Across Instances

```
Instance A updates Product #5 → evicts "product:5" from its LOCAL cache (Caffeine)
Instance B still has stale "product:5" in its Caffeine cache → returns old data

Solutions:
  1. Use Redis (shared) → eviction is visible to all instances immediately
  2. Use Redis + Caffeine → Redis is source of truth; Caffeine is local L1 (with TTL)
  3. Pub/sub invalidation → A publishes "product:5:invalidated", B subscribes and evicts locally
```

```java
// Spring Cache + Redis pub/sub invalidation:
@Component
class CacheInvalidationListener {
    @Autowired RedisTemplate<String, String> redis;

    @EventListener
    void on(ApplicationEvent event) {
        // Redis pub/sub channel: "cache-invalidation:product:5"
        // Each instance subscribes and evicts local Caffeine
    }
}
```

**Best practice for multi-instance:**
- **Hot read-heavy data** → Redis (shared, consistent)
- **Per-instance config** → Caffeine (local, no network hop)
- **Layered:** Redis (L2) + Caffeine (L1) — Caffeine holds recent items for μs access; Redis holds everything for consistency

---

### 7. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Caching mutable data with long TTL | Users see stale data after update | `@CacheEvict` on update/delete; or short TTL |
| Caching `null` results | Repeated misses → DB stampede | `.disableCachingNullValues()`; cache nulls with 1-5s TTL |
| `@CacheEvict` before method (default) | Cache cleared even if method throws | Use `@CacheEvict(beforeInvocation = false)` to clear only on success |
| No cache stats enabled | Can't identify misses/hit rates | `.recordStats()` in Caffeine config; monitor via Actuator |
| Serialization mismatch | `ClassNotFoundException` / invalid JSON | Use `GenericJackson2JsonRedisSerializer`; keep DTOs immutable |
| Cache on per-user data (no key differentiation) | User A sees user B's data | Always include user id in key: `key = "#userId + ':' + #productId"` |
| Caching too aggressively | Cache pressure → evictions → thrashing | Measure hit rate; cache only hot data (top 20% of items) |

### Summary

- Spring Boot `CacheManager` abstracts **Caffeine** (local, fast, per-instance) and **Redis** (distributed, shared) — use `@Cacheable` / `@CacheEvict` / `@CachePut` to add caching with zero boilerplate.
- **Cache-Aside** (lazy load) is the default and most common — `@Cacheable` loads on miss; **evict** on update/delete (`@CacheEvict`) to prevent stale data.
- Avoid **cache stampede** (thundering herd on expiry) with Caffeine `refreshAfterWrite` or Redis locks; prevent **cache penetration** by not caching nulls or using a Bloom filter.
- For multi-instance consistency, use **Redis** as the shared cache (or Redis + Caffeine as layered L1/L2); serialize with JSON (`GenericJackson2JsonRedisSerializer`); always include user id in keys for per-user data.

---

## 230. How do you define and monitor SLIs, SLOs, and alerts in production? Explain golden signals, SLIs, SLOs, and error budgets.

Observability without **Service Level Objectives (SLOs)** is just data collection — you measure everything and know nothing matters. SLOs define **what "good" means** for a service (e.g., p99 latency < 500ms, availability 99.9%), and **error budgets** quantify how much failure you can tolerate before an incident. Golden signals (latency, traffic, errors, saturation) are the four metrics every service must expose.

---

### 1. The Four Golden Signals

```
Golden Signals (Google SRE):
  1. LATENCY    — How long does the request take?  (p50/p95/p99)
  2. TRAFFIC    — How much demand is on the system? (requests/s, bytes/s)
  3. ERRORS     — What fraction of requests fail?    (5xx rate, 4xx rate)
  4. SATURATION — How full is the system?             (CPU, memory, queue depth)
```

```
Service Health Dashboard (Grafana):
  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
  │  Latency    │  │  Traffic    │  │  Errors     │  │ Saturation  │
  │ p50/p95/p99 │  │ req/s       │  │ 5xx/4xx     │  │ CPU/MEM/Queue│
  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘
```

| Signal | Prometheus metric | Why it matters |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Latency** | `histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m]))` | Users feel p99, not average |
| **Traffic** | `rate(http_server_requests_total[5m])` | Capacity planning, detect traffic spikes |
| **Errors** | `rate(http_server_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])` | User-facing failures |
| **Saturation** | `process_cpu_seconds_total`, `jvm_memory_used_bytes` | Resource exhaustion → upcoming failure |

---

### 2. SLIs, SLOs, and Error Budgets

```
SLI (Service Level Indicator):  a measurable metric
SLO (Service Level Objective):  a target for the SLI
     (e.g., p99 latency < 500ms, availability ≥ 99.9%)
Error Budget:                   how much failure is tolerated
     (e.g., 0.1% of requests can fail per month)
```

```yaml
# SLO definitions:
services:
  order-service:
    slis:
      availability: 99.95%          # "up" per /actuator/health over 30 days
      latency_p99: 500ms            # p99 < 500ms for /api/v1/orders
      error_rate: 0.5%              # 5xx < 0.5% of all requests
    error_budget:
      monthly: 0.05%                # 0.05% of requests can fail (43 min/month)
      burn_rate:  # how fast is the budget burning
        hourly:  0.25%              # 3.6h of budget per hour
        daily:   2%                 # 2% of daily budget consumed per day
```

| SLO type | Metric | Typical target |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Availability** | `up` (200 from /health) | 99.9% (43 min/month down) / 99.95% / 99.99% |
| **Latency** | p50/p95/p99 of requests | p99 < 200ms (internal), < 1s (external) |
| **Error rate** | `rate(...{status=~"5.."}[5m]) / rate(...[5m])` | < 0.1%–1% depending on criticality |
| **Throughput** | requests/s | Sustained within capacity |

```
Error budget burn rate alerts:
  • Burn rate > 1/24 (daily)  → Warning — consume budget faster than allowed
  • Burn rate > 1/4  (hourly) → Critical — severe incident, page the on-call
  (Google's "burn rate" model — alerts fire faster when budget burns quickly)
```

---

### 3. Prometheus Recording Rules — Pre-compute SLIs

```yaml
# prometheus/rules.yml — expensive queries computed and stored as new metrics:
groups:
  - name: slo_rules
    rules:
      - record: job:request_latency_p99:5m
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m])) by (job, le))

      - record: job:availability:30d
        expr: |
          1 - (
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[30d]))
            /
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_total[30d]))
          )

      - record: job:error_rate:5m
        expr: |
          rate(http_server_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])
          /
          rate(http_server_requests_total[5m])
```

Recording rules pre-aggregate expensive histogram queries into new time-series — Grafana dashboards read the cheap pre-computed metric instead of scanning raw histograms at render time.

---

### 4. Alerting Rules — Burn Rate Model

```yaml
# prometheus/alerts.yml
groups:
  - name: slo_alerts
    rules:
      # Availability SLO: 99.9% over 30 days
      # Critical: error budget burning fast (1/4 hour rate over 1h)
      - alert: OrderServiceAvailabilityCritical
        expr: |
          (
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_total{job="order-service",status=~"5.."}[1h]))
            /
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_total{job="order-service"}[1h]))
          )
          > 0.0025   # > 0.25% error rate → 1/4 of hourly budget burned
        for: 10m
        labels:
          severity: critical
        annotations:
          summary: "OrderService availability burning error budget"
          description: "5xx error rate > 0.25% for 10m — paging on-call"

      # Warning: burn rate over 24h window
      - alert: OrderServiceAvailabilityWarning
        expr: |
          (
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_total{job="order-service",status=~"5.."}[6h]))
            /
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_total{job="order-service"}[6h]))
          )
          > 0.0002   # > 0.02% over 6h → slower burn
        for: 30m
        labels:
          severity: warning

      # Latency SLO: p99 < 500ms
      - alert: OrderServiceLatencyHigh
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket{job="order-service"}[5m]))
            by (le)) > 0.5
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: warning
```

| Severity | Burn rate condition | Response |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Critical** | 1/4 hour rate over 1h | Page on-call (severe incident) |
| **Warning** | 1/24 daily rate over 6h | Slack/email (trend, not yet urgent) |

**Multi-window multi-burn-rate (Google model):** alert fires when budget is burning too fast *over multiple time windows* — avoids false positives from brief spikes.

---

### 5. Monitoring Stack — Prometheus + Grafana + Alertmanager

```
Spring Boot app
  │ /actuator/metrics (Micrometer) ──► Prometheus (scrape every 15s)
  │
  │ ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────┐
  │ │Prometheus│───►│ Alertmanager │───►│  PagerDuty │
  │ │  (DB)    │    │ (routes by   │    │ / Slack    │
  │ │          │    │  severity,   │    │            │
  │ │          │    │  receiver)   │    │            │
  │ └──────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────┘
  │
  │ Grafana ←── Prometheus (dashboards)
  │
  │ Loki ←── JSON logs (ELK/Loki, Q214)
  │ Jaeger ←── Traces (OTel, Q214)
```

**Alertmanager configuration (dedup, grouping, silencing):**

```yaml
# alertmanager.yml
route:
  receiver: 'pagerduty-critical'
  group_by: [alertname, job, severity]     # group identical alerts
  group_wait: 30s                          # wait for more matching alerts
  group_interval: 5m                       # send update if more arrive within 5m
  repeat_interval: 4h                      # don't re-alert on same issue
  routes:
    - match: { severity: "critical" }
      receiver: 'pagerduty-critical'
    - match: { severity: "warning" }
      receiver: 'slack-warnings'

receivers:
  - name: 'pagerduty-critical'
    pagerduty_configs:
      - service_key: ${PAGERDUTY_KEY}
        severity: critical
  - name: 'slack-warnings'
    slack_configs:
      - api_url: ${SLACK_WEBHOOK}
        channel: '#alerts'
```

---

### 6. Burn Rate Calculation — Worked Example

```
Order Service SLO: 99.9% availability over 30 days
Error budget = 0.1% of requests = 0.1% × 10M requests/month = 10,000 errors/month

Burn rate = (actual error rate) / (SLO error rate per window)

Example: 0.5% error rate over 1h
  SLO error rate per 1h = 0.1% / 720 hours = 0.000139% per hour
  Burn rate (1h) = 0.5% / 0.000139% ≈ 3600× → CRITICAL

  SLO error rate per 24h = 0.1% / 30 days = 0.000333% per day
  Burn rate (24h) = 0.5% / 0.000333% ≈ 1500× → CRITICAL

Result: page on-call immediately.
```

```
Formula (Google SRE):
  burn_rate(hours) = actual_error_rate / (SLO_error_rate / hours_window)
  Alert if:
    burn_rate(1h)  > 1/2   OR
    burn_rate(6h)  > 1/4   OR
    burn_rate(24h) > 1/8
```

---

### 7. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| No SLOs, just metrics | Too many alerts, alert fatigue | Define 1–2 SLIs per service, alert on SLO breach only |
| Alerting on averages | p50 looks fine, p99 is terrible | Alert on p95/p99, not average |
| No error budget tracking | Can't tell if you're approaching SLO breach | Track `availability` recording rule; set burn-rate alerts |
| Alert on every 5xx | Noise, pager fatigue | Use burn-rate model (sustained failure), not single 5xx |
| Single-window alert | False positives from brief spikes | Multi-window burn rate (1h + 6h + 24h) |
| No runbook | On-call doesn't know what to do | Link alert → runbook; define severity → response |
| Alert on metrics not visible to users | False "up" when user-facing feature is broken | Monitor **user-facing** SLIs (e.g., "checkout succeeds"), not just `/health` |

### Summary

- **Golden signals** — latency (p99/p95), traffic (req/s), errors (5xx%), saturation (CPU/mem/queue) — are the four metrics every service must expose; **SLIs** are the measurable values, **SLOs** are targets, **error budgets** are tolerable failures.
- **Burn-rate alerts** (Google SRE model) fire when the budget burns faster than allowed over multiple windows (1h/6h/24h), avoiding false positives from brief spikes — Alertmanager handles dedup, grouping, and routing to PagerDuty/Slack.
- **Recording rules** pre-compute expensive histogram quantiles and rates into cheap new metrics for dashboards. Always alert on user-facing SLIs, not just `/health`.
- Use **Prometheus + Grafana + Alertmanager + Loki + Jaeger** for the full observability stack.

---

## 231. How do you secure Spring Boot microservices end-to-end? Explain OAuth2/JWT, mTLS, API gateway security, and defense-in-depth.

Securing a single service with Spring Security (Q207) is straightforward — securing a **mesh of microservices** requires defense-in-depth: authenticating every call, authorizing every request, encrypting every hop, and isolating blast radius. The architecture combines **OAuth2/JWT** for identity, **mTLS** for service-to-service encryption, and **API gateway** as the unified security enforcement point.

---

### 1. Threat Model — The Attack Surface in Microservices

```
Internet → API Gateway (public) → Internal services (private network)

Attack surfaces:
  1. External: unauthenticated clients, token replay, DDoS
  2. Inter-service: spoofed calls, man-in-the-middle, lateral movement
  3. Internal: compromised pod, container escape, data exfiltration
```

| Layer | Threat | Defense |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **External** | Anonymous access, stolen token | OAuth2/JWT, rate limiting (Q216), WAF |
| **Inter-service** | Spoofed service, plaintext traffic | mTLS, service mesh, short-lived tokens |
| **Internal** | Compromised pod, privilege escalation | Network policies, least-privilege RBAC, pod security |
| **Data** | DB breach, injection | Parameterized queries (Q208), encryption at rest |

---

### 2. OAuth2/JWT — Token-Based Authentication

```
Login flow:
  Client ──POST /login (username/password)──► Auth Server (Keycloak/Auth0)
  Auth Server ──access_token (JWT) + refresh_token──► Client
  Client ──GET /api/orders (Authorization: Bearer <JWT>)──► API Gateway
  Gateway validates JWT ──► Forward to service
```

```java
// API Gateway or each service validates the JWT:
@Configuration
@EnableResourceServer
class SecurityConfig extends WebSecurityConfigurerAdapter {
    @Override protected void configure(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
        http
            .authorizeRequests()
                .antMatchers("/api/public/**").permitAll()
                .antMatchers("/api/admin/**").hasRole("ADMIN")
                .anyRequest().authenticated()
            .and()
            .oauth2ResourceServer()
                .jwt()
                .decoder(jwtDecoder());          // validates signature, expiry, issuer
    }

    JwtDecoder jwtDecoder() {
        return NimbusJwtDecoder.withJwkSetUri("https://auth-server/.well-known/jwks.json").build();
    }
}
```

```java
// Extract roles/permissions from JWT claims:
@Bean JwtAuthenticationConverter jwtAuthenticationConverter() {
    JwtAuthenticationConverter conv = new JwtAuthenticationConverter();
    conv.setJwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter(jwt -> {
        List<String> roles = jwt.getClaimAsStringList("roles");  // ["ROLE_ADMIN","ROLE_USER"]
        return roles.stream().map(r -> new SimpleGrantedAuthority(r)).toList();
    });
    return conv;
}
```

| JWT claim | Use |
| :--- | :--- |
| `sub` / `preferred_username` | Identity (who) |
| `roles` / `scope` | Authorization (what) |
| `iss` / `aud` | Token issuer + intended audience (validate!) |
| `exp` | Expiry — short-lived access token (5–15 min), refresh token |
| `jti` | Unique ID — revoke/blocklist tracking |

**Short-lived access token + refresh token** limits damage if a token is stolen; the refresh token is stored securely (HTTP-only cookie).

---

### 3. mTLS — Mutual TLS for Service-to-Service

mTLS requires **both** client and server to present certificates — every service verifies every other service's identity.

```
Without mTLS:
  Service A ──► Service B    (plain HTTP — anyone can impersonate A)

With mTLS:
  Service A ──cert──► Service B (A presents cert; B validates it)
  Service B ──cert──► Service A (B presents cert; A validates it)
  Neither can impersonate the other
```

```yaml
# Each service's application.yml:
server:
  ssl:
    enabled: true
    key-store: classpath:service.p12
    key-store-password: ${KEYSTORE_PASSWORD}
    key-store-type: PKCS12
    client-auth: need              # require client certificate (mTLS)
    trust-store: classpath:ca.p12  # CA that signed all service certs
    trust-store-password: ${TRUSTSTORE_PASSWORD}
```

```
mTLS with service mesh (Istio/Linkerd):
  Sidecar proxy handles TLS automatically:
    ┌────────────┐     ┌────────────┐
    │ Service A  │────►│ Service B  │
    │ (sidecar)  │ mTLS│ (sidecar)  │
    └────────────┘     └────────────┘
  No code changes needed — mesh injects sidecars, manages certs (SPIFFE/SPIRE)
```

| Approach | Pros | Cons |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **App-level mTLS** (Spring SSL) | No mesh dependency, full control | Code changes, cert rotation logic |
| **Service mesh mTLS** (Istio/Linkerd) | Zero-code, automatic cert rotation | Infrastructure complexity |

---

### 4. API Gateway — Centralized Security Enforcement

The gateway is the **single entry point** — authenticate, authorize, rate-limit, and log every request before it reaches internal services.

```
Internet → API Gateway (Spring Cloud Gateway / Kong)
                ├── JWT validation (OAuth2/JWT, Q207)
                ├── Rate limiting (token bucket per client)
                ├── IP whitelist / WAF rules
                ├── Request logging (audit trail)
                ├── Path-based authZ (admin routes → ADMIN role)
                └── Forward to internal services (private network only)
```

```yaml
# Spring Cloud Gateway security filter chain:
spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      routes:
        - id: admin-route
          uri: lb://admin-service
          predicates:
            - Path=/api/admin/**
          filters:
            - TokenRelay          # forwards OAuth2 token to downstream
            - name: RequestRateLimiter
              args: { redis-rate-limiter.replenishRate: 100, burstCapacity: 200 }
          metadata:
            roles: [ADMIN]
        - id: public-route
          uri: lb://public-service
          predicates:
            - Path=/api/public/**
          filters:
            - TokenRelay          # optional — some public routes still have user context
```

**Zero-trust at the gateway:**
- Every request authenticated — no internal routes are exempt
- Strip `Authorization` header before forwarding to services that don't need it
- Log all requests for audit (Q214)

---

### 5. Defense-in-Depth — Layered Security Model

```
Layer 1 — Edge (API Gateway)
  • JWT validation, rate limiting, WAF, IP whitelist

Layer 2 — Service-to-Service (mTLS + tokens)
  • mTLS for encryption; short-lived OAuth2 token per call
  • Circuit breaker (Q213) — prevent cascading failures from compromised services

Layer 3 — Application (Spring Security)
  • Method-level security: @PreAuthorize, @Secured, @RolesAllowed
  • Input validation (@Valid, sanitize) — prevent injection
  • Output encoding — prevent XSS

Layer 4 — Data
  • Parameterized queries — prevent SQL injection (Q208)
  • Encryption at rest (AES-256) — protect data if DB is breached
  • Vault-secrets (Q218) — no hardcoded passwords

Layer 5 — Infrastructure
  • Network policies — only allow traffic between specific services
  • Pod security — read-only filesystem, no privileged containers
  • RBAC — least privilege for Kubernetes service accounts
```

```java
// Application-level authorization (Q207 — method security):
@RestController
@PreAuthorize("hasRole('USER')")        // class-level default
class OrderController {
    @PreAuthorize("hasRole('ADMIN') or #userId == authentication.principal.id")
    @GetMapping("/orders/{userId}")      // per-resource authorization
    Order getMyOrder(@PathVariable Long userId) { ... }

    @PreAuthorize("hasRole('ADMIN')")
    @DeleteMapping("/orders/{id}")       // admin-only
    void deleteOrder(@PathVariable Long id) { ... }
}
```

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| JWT not validated properly | Accept any token (no signature check) | Validate `iss`, `aud`, `exp`, signature via `NimbusJwtDecoder` |
| Long-lived access token | Stolen token gives long access window | Short-lived access (5–15 min) + refresh token (HTTP-only cookie) |
| Plain HTTP between services | Traffic sniffable, MITM attacks | mTLS (app-level or service mesh) |
| No rate limiting at gateway | DDoS, brute-force | Rate limiting at gateway (Q216) |
| No audit logging | Can't trace breaches | Log all requests + decisions (Q214) |
| Over-permissive network policies | Compromised pod reaches all services | Kubernetes NetworkPolicy — deny-all + allow specific pairs |
| Hardcoded secrets | Credentials in Git | Vault (Q218) — never commit secrets |

### Summary

- **OAuth2/JWT** (Keycloak/Auth0) authenticates users via short-lived access tokens + refresh tokens; validate `iss`, `aud`, `exp`, signature at the gateway — never trust a JWT without full validation.
- **mTLS** encrypts and authenticates inter-service traffic — either app-level (Spring SSL) or via service mesh (Istio/Linkerd); prevents spoofing and MITM.
- **API Gateway** is the single security enforcement point — JWT validation, rate limiting, WAF, audit logging — internal services live on a private network.
- **Defense-in-depth** (5 layers): edge → service-to-service → application (method security + input validation) → data (parameterized queries, Vault secrets, encryption at rest) → infrastructure (network policies, pod security, RBAC).

---

## 232. How does Database Connection Pooling (HikariCP) work in Spring Boot? Explain core configurations, connection sizing, and leak detection.

Opening physical database connections involves TCP handshakes, TLS negotiation, authentication, and memory allocation—which is expensive for high-throughput applications. **HikariCP** is the default connection pool in Spring Boot (since 2.0). It pre-allocates and manages a pool of reusable connections using zero-overhead byte-code optimizations (`FastList`, custom lock-free data structures).

---

### 1. Key Configuration Parameters

In Spring Boot, configure Hikari under `spring.datasource.hikari.*`:

| Property | Default | Description & Best Practice |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `maximum-pool-size` | `10` | Maximum connections in the pool (idle + in-use). Keep small; oversized pools degrade DB performance. |
| `minimum-idle` | Same as max | Minimum idle connections. Hikari recommends **not** setting this differently to maintain a fixed-size pool for peak stability. |
| `connection-timeout` | `30000` (30s) | Max milliseconds a thread waits for a connection before throwing `SQLTransientConnectionException`. |
| `idle-timeout` | `600000` (10m) | Max time a connection can sit idle before retirement (only applies if `minimum-idle < maximum-pool-size`). |
| `max-lifetime` | `1800000` (30m) | Max lifespan of a connection. Must be 2–3 minutes shorter than the DB's wait/connection timeout to avoid abrupt termination. |
| `leak-detection-threshold` | `0` (disabled) | Milliseconds a connection can be out of the pool before Hikari logs a leak warning with a stack trace. |

```yaml
spring:
  datasource:
    hikari:
      maximum-pool-size: 15
      connection-timeout: 20000      # 20 seconds wait time
      max-lifetime: 1200000          # 20 minutes lifespan
      leak-detection-threshold: 3000 # Alert if a connection is held > 3 seconds
```

---

### 2. Pool Sizing: The Common Myth

> **Myth:** "More threads and higher concurrent load require more database connections (e.g. 100+)."

In reality, databases are bound by disk I/O and CPU cores. When too many connections execute queries concurrently, the database spends more time on **CPU context switching and disk contention** than actual query execution.

The PostgreSQL / Hikari pool sizing guideline:
$$\text{pool size} = (\text{CPU cores} \times 2) + \text{effective spindle count}$$

For an 8-core database server with SSDs, a pool of **15–20 connections** can easily handle thousands of requests per second when transactions are kept short.

---

### 3. Connection Leak Detection

A **connection leak** occurs when an application borrows a connection but fails to return it to the pool (e.g., forgetting `connection.close()` in raw JDBC, or executing slow external HTTP calls inside a `@Transactional` block).

When `leak-detection-threshold` is set (e.g., `3000` ms):
```
WARN [HikariPool-1 housekeeper] com.zaxxer.hikari.pool.ProxyLeakTask: 
Connection leak detection triggered for Connection leak-task-1 on thread http-nio-8080-exec-4, 
stack trace follows:
java.lang.Exception: Apparent connection leak detected
    at com.example.service.OrderService.processOrder(OrderService.java:42)
```
- It prints the exact stack trace of the method that checked out the connection.
- The connection is **not** terminated forcefully; it merely provides an actionable diagnostic alert.

---

### Summary

- **HikariCP** maintains reusable DB connections to avoid costly per-request handshakes.
- Prefer a **fixed-size pool** (keep `minimum-idle` identical to `maximum-pool-size`).
- Size pools based on database CPU cores and I/O capacity, not application thread count.
- Keep `max-lifetime` shorter than the database's own connection timeout.
- Set `leak-detection-threshold: 2000-5000` to quickly catch unclosed connections or bloated transactions.

---

## 233. How does Service Discovery and Client-Side Load Balancing work in Spring Cloud? Explain Eureka, Spring Cloud LoadBalancer, and service registration.

In a microservices fleet, instances are **dynamic** — they scale up/down, restart, and move IPs. Hardcoding URLs breaks instantly. **Service discovery** is the registry that answers "where is order-service right now?", and **client-side load balancing** makes each caller pick a healthy instance. Spring Cloud Eureka (registry) + Spring Cloud LoadBalancer (client-side LB) are the classic pairing, now superseded by Kubernetes-native discovery in many deployments.

---
