### 1. The Two Discovery Models

```
Server-side discovery (Kubernetes / NGINX / AWS ALB):
  Client → LB (known URL) → picks a backend instance
  Client doesn't know the instances — LB does the registry lookups + balancing

Client-side discovery (Eureka / Consul + Spring Cloud LoadBalancer):
  Client → asks Eureka for "order-service" → gets [10.0.0.1:8080, 10.0.0.2:8080]
  Client picks one (round-robin/random/least-connections) → calls it directly
  Failover: marks unhealthy instance, retries the next
```

```
Eureka Registry
   ├─ registers/ heartbeat ─┐          ┌─ heartbeats every 30s ┐
   │                        ▼          ▼                       │
  Order-Service-1 ──┐   Order-Service-2 ──┐                 Cart-Service ─┐
                    │                      │                               │
   ┌────────────────▼──────────────────────▼───────────────────────────────▼───────────┐
   │ Payment-Service asks "order-service instances?" → round-robin across available      │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── ┘
```

| Model | Who picks the instance | Used by |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Server-side | LB (Kubernetes Service, ALB, NGINX) | K8s workloads, cloud-native |
| Client-side | The caller (Spring Cloud LoadBalancer) | Spring Cloud apps, Netflix-era |

---

### 2. Eureka — The Service Registry

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-netflix-eureka-server</artifactId>
</dependency>
<!-- Client: spring-cloud-starter-netflix-eureka-client -->
```

```java
// Registry server:
@SpringBootApplication
@EnableEurekaServer
class EurekaServer { public static void main(String[] a) { SpringApplication.run(EurekaServer.class, a); } }
```

```yaml
# Registry — application.yml:
server:
  port: 8761
eureka:
  instance:
    hostname: eureka
  client:
    register-with-eureka: false   # registry doesn't register itself
    fetch-registry: false
```

```yaml
# Client — application.yml (every service):
spring:
  application:
    name: order-service
eureka:
  client:
    service-url: { defaultZone: http://eureka:8761/eureka/ }
    healthcheck:
      enabled: true                # use /actuator/health instead of mere liveness
  instance:
    lease-renewal-interval-in-seconds: 30   # heartbeat every 30s
    lease-expiration-duration-in-seconds: 90 # evict if no heartbeat for 90s
    prefer-ip-address: true
```

```java
// Client — discover instances programmatically:
@Service
class DiscoveryClientExample {
    @Autowired DiscoveryClient discoveryClient;

    List<ServiceInstance> instances(String serviceId) {
        return discoveryClient.getInstances(serviceId);   // [10.0.0.1:8080, 10.0.0.2:8080]
    }
}
```

| Eureka concept | Meaning |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Lease / heartbeat** | Instance renews every 30s — expired lease (90s+) is evicted |
| **Self-preservation** | If >85% heartbeats fail, Eureka stops evicting (protects against network partition, not instance death) |
| **`healthcheck.enabled`** | Reflect real `/actuator/health` — DB down → unregister, not just process alive |
| **`prefer-ip-address`** | Register IP not hostname — essential in containers |

---

### 3. Spring Cloud LoadBalancer — Client-Side Balancing

```java
@Configuration
class LoadBalancerConfig {
    @Bean
    ServiceInstanceListSupplier defaultServiceInstanceListSupplier(
            ConfigurableApplicationContext ctx) {
        // round-robin across healthy instances, using health checks:
        return ServiceInstanceListSupplier.builder()
            .withBlockingHealthChecks()
            .withCaching()          // cache instance list (default 35s)
            .build(ctx);
    }
}

// RestTemplate with @LoadBalanced — auto-picks an instance per request:
@Bean
@LoadBalanced
RestTemplate restTemplate() { return new RestTemplate(); }

// Usage: URL uses the SERVICE NAME, not a host:
String resp = restTemplate.getForObject(
    "http://order-service/api/v1/orders/42", String.class);
```

```java
// WebClient (reactive) with LoadBalancer + Resilience4j — the modern stack:
@Bean
WebClient webClient(WebClient.Builder builder) {  // auto-load-balanced via spring-cloud-starter-loadbalancer
    return builder.build();
}

Mono<OrderDTO> getOrder(Long id) {
    return webClient.get()
        .uri("http://order-service/api/v1/orders/{id}", id)
        .retrieve()
        .bodyToMono(OrderDTO.class)
        .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(3))
        .retryWhen(Retry.backoff(2, Duration.ofMillis(200)));   // retry next instance
}
```

**How `@LoadBalanced` works:** a `LoadBalancerInterceptor` wraps the `RestTemplate`, resolves `http://order-service/...` via `LoadBalancerClient` → picks an instance from the registry → replaces the host with the actual IP → performs the call. If one instance fails, downstream retry (Q213) redials on the next instance.

---

### 4. Kubernetes-Native Discovery vs Eureka

```
Kubernetes replaces Eureka for most modern stacks:
  Service "order-service" (a virtual IP) → kube-proxy load-balances to pods
  DNS: order-service.namespace.svc.cluster.local → cluster IP
  No Eureka registry needed — K8s API server is the registry
```

```java
// Spring Cloud Kubernetes discovery — same @LoadBalanced code, K8s under the hood:
spring:
  cloud:
    kubernetes:
      discovery:
        all-namespaces: false
        discovery-server-url: ${KUBERNETES_MASTER:https://kubernetes.default.svc}

// service URL still works: http://order-service/api/... (resolved via K8s DNS/service)
```

| | Eureka + LoadBalancer | Kubernetes Service |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Registry | Eureka server | K8s API server |
| Balancing | Client-side (round-robin) | kube-proxy server-side |
| Health | `/actuator/health` + leases | Readiness probe (Q215) |
| Zero-downtime deployments | Manual | Rollout (Q226) |
| When to use | On-prem legacy Spring Cloud | Any K8s deployment |

---

### 5. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `healthcheck` disabled | Registry shows dead instances → calls fail | `eureka.client.healthcheck.enabled: true` |
| Heartbeat too long | Instance eviction delayed 90s+ → traffic to dead instance | `lease-renewal-interval-in-seconds: 30`, `lease-expiration-duration-in-seconds: 90` |
| No caching on LoadBalancer | Every call hits the registry — latency, registry load | `.withCaching()` on `ServiceInstanceListSupplier` |
| Hardcoded URLs instead of service names | Dead IP → broken calls after scale | Always `http://{service-name}/...` via `@LoadBalanced` |
| Self-preservation evicting real nodes | Instances crash but registry keeps them | Understand self-preservation; enable `healthcheck`; monitor registry size |

### Summary

- **Service discovery** answers "where is service X?" — **Eureka** (registry + heartbeats + eviction + self-preservation) is the classic Spring Cloud answer, now largely replaced by **Kubernetes** service discovery in modern stacks.
- **Client-side load balancing** via `@LoadBalanced RestTemplate` / `WebClient` + **Spring Cloud LoadBalancer** resolves `http://order-service/...` to a healthy instance (round-robin) each request — combine with retries (Q213) for instance failover.
- Health checks (`/actuator/health`) drive registry freshness; leases (30s renew / 90s evict) bound stale entries; in Kubernetes, Service + probe replaces the whole Eureka tier.

---

## 234. What is Event-Driven Architecture and when should you use it? Explain domain events, event sourcing, CQRS, and eventual consistency.

Event-Driven Architecture (EDA) treats **facts that happened** (OrderPlaced, PaymentFailed, InventoryReserved) as first-class citizens — services react to events instead of calling each other synchronously. This decouples services, enables asynchronous scale, and matches real business semantics. **Event sourcing** and **CQRS** are the two advanced patterns built on EDA: store the past as immutable events, and separate the write model from optimized read models.

---

### 1. Synchronous Call Chains vs Event-Driven

```
Synchronous (HTTP, Q206):
  Order -> Payment -> Inventory -> Ship (N round-trips)
  Slowest service sets the pace — all must be up, all couple together

Event-driven (Kafka/RabbitMQ, Q212/Q224):
  Order service: persist + publish "OrderPlaced" → returns immediately
  Payment/Inventory/Ship services subscribe and react independently
  No caller blocks; services evolve independently
```

| | Synchronous | Event-driven |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Coupling | Tight (direct calls) | Loose (events only) |
| Latency perceived | Sum of all calls | Just the first write |
| Availability | All services must be up | Each can degrade independently |
| Scaling | Scale whole chain together | Scale per consumer |
| Consistency | Strong (by join) | Eventual (Q223) |
| Debugging | Single request trace | Follow `correlationId` across events |
| Use when | Simple flows, few hops | Complex flows, many consumers, scale |

**Rule of thumb:** HTTP for commands/operations that must complete inline; events for **facts** that multiple services care about. A hybrid is normal — e.g., `OrderPlaced` event triggers notification + inventory while the HTTP response returns the order id.

---

### 2. Domain Events — The Foundation

```java
// A domain event is an immutable fact, named in the past tense:
public record OrderPlaced(OrderId orderId, CustomerId customerId,
                          Money total, Instant occurredAt) {}

// Aggregate publishes events as part of its business logic:
@Entity
class Order {
    @Id OrderId id;
    List<DomainEvent> events = new ArrayList<>();   // in-memory, transient

    void place() {
        // ... business rules ...
        events.add(new OrderPlaced(id, customerId, total, Instant.now()));
    }
}

// Application service builds the durable events and publishes them (outbox, Q223):
@Service
class PlaceOrderService {
    @Transactional
    OrderId place(PlaceOrderCmd cmd) {
        Order order = new Order(cmd);
        orderRepository.save(order);
        orderPublisher.publish(order.events());    // transactional outbox → Kafka
        return order.getId();
    }
}
```

| Property | Rule |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Past tense** | `OrderPlaced`, `PaymentFailed` — it already happened |
| **Immutable** | A record of a fact — never mutated |
| **Self-contained** | Carries enough data for consumers (id, amounts, timestamps) |
| **Stored durably** | Killed process must not lose the fact → transactional outbox (Q223) |
| **Idempotent consumers** | Re-delivery (at-least-once, Q212) must be safe |

---

### 3. Event Sourcing — The Events Are the Source of Truth

```
Traditional (state-snapshot):
  Orders table: [id=5, status=CANCELLED, total=100]
  → only the CURRENT state; history (who changed what, when) is lost or logged elsewhere

Event sourcing:
  events table: [OrderPlaced(id=5,100), PaymentCharged(id=5), OrderCancelled(id=5)]
  → current state is a FUNCTION of the event stream; replay events → rebuild state
  → full audit log, time travel, no "lost history"
```

| Pattern | How state is stored | Pros | Cons |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| CRUD | Update row in place | Simple, fast writes | No history; overwrite loses facts |
| **Event sourcing** | Append-only event log | Full audit, rebuild/replay, temporal queries | Event schema migration is hard; eventual state; replay time |

```java
// Example — BankAccount as event-sourced aggregate:
@Entity
class BankAccount {
    @Id AccountId id;
    BigDecimal balance;            // derived current state (cached)

    static BankAccount from(List<DomainEvent> history) {
        BankAccount acc = new BankAccount();           // empty
        history.forEach(acc::apply);                   // replay events → state
        return acc;
    }

    void apply(DomainEvent e) {
        switch (e) {
            case AccountOpened ev  -> balance = BigDecimal.ZERO;
            case MoneyDeposited ev -> balance = balance.add(ev.amount());
            case MoneyWithdrawn ev -> balance = balance.subtract(ev.amount());
            default -> {}
        }
    }

    void deposit(Money amount) {
        apply(new MoneyDeposited(id, amount, Instant.now()));  // apply to memory
        publish(new MoneyDeposited(id, amount, Instant.now())); // append to event store
    }
}
```

**Snapshotting:** replaying 10M events to rebuild state is slow — persist a periodic **snapshot** (state at event N), then replay only the tail from N+1. This bounds replay latency.

---

### 4. CQRS — Command and Query Responsibility Segregation

```
                    ┌────────── Command model ──────────┐
   Writes ──►       │ Appends events (Event Sourcing)    │ ──► Primary store
 (PlaceOrder)       └────────────────────────────────┘
                                  │ projected
                                  ▼
                    ┌────────── Read model ─────────────┐
   Reads ──►        │ Optimized for queries             │
 (Dashboard)        │ e.g., denormalized tables, Elasticsearch (Q225) ──► Read store
                    └────────────────────────────────┘
```

```
Why separate:
  Command side: correctness, append-only, business rules
  Query side:   fast reads, denormalized shapes, aggregations
  Without CQRS, one table must serve both — read shape compromises writes, write locks slow reads
```

```kotlin
// Command side — write API (small, expressive): 
record PlaceOrderCommand(CustomerId id, List<LineItem> lines) {}
interface OrderCommandHandler { OrderId handle(PlaceOrderCommand cmd); }

// Query side — read API (shaped for UI):
record OrderViewResponse(String id, String status, Money total,
                         List<String> displayLines, String lastEventAt) {}
interface OrderReadService { OrderViewResponse findViewByOrderId(OrderId id); }
```

| | Command model | Query model |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Responsibility | Enforce rules, append events | Serve reads, projections |
| Storage | Event store (append-only) | Denormalized views, ES index |
| Consistency | Strong within aggregate | Eventual (projection lag) |
| Scale | Scales with write volume | Scales independently (many replicas/sharded) |

**Projection lag:** the read model is updated asynchronously from events — so reads trail by milliseconds–seconds. Accept this; if a flow needs the write result returned, write-then-read-own-write as one aggregate or return the id and query with eventual tolerance.

---

### 5. When to Use (and When NOT to)

| Use EDA/Event sourcing | Prefer synchronous CRUD |
| :--- | :--- |
| Many consumers care about one fact (orders, payments, audits) | Tight, short, transactional flows |
| Audit/history is a requirement (finance, compliance) | Simple CRUD with no history needs |
| Independent team boundaries must scale/evolve separately | Small monolith where coupling is cheap |
| Temporal queries ("state as of date X") | No need to travel back in time |
| High write volume, scattered reads | Low write volume |

Event sourcing + CQRS is **complex** — schema evolution, replay, projection, idempotency. Adopt only when the benefits (audit, scale, independent read models) clearly outweigh; many teams use **plain EDA with a CRUD store** (events for integration, DB for state) and never cross into full event sourcing.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Event schema changes break older events | Replay fails on renamed field | Store a `version` in each event; versioned `apply()`; upcasters |
| No idempotency in consumers | Duplicate side effects on redelivery (Q212/Q223) | Idempotency key + dedup on event id |
| Full replay too slow | Hours to rebuild state on new read model | **Snapshots** + replay tail; keep event store compact |
| Command and query share one table | Write locks slow reads, read shapes pollute writes | CQRS separation; projections to read store |
| Emitting events outside the DB TX | Event lost when TX rolls back | Transactional outbox (Q223) |
| Using event sourcing for everything | Unnecessary complexity for a small CRUD domain | Plain EDA + CRUD; adopt ES only where history matters |

### Summary

- **Event-Driven Architecture** treats facts as messages: services publish events (Kafka/RabbitMQ, Q212/Q224) and react — decoupling, async scaling, independent availability; HTTP stays for inline operations.
- **Domain events** (past-tense, immutable, self-contained) published via **transactional outbox** (Q223) guarantee no lost facts; consumers must be **idempotent**.
- **Event sourcing** stores state as an append-only event stream — full audit, replay, time travel — with **snapshots** to bound replay; effective when history and compliance matter.
- **CQRS** separates a correctness-focused command model from optimized, denormalized read models (tables, ES) — projections converge eventually, so design for **eventual consistency**.

---

## 235. How does Docker Compose help with local development and how does it compare to Kubernetes? Explain compose files, service networking, and when to use each.

Docker Compose defines **multi-container apps as YAML** for a single host — perfect for local development and small deployments. Kubernetes orchestrates **container fleets across many hosts** with self-healing, scaling, and rolling updates. The same Dockerfile (Q215) powers both: Compose for dev-loop speed, Kubernetes for production-grade orchestration.

---

### 1. Docker Compose — Declaring the Dev Environment

```yaml
# docker-compose.yml — three services, one command (`docker compose up`):
version: "3.8"

services:
  postgres:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: orders
      POSTGRES_USER: app
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: devpass
    ports: ["5432:5432"]
    volumes: [pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data]
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U app"]
      interval: 5s
      retries: 5

  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    ports: ["6379:6379"]

  kafka:
    image: bitnami/kafka:3.7
    environment:
      KAFKA_CFG_NODE_ID: "0"
      KAFKA_CFG_PROCESS_ROLES: controller,broker
      KAFKA_CFG_LISTENERS: CLIENT://:9092,CONTROLLER://:9093
      KAFKA_CFG_ADVERTISED_LISTENERS: CLIENT://localhost:9092
      KAFKA_CFG_LISTENER_SECURITY_PROTOCOL_MAP: CLIENT:PLAINTEXT,CONTROLLER:PLAINTEXT
      KAFKA_CFG_AUTO_CREATE_TOPICS_ENABLE: "true"
    ports: ["9092:9092"]

  order-service:
    build: .                              # build from local Dockerfile (Q215)
    image: order-service:dev
    depends_on:
      postgres: { condition: service_healthy }
      kafka:    { condition: service_started }
      redis:    { condition: service_started }
    environment:
      SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE: dev
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/orders
      SPRING_KAFKA_BOOTSTRAP_SERVERS: kafka:9092
      SPRING_DATA_REDIS_HOST: redis
    ports: ["8080:8080"]

volumes:
  pgdata:
```

```bash
docker compose up -d          # start all services
docker compose logs -f order-service   # tail logs
docker compose ps             # status per service
docker compose down -v        # stop + remove volumes (fresh DB)
docker compose up --build     # rebuild app image after code change
```

| Compose concept | Meaning |
| :--- | :--- |
| `services` | Each container (postgres, kafka, order-service...) |
| `ports` | Host→container mapping (`5432:5432`) |
| `volumes` | Persistent data (survives container restart) |
| `depends_on` | Startup order — with `condition: service_healthy` waits for real readiness |
| `networks` | Default local network — services reach each other by **service name** (`jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432`) |
| `environment` | Injects env vars (externalized config, Q218) |

---

### 2. Development Workflow — Hot Reload with Compose

```
Local dev loop:
  edit Java code → ./mvnw spring-boot:run (or devtools) → auto-restart
  DB/Kafka/Redis run in Compose → app runs on host (or in Compose with mounted bind)

Two common setups:
  1. Everything in Compose (app builds inside) — matches prod closely
  2. Infra in Compose + app on host IDE (devtools hot reload) — fastest iteration
```

```yaml
# Bind-mount your source for a dev container with live reload (spring-boot-devtools):
  order-service:
    build: .
    volumes:
      - ./src:/app/src               # live code → container
      - ~/.m2:/root/.m2:ro           # Maven cache
    command: ["./mvnw", "spring-boot:run", "-Dspring-boot.run.profiles=dev"]
```

```bash
docker compose down && docker compose up -d    # full stack reset in ~15s
docker compose restart order-service            # single service reload
```

Compose is the fastest way to get a **realistic local environment** (real Postgres, real Kafka) without managing a cluster — combined with Testcontainers (Q209) for tests, it covers the whole local story.

---

### 3. Kubernetes vs Docker Compose — Same Containers, Different Scope

```
Docker Compose: one host, manual scaling, no self-healing, no rolling updates — OK for dev/small
Kubernetes:     fleet of hosts, auto-scaling/scaling, self-healing, rolling updates, service discovery

                Compose                        Kubernetes
  Hosts        1 node                           cluster of N nodes
  Scaling      manual `--scale` (single host)   HorizontalPodAutoscaler
  Self-heal    no (restart policy only)         restart/evict/replace unhealthy pods
  Rolling      docker compose up --build        Deployment rollout (Q226)
  Discovery    service-name DNS (per compose)   Service + DNS (Q233)
  Secrets      env vars / .env                  Secrets
  Config       env vars                         ConfigMaps + Secrets
  Networking   compose network                  Overlay network + NetworkPolicies
  Storage      volumes (one node)               PersistentVolumes (shared/clustered)
  When to use  local dev, small demo, single VM production      real production, HA, scale
```

| Aspect | Docker Compose | Kubernetes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Setup effort | Minutes | Hours–days (minikube/k3s easier) |
| Resource footprint | Single daemon | Many components, needs >3 nodes for HA |
| Learning curve | Low | High |
| Dev loop | Excellent | Heavy — use for prod-like testing only |
| Production readiness | Limited (no self-heal/scale) | Yes (rolling, autoscaling, HA) |
| Ideal use | Local dev, CI, demos | Production clusters |

---

### 4. When to Use Which

```
Use Docker Compose:
  ✓ Local development (DB, Kafka, Redis alongside your app)
  ✓ CI smoke environment
  ✓ Small single-host deployments (demo, personal projects)
  ✗ Production at scale (needs HA, scaling, self-healing)

Use Kubernetes:
  ✓ Production microservices (HA, autoscaling, rolling updates, probes Q215)
  ✓ Teams with the platform skill
  ✗ Local dev for a single app (overkill — Compose is faster)
  ✗ When you need a quick feedback loop

Common hybrid:
  Compose locally → Kubernetes in prod (same Dockerfile, different config)
  "It works in my Compose" ≈ "it will work on K8s" (same image, Q215)
```

```yaml
# The same service — Compose profile vs K8s deployment:
# Compose (env-driven, Q218):
spring.datasource.url: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/orders
# K8s (ConfigMap/Secret-driven):
spring.datasource.url: jdbc:postgresql://postgres.default.svc:5432/orders
# Both resolve "postgres" to the DB — keep config external (12-factor, Q218)
```

---

### 5. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Start DB and app simultaneously | App crashes “connection refused” | `depends_on: postgres: condition: service_healthy` with a real healthcheck |
| Rebuilding image for every code change | Slow dev loop | Bind-mount `./src` + devtools; or run the app on host, infra in Compose |
| `docker compose down` wiping data | Dev DB data lost | Named volumes (persist), don't use anonymous volumes |
| Expected local==prod parity | Works locally, breaks in prod | Same Dockerfile/image both places; externalize config (Q218/Q226) |
| Using K8s for a single dev container | Overkill, slow iteration | Compose locally; K8s only for prod-like environments |
| Mounting secrets into images | Secrets baked into layers (Q215) | Env vars / volumes / Vault (Q218) — never `COPY` secrets |

### Summary

- **Docker Compose** declares multi-container infra as YAML (`services`/`ports`/`volumes`/`depends_on` + healthchecks) — `docker compose up` gives a real Postgres+Kafka+Redis+app stack on one host in minutes; services reach each other by **service name**.
- Compose supports **bind-mount + devtools hot reload** for a fast local loop and is ideal for **local dev, CI, demos, and small single-host deployments**.
- **Kubernetes** orchestrates across many hosts with **self-healing, autoscaling, rolling updates, Service discovery (Q233), probes (Q215)** — the production-grade layer; using it for local dev is usually overkill.
- **Hybrid**: the same Dockerfile (Q215) runs under Compose locally and K8s in prod — keep config external (Q218) so the only difference is how config is injected.

---

## 236. How does JWT-based authentication flow work end-to-end in Spring? Explain filters, AuthenticationManager, token validation, and revocation.

JWT authentication is a chain of decisions: a **filter** intercepts the request, a **resolver** extracts the `Authorization: Bearer` token, a **decoder+validator** checks signature/expiry/issuer, an **AuthenticationManager** loads the principal into the SecurityContext, and **method security** (Q207) enforces roles on the downstream call. Understanding this flow — and how to revoke a stateless token — matters for any production OAuth2/JWT setup (Q231).

---

### 1. The Request Flow, Step by Step

```
HTTP request (Authorization: Bearer eyJ...)
  │
  1. SecurityFilterChain (filter chain, Q207)
  2. JwtAuthenticationFilter (once per request)
  3. BearerTokenResolver  → extracts token from header
  4. NimbusJwtDecoder     → verifies signature, exp, iss, aud, jti
  5. If valid: build JwtAuthenticationToken (principal = JWT claims, authorities from scopes/roles)
  6. SecurityContextHolder set (ThreadLocal) → authenticated
  7. Controller runs; @PreAuthorize checks roles from the JWT
  │
  On any validation failure → AuthenticationEntryPoint → 401
```

```java
@Configuration
@EnableMethodSecurity                     // enables @PreAuthorize/@Secured/@RolesAllowed
class SecurityConfig {

    @Bean
    SecurityFilterChain filterChain(HttpSecurity http, JwtDecoder decoder) throws Exception {
        http
            .csrf(csrf -> csrf.disable())          // REST API — no cookies
            .sessionManagement(sm -> sm.sessionCreationPolicy(SessionCreationPolicy.STATELESS))
            .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
                .requestMatchers("/api/public/**").permitAll()
                .anyRequest().authenticated())
            .oauth2ResourceServer(oauth -> oauth
                .jwt(jwt -> jwt.decoder(decoder)
                    .jwtAuthenticationConverter(jwtAuthenticationConverter())));
        return http.build();
    }

    @Bean
    JwtDecoder jwtDecoder() {
        return NimbusJwtDecoder.withJwkSetUri("https://auth.example.com/.well-known/jwks.json")
            .build();
    }

    @Bean
    JwtAuthenticationConverter jwtAuthenticationConverter() {
        JwtAuthenticationConverter converter = new JwtAuthenticationConverter();
        converter.setJwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter(jwt -> {
            List<String> roles = jwt.getClaimAsStringList("roles");   // ["ROLE_ADMIN","USER"]
            if (roles == null) return List.of();
            return roles.stream().map(SimpleGrantedAuthority::new).toList();
        });
        return converter;
    }
}
```

**Stateless:** no `HttpSession` — the request is either authenticated by the JWT or it isn't. Every instance can validate the token independently (no shared session store).

---

### 2. Bearer Token Resolution — Where the Token Comes From

```java
@Component
class MyBearerTokenResolver implements BearerTokenResolver {
    @Override
    public String resolve(HttpServletRequest request) {
        // Default reads "Authorization: Bearer <token>".
        // Custom: support header, query param, or cookie:
        String header = request.getHeader("Authorization");
        if (header != null && header.startsWith("Bearer ")) return header.substring(7);

        String fromCookie = request.getCookies() == null ? null : Arrays.stream(request.getCookies())
            .filter(c -> "access_token".equals(c.getName())).map(Cookie::getValue).findFirst().orElse(null);
        return fromCookie;    // or throw new BadCredentialsException("No bearer token")
    }
}
```

Why it matters: mobile/web clients may send tokens differently (header vs cookie), and **single-sign-on** across micro-frontends often relies on a shared cookie-read.

---

### 3. Token Validation — What Could Go Wrong

| Validation | How | Failure |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Signature** | Verify with public key from JWKS | Forged token → 401 |
| **Expiry (`exp`)** | `JwtTimestampValidator` | Expired token → 401 |
| **Issuer (`iss`)** | Custom `JwtIssuerValidator` | Token from wrong auth server → 401 |
| **Audience (`aud`)** | Custom validator | Token meant for another service → 401 |
| **Not-before (`nbf`)** | Default validator | Too-early token → 401 |

```java
@Bean
JwtDecoder jwtDecoder() {
    NimbusJwtDecoder decoder = NimbusJwtDecoder.withJwkSetUri("https://auth/.well-known/jwks.json")
        .build();
    decoder.setJwtValidator(new DelegatingOAuth2TokenValidator<>(
        JwtValidators.createDefault(),                    // exp + nbf
        new JwtIssuerValidator("https://auth.example.com"),
        jwt -> jwt.getAudience().contains("order-service")
            ? ValidationResult.success()
            : new ValidationResult(List.of(new OAuth2Error("invalid_aud", "wrong audience", null)))));
    return decoder;
}
```

Remote JWKS keys are **cached** — the decoder fetches the key set once and reuses it, so validation is a local cryptographic check, not a network round-trip per request.

---

### 4. Revoking a Stateless Token

The JWT is valid until `exp` — a logged-out user, or an employee who left, keeps access until then. Revocation is **trading statelessness for control**:

```java
// Approach 1 — Blocklist (Redis) — check on every request:
@Component
class RevokedTokenFilter extends OncePerRequestFilter {
    @Autowired RedisTemplate<String, String> redis;

    @Override protected void doFilterInternal(HttpServletRequest req, HttpServletResponse res, FilterChain chain)
            throws ServletException, IOException {
        String token = new DefaultBearerTokenResolver().resolve(req);
        if (token != null && Boolean.TRUE.equals(redis.hasKey("revoked:" + sha256(token)))) {
            throw new BadCredentialsException("Token revoked");
        }
        chain.doFilter(req, res);
    }
}

// Approach 2 — Allowlist for short-lived tokens:
//   сервера store "active sessions" (jti → user) in Redis; filter checks membership (faster positives, worse if huge)

// Approach 3 — Short-lived access token (5 min) + refresh token revocation:
//   Auth server revokes the refresh token (in its DB/Redis) → the next refresh fails
//   Access token expiry is naturally ≤ 5 min — worst-case stale window is small
```

| Approach | Trade-off |
| :--- | :--- |
| Short TTL + refresh | Small stale window, no per-request lookup — **recommended default** |
| Blocklist (Redis `revoked:{hash}`) | Immediate revoke for banned users; adds a Redis hit per request |
| Disable key when rotated (JWKS) | Implicitly revokes all tokens signed with old key — heavy |

Choose: normal users → short TTL + refresh revocation; suspected compromise → immediate Redis blocklist entry keyed by `jti`; rotated key → JWKS rollover.

---

### 5. Testing Authenticated Endpoints

```java
@SpringBootTest @AutoConfigureMockMvc
class OrderControllerSecuredTest {

    @Test @WithMockUser(roles = "ADMIN")
    void adminCanDelete() throws Exception {
        mockMvc.perform(delete("/api/v1/orders/42"))
            .andExpect(status().isNoContent());
    }

    // Real JWT — build a signed token in the test:
    @Test
    void validJwtAuthorizes() throws Exception {
        String token = jwt();  // signed with a test JWK
        mockMvc.perform(get("/api/v1/orders/42")
                .header("Authorization", "Bearer " + token))
            .andExpect(status().isOk());
    }
}
```

`@WithMockUser` mocks the SecurityContext (fast); JWT integration tests verify the real decoder/validator wiring.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Only validating signature, not `iss`/`aud` | Accepted tokens minted by another server or for another service | `JwtIssuerValidator` + audience check (Q231) |
| Long-lived access token (days) | Account takeover window is huge if stolen | 5–15 min access token + refresh token with revocation |
| No revocation path | Fired user still accesses for hours/days | Revoke refresh token + Redis blocklist for emergencies |
| Storing JWT in HttpSession anyway | Contradicts stateless; adds session store dependency | `STATELESS` + bearer resolution from header/cookie |
| Caching JWKS with long refresh | New key not picked up after rotation (401s) | Respect `Cache-Control` of JWKS endpoint; short TTL on key cache |
| Roles in a custom claim but not mapped | `hasRole` never matches | Map the `roles`/`scope` claim via `JwtAuthenticationConverter` |

### Summary

- JWT flow = **BearerTokenResolver → JwtDecoder (signature/exp/iss/aud) → JwtAuthenticationConverter (claims → authorities) → SecurityContext** — stateless, no session, validated locally with cached JWKS keys.
- Validate **signature, `exp`, `iss`, `aud`, `nbf`** with `DelegatingOAuth2TokenValidator`; map the `roles` claim into `GrantedAuthority`s so `@PreAuthorize` (Q207) works.
- **Revocation** trades statelessness for control: short-lived access + revocable refresh (default), or Redis blocklist by `jti` when you need immediate revoke.
- Test with `@WithMockUser` for unit speed and real signed JWTs for end-to-end decoder validation; never trust a token without full validation (Q231).

---

## 237. How do you build database sharding and horizontal scaling in Spring Boot? Explain partitioning, sharding, connection routing, and consistency trade-offs.

A single database has a ceiling — when reads and writes exceed one node, you **partition** the data. Two strategies: **partitioning** splits a big table into smaller tables on the same DB (management win), **sharding** splits data across **multiple physical databases** (scale win). Spring with `@Shard`, `AbstractRoutingDataSource`, and libraries like ShardingSphere route each request to the right shard by a **shard key**.

---

### 1. Partitioning vs Sharding — What's the Difference

```
Partitioning (same database, same schema):
  orders —> orders_2024, orders_2025 (by range)
  Queries hit only the relevant partition — faster, easier to drop old partitions
  Still one machine, one architecture — doesn't scale write throughput beyond a node

Sharding (multiple databases, same schema):
  shard_0: orders where customer_id % 4 == 0
  shard_1: orders where customer_id % 4 == 1
  shard_2: orders where customer_id % 4 == 2
  shard_3: orders where customer_id % 4 == 3
  Each shard is a full DB → total capacity = 4× writes, 4× storage
```

| | Partitioning | Sharding |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Splits at | Table row level (same DB) | Across physical databases |
| Solves | Query performance, data lifecycle | Total scale (writes, storage, capacity) |
| Single point | One DB still the ceiling | No single DB bottleneck |
| Complexity | Low (native `PARTITION BY`) | High (router, rebalancing, joins across shards) |

---

### 2. Choosing the Shard Key — The Most Important Decision

```
Good shard key: high cardinality, evenly distributed, included in most queries
  customer_id (orders)   → uniform spread via hash → good
  country (users)         → "US" is 40% → ONE hot shard → bad

Route query by shard key: SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 42
  -> shard = hash(42) % 4 → shard_2 → only shard_2 is queried (1/4 of data)
```

```java
// Deterministic shard function (consistent hashing avoids re-hash storms):
int shardForKey(String key, int shardCount) {
    int hash = key.hashCode() & 0x7fffffff;      // non-negative
    return hash % shardCount;                    // shard index
    // 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF % shardCount alternatives: use a 64-bit hash for larger clusters
}
```

Rule: every query **must** include the shard key, or you do a **scatter-gather** (query all shards and merge) — expensive. Design APIs around the shard key (e.g., "all endpoints take `customerId`").

---

### 3. Spring Routing with `AbstractRoutingDataSource`

```java
@Configuration
class ShardRoutingConfig {

    // Map shard index → DataSource:
    @Bean
    AbstractRoutingDataSource dataSource(
            DataSource shard0, DataSource shard1, DataSource shard2, DataSource shard3) {
        Map<Object, Object> targets = new HashMap<>();
        targets.put(0, shard0);
        targets.put(1, shard1);
        targets.put(2, shard2);
        targets.put(3, shard3);

        AbstractRoutingDataSource dyn = new AbstractRoutingDataSource() {
            @Override protected Object determineCurrentLookupKey() {
                return ShardContextHolder.getShard();   // set per request by the shard key
            }
        };
        dyn.setTargetDataSources(targets);
        dyn.setDefaultTargetDataSource(shard0);
        return dyn;
    }
}

// ThreadLocal holds the active shard for this request:
public final class ShardContextHolder {
    private static final ThreadLocal<Integer> SHARD = new ThreadLocal<>();
    public static void setShard(int s) { SHARD.set(s); }
    public static Integer getShard() { return SHARD.get(); }
    public static void clear() { SHARD.remove(); }        // ALWAYS clear (Q211/Q227 leak pitfall)
}

// Filter computes the shard from the URL and routes the whole request:
@Component
class ShardRoutingFilter extends OncePerRequestFilter {
    @Override protected void doFilterInternal(HttpServletRequest req, HttpServletResponse res, FilterChain chain)
            throws ServletException, IOException {
        try {
            String id = req.getParameter("customerId");
            if (id != null) ShardContextHolder.setShard(shardForKey(id, 4));
            chain.doFilter(req, res);
        } finally {
            ShardContextHolder.clear();                 // prevent ThreadLocal leak across requests
        }
    }
}
```

```
customerId=42 → filter computes 42 % 4 = 2 → routing DataSource → connection on shard_2
             → repository runs SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id=42 against shard_2
```

With Spring Data JPA, the repository stays unchanged — the **DataSource** abstraction (not the entity code) does the routing. This is how `@Shard`-style annotations or ShardingSphere bytecode instrumentation work.

---

### 4. Cross-Shard Problems — Joins, Transactions, Sequences

| Problem | Why it breaks | Mitigation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joins across shards** | Row for customer A is on shard 1, their order on shard... | Denormalize; duplicate dimension data per shard; application-side join (scatter-gather) |
| **Global uniqueness** | Auto-increment IDs collide across 4 DBs | Snowflake ID (64-bit: timestamp + machine + sequence) or UUID; DB sequence per shard with offset |
| **Transactions across shards** | Distributed TX (Q223) — no single DB to commit against | Keep one aggregate on one shard; Saga/outbox (Q223) for cross-shard flows |
| **Rebalancing when adding a shard** | `hash % N` changes → most keys move | **Consistent hashing** (only 1/N of keys move); or double hashing to a virtual bucket space |

```java
// Snowflake-like ID generator (orderId) — globally unique, sortable:
class SnowflakeId {
    private final long epoch = 1288834974657L;      // Twitter epoch
    private final long machineId;                    // per instance
    private long sequence = 0, lastTs = -1;

    synchronized long nextId() {
        long ts = System.currentTimeMillis();
        if (ts == lastTs) sequence = (sequence + 1) & 4095;      // 12 bits seq
        else { sequence = 0; lastTs = ts; }
        return (ts - epoch) << 22 | machineId << 12 | sequence; // 41+10+12 = 63 bits
    }
}
```

Rule: **one aggregate per shard** — design so orders + their lines + customer live on the same shard (shard key shared among tables), minimizing cross-shard work.

---

### 5. Consistency Trade-offs

```
Single shard: strong consistency within the shard (ACID, Q204)
Across shards: no global ACID — you own consistency
  • Cross-shard update → saga (Q223): local TX per shard + compensation
  • Exactly-once propagation → outbox (Q223) per source shard
  • Read-your-own-write → route by the SAME shard key used to write
  • Search across all shards → dedicated read model (Elasticsearch, Q225) fed by events
```

| Requirement | Approach |
| :--- | :--- |
| ACID within business aggregate | One shard hosts all tables of the aggregate (shared shard key) |
| Eventual consistency across shards | Saga + outbox + idempotent consumers (Q223) |
| Cross-shard search/aggregates | Project events to a read store (Q225/Q234 CQRS) |
| Reporting/dashboards | Dedicated analytics DB via CDC (outbox, Q223) — never query all shards in prod path |

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Bad shard key (uneven distribution) | One shard saturated, others idle | Hash-based shard key with high cardinality |
| Queries without the shard key | Scatter-gather on every request, slow, expensive | Enforce shard key in every API/repository path |
| Modulo sharding then adding a node | Massive data migration (re-hash of most keys) | Consistent hashing / virtual buckets |
| Auto-increment shared across shards | PK collisions | Snowflake/UUID (above) |
| `ThreadLocal` shard not cleared | Leak → wrong shard on a pooled thread (Q227) | `try/finally { ShardContextHolder.clear(); }` |
| Cross-shard TX implemented naively | Distributed locks, contention, SPOF (Q223) | Aggregates per shard; saga/outbox for the rest |

### Summary

- **Partitioning** splits one table within one DB (`PARTITION BY range`) — management and query wins; **sharding** splits across **multiple physical DBs** via a shard key — real horizontal scale at the cost of joins/TX.
- **Routing** in Spring uses `AbstractRoutingDataSource` + `determineCurrentLookupKey()` with a **`ThreadLocal` shard** set by the shard key in a request filter — repositories stay unchanged.
- The **shard key** must be high-cardinality, evenly distributed, and present in every query; use **consistent hashing** for rebalance-friendly sharding, and **Snowflake/UUID** IDs for global uniqueness.
- **Consistency**: one aggregate per shard (shared shard key across tables) for ACID; cross-shard flows via **saga + outbox** (Q223); global search via projected read models (Q225/Q234).

---

## 238. How do you implement effective rate limiting and throttling? Explain token bucket, fixed window, Redis-based distributed limiting, and Bucket4j.

Rate limiting protects your API and downstreams from overload, abuse, and runaway costs. The core algorithms — **token bucket**, **fixed window**, **sliding window**, **leaky bucket** — each trade precision against memory. In Spring, **Bucket4j** implements token bucket locally; for **distributed** limits shared across instances, you need Redis-backed counters (or an API gateway, Q216).

---

### 1. The Algorithms Compared

```
Token bucket (Bucket4j):
  Bucket holds B tokens; refills at r tokens/sec.
  Each request consumes 1 token → allowed if tokens remain, else 429.
  Allows BURSTS up to B, sustains at r/sec. ← the reason it's preferred

Fixed window:
  Counter per minute; reset at minute boundaries.
  Cheap, but two requests at 59.999 and 60.000 both count in their own window → 2× burst.

Sliding window (log / counter):
  Approximates the last 60s precisely — more memory.
  Sliding window counter: weighted sum of current + previous window.
```

| Algorithm | Burst tolerance | Memory | Accuracy | Implementation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Token bucket** | Yes (B) | Low (1 refill lambda) | Precise long-run | Bucket4j, Redis + Lua |
| **Fixed window** | Up to 2× | Very low (1 counter) | Coarse | Redis `INCR` + `EXPIRE`, gateway |
| **Sliding window** | No (exact window) | Medium (log/time buckets) | Precise | Redis sorted sets, ZSET counts |
| **Leaky bucket** | No (queue drains) | Low | Precise, smooths bursts | Redis list, token bucket variant |

---

### 2. Token Bucket with Bucket4j

```xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>com.bucket4j</groupId>
    <artifactId>bucket4j-core</artifactId>
    <version>8.10.1</version>
</dependency>
```

```java
@Service
class RateLimitService {
    private final ConcurrentHashMap<String, Bucket> buckets = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();

    public Bucket getBucket(String clientKey) {
        return buckets.computeIfAbsent(clientKey, k ->
            Bucket.builder()
                .addLimit(Bandwidth.classic(10, Refill.greedy(10, Duration.ofSeconds(1))))
                //               capacity=10 bursts, refills 10 tokens/sec
                .addLimit(Bandwidth.classic(60, Refill.intervally(60, Duration.ofMinutes(1))))
                //               per-minute hard ceiling
                .build());
    }
}

// In the controller/interceptor:
@Component
class RateLimitInterceptor implements HandlerInterceptor {
    @Autowired RateLimitService rateLimit;

    @Override public boolean preHandle(HttpServletRequest req, HttpServletResponse res, Object handler) {
        String key = clientKey(req);                        // API key / user id / IP
        Bucket bucket = rateLimit.getBucket(key);
        ConsumptionProbe probe = bucket.tryConsumeAndReturnRemaining(1);
        if (probe.isConsumed()) {
            res.setHeader("X-RateLimit-Remaining", String.valueOf(probe.getRemainingTokens()));
            return true;
        }
        res.setStatus(429);                                 // Too Many Requests
        res.setHeader("X-RateLimit-Retry-After",
            String.valueOf(probe.getNanosToWaitForRefill() / 1_000_000_000));
        return false;
    }

    private String clientKey(HttpServletRequest req) {
        // prefer authenticated API key; fall back to IP:
        String apiKey = req.getHeader("X-API-Key");
        return apiKey != null ? apiKey : req.getRemoteAddr();
    }
}
```

```java
// Register the interceptor:
@Configuration
class WebConfig implements WebMvcConfigurer {
    @Autowired RateLimitInterceptor rateLimitInterceptor;
    @Override public void addInterceptors(InterceptorRegistry registry) {
        registry.addInterceptor(rateLimitInterceptor).addPathPatterns("/api/**");
        // exclude critical admin endpoints or separate limits
    }
}
```

The token bucket allows a client to burst 10 immediately then is throttled to 10/s — ideal when a legitimate client fetches 10 items at once.

---

### 3. Distributed Rate Limiting with Redis

Per-instance Bucket4j means 10 pods each allow 10/s → 100/s globally. A **shared Redis counter** enforces the true global limit:

```java
// Fixed window, atomic via Redis INCR with EXPIRE (one round-trip):
@Service
class RedisRateLimiter {
    @Autowired RedisTemplate<String, String> redis;

    boolean allow(String clientKey, int limit, Duration window) {
        String counterKey = "rl:" + clientKey + ":" + windowSeconds(window);
        Long count = redis.opsForValue().increment(counterKey);        // atomic INCR
        if (count != null && count == 1) redis.expire(counterKey, window); // first hit sets TTL
        return count != null && count <= limit;
    }

    private long windowSeconds(Duration w) { return w.toSeconds(); }
}

// Sliding window — ZSET: count entries in last 60s, add current timestamp:
boolean allowSliding(String key, int limit, Duration window) {
    long now = System.currentTimeMillis();
    long cutoff = now - window.toMillis();
    String zkey = "rl:z:" + key;
    redis.opsForZSet().removeRangeByScore(zkey, 0, cutoff);      // evict old
    Long count = redis.opsForZSet().card(zkey);
    if (count != null && count >= limit) return false;
    redis.opsForZSet().add(zkey, String.valueOf(now), now);       // add this request
    redis.expire(zkey, window);
    return true;
}
```

| Sentinel command | Pattern | Use |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `INCR key` + `EXPIRE key` | Fixed window — 2 round trips, atomic | Cheap per-client counters |
| `ZADD key member score` + `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE` | Sliding window — precise | Stricter limits |
| Redis **Lua script** (`EVAL`) | Token bucket — atomic check-and-refill | Production distributed token bucket |

For correctness under concurrency, **Lua scripts** make the whole check-and-consume atomic:

```lua
-- token bucket in Redis (simplified):
local tokens = redis.call('GET', KEYS[1])
local last = redis.call('GET', KEYS[2])
local now = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local rate = tonumber(ARGV[1])
-- (compute decay then decrement) ...
```

```java
// Bucket4j with Redisson distributed buckets:
//   bucket4j-redis + redisson → Bucket stored in Redis, shared across instances:
Bucket bucket = Bucket4j.extension(RedissonBucketBuilder.class)
    .builder()
    .addLimit(Bandwidth.classic(100, Refill.greedy(100, Duration.ofSeconds(1))))
    .build(redissonClient.getBucket("bucket:" + clientKey));
```

---

### 4. Where to Enforce — App vs Gateway

```
Application-level: precise, per-key logic, business rules — but per-instance unless Redis
Gateway-level: global by construction (all traffic passes through, Q216) — limits, WAF, generic
```

| Where | Pros | Cons |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Application (Bucket4j/Redis)** | Nuanced (per-user, per-plan), business-aware | Extra Redis hop per request; per-instance drift if not shared |
| **API gateway (Q216)** | Global, one place, cheapest to add | Coarse-grained; can't differentiate per business rule |
| **CDN/WAF** | Edge protection, filters floods early | Even coarser |

Best practice: **defense in depth** — gateway caps per-IP/per-API-key (coarse), application refines per-user-plan (precise).

---

### 5. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Per-instance bucket in a multi-pod deployment | Global limit exceeded (10 pods × 100 = 1000) | Redis-based limit or Lua script (above) |
| Counting by IP while behind a proxy | All users share one proxy IP → everyone limited together | `X-Forwarded-For` / API key / user id as the key |
| No `Retry-After` / 429 semantics | Clients hammer, no backoff signal | Return `429` + `Retry-After` header + `X-RateLimit-Remaining` |
| Redis outage = no limiting | Unbounded load during cache failover | Fall back to local bucket (degrade) or fail-open with alerting |
| Fixed window at the boundary | 2× burst at minute flip | Token bucket (Bucket4j) or sliding window |
| Forgetting to restrict admin endpoints separately | Everything shares one small limit | Distinct buckets per endpoint class (and Q207 authz first) |

### Summary

- **Token bucket** (Bucket4j) allows controlled bursts and sustains a rate — the standard; fixed window is cheapest but allows 2× at boundaries; sliding window is exact but pricier.
- **Bucket4j** (`Bandwidth.classic(capacity, Refill.greedy(rate, window))`) gives local token-bucket limiting with `Bucket`/`ConsumptionProbe`; enforce with a `HandlerInterceptor` and standard `429` + `Retry-After` headers.
- **Distributed limits** need Redis: fixed window (`INCR`+`EXPIRE`), sliding window (`ZSET`), or atomic token bucket via **Lua**; use key = API key/user (not raw IP behind proxies).
- Enforce in **layers**: gateway for coarse global caps (Q216), application for precise per-user-plan rules — and design the 429 contract so clients back off correctly.

---

## 239. How does Spring Cloud Gateway work with reactive filters and how do you build a BFF pattern? Explain routes, predicates, filters, and aggregation.

The API Gateway is the front door: routing, authn/authz, rate limits, and response shaping (Q216/Q231). **Spring Cloud Gateway** is the reactive (WebFlux-based, Q221) gateway — routes + **predicates** (match conditions) + **filters** (transform/execute). The **Backend-for-Frontend (BFF)** pattern uses a dedicated gateway per client type (web, mobile, admin) to aggregate and shape responses for exactly that client — hiding service complexity and minimizing payloads.

---

### 1. Route = Predicates + Filters + URI

```yaml
spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      routes:
        - id: order-route
          uri: lb://order-service                # load-balanced (Q233), or http://host:port
          predicates:                            # ALL must match
            - Path=/api/v1/orders/**
            - Method=GET,POST
            - Header=X-Client-Version, v2
          filters:
            - StripPrefix=1                      # /api/orders/5 → /orders/5 downstream
            - AddRequestHeader=X-Gateway, edge
            - name: CircuitBreaker                # resilience at the edge (Q213)
              args: { name: ordersCB, fallbackUri: forward:/fallback/orders }
        - id: admin-route
          uri: lb://admin-service
          predicates:
            - Path=/api/admin/**
            - Role=ADMIN                         # custom predicate (authorization)
          filters:
            - TokenRelay                         # forward oauth2 token (Q231)

spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      globalcors:                       # CORS for browser clients
        cors-configurations:
          '[/**]':
            allowedOrigins: "https://app.example.com"
            allowedMethods: [GET, POST, PUT, DELETE]
```

| Element | What it does |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Route** | id + uri + predicates + filters |
| **Predicate** | Match conditions: `Path`, `Method`, `Header`, `Query`, `Host`, `Cookie`, time windows |
| **Filter** | Global or per-route: `StripPrefix`, `AddRequestHeader`, `CircuitBreaker`, `RequestRateLimiter`, custom |
| **uri** | Where to forward — `lb://service-name` (client-side LB, Q233) or fixed host |

---

### 2. Custom Predicates and Filters (Java DSL)

```java
@Configuration
class GatewayFiltersConfig {

    // Route with a custom predicate — builds a predicate from a server req:
    @Bean
    RouteLocator routes(RouteLocatorBuilder builder) {
        return builder.routes()
            .route("orders", r -> r
                .path("/api/v1/orders/**")
                .and().header("X-Client-Version", "v2")          // combine predicates
                .filters(f -> f
                    .stripPrefix(1)
                    .circuitBreaker(c -> c.setName("ordersCB").setFallbackUri(URI.create("forward:/fallback/orders")))
                    .modifyRequestBody(String.class, String.class,
                        (exchange, body) -> Mono.just(sanitize(body))))
                .uri("lb://order-service"))
            .build();
    }

    @Bean
    GlobalFilter requestLoggingFilter() {
        return (exchange, chain) -> {
            ServerHttpRequest req = exchange.getRequest();
            log.info("{} {} from {}", req.getMethod(), req.getURI(), req.getRemoteAddress());
            long start = System.currentTimeMillis();
            return chain.filter(exchange).then(Mono.fromRunnable(() ->
                log.info("{} completed in {}ms", req.getURI(), System.currentTimeMillis() - start)));
        };
    }
}
```

Note: Spring Cloud Gateway filters are **reactive** — they return `Mono`/`Flux` (Q221), run on the event loop, and must never block. Any blocking call (JDBC) inside a filter goes to `Schedulers.boundedElastic()`.

---

### 3. The BFF Pattern — One Gateway Per Client

```
Monolith before:  Mobile app makes 8 calls to 8 endpoints to render one page (N+1 at API level!)

BFF after:        Mobile BFF aggregates those 8 calls server-side and returns ONE payload
                  Web BFF returns extra HTML/SEO data; Admin BFF has admin privileges

  ┌──────────┐      ┌───────────────┐   orders   ──► order-service
  │  Mobile  │─────►│ BFF (mobile)  │───► payments ─► payment-service
  └──────────┘      │ aggregates    │───► inventory► inventory-service
  ┌──────────┐      └───────────────┘        (1 request in, 1 response out)
  │  Web     │─────►│ BFF (web)     │───► order-service + more fields
  └──────────┘      └───────────────┘
```

```java
// BFF = a service (WebFlux, Q221) that calls downstreams reactively and merges:
@RestController
class MobileCheckoutBff {
    private final WebClient.Builder web;   // load-balanced client (Q233)

    @GetMapping("/bff/mobile/order/{id}")
    Mono<MobileOrderResponse> getOrder(@PathVariable Long id) {
        Mono<OrderDTO> order  = web.build().get()
            .uri("http://order-service/api/v1/orders/{id}", id)
            .retrieve().bodyToMono(OrderDTO.class);
        Mono<PaymentDTO> payment = web.build().get()
            .uri("http://payment-service/api/v1/payments?orderId={id}", id)
            .retrieve().bodyToMono(PaymentDTO.class);
        return Mono.zip(order, payment)                        // parallel, non-blocking
            .map(t -> new MobileOrderResponse(t.getT1(), t.getT2().status(), t.getT2().amount()));
    }
}
```

| BFF benefit | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Reduced round trips** | 8 service calls → 1 BFF call to the client |
| **Client-shaped payloads** | Mobile gets lean JSON; web gets rich JSON |
| **Per-client security** | Mobile BFF issues narrower scopes than admin BFF (Q231) |
| **Independent evolution** | Service APIs can change; BFF absorbs the translation |
| **Cost** | An extra service per client type to build/maintain |

---

### 4. Gateway vs BFF — Sizing the Front Door

```
API Gateway (shared, generic):  routing, authN/JWT (Q236), rate limits (Q238), WAF, logging
BFF (per client, business):     aggregating endpoints, DTO shaping, per-client orchestration

Common stack:
  Internet → Spring Cloud Gateway (auth, limits, routing) → BFF per client (aggregation)
                                                          → backend services
```

| | API Gateway | BFF |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Scope | All clients, generic cross-cutting | One client type, business logic |
| Owns | Routing, auth, rate-limit, logs | Aggregation, DTO shaping, orchestration |
| Code style | Filters/predicates (reactive) | Controllers + WebClient (reactive MVC or WebFlux) |
| When to add | Every service fleet needs one | When clients suffer N+1 or payload bloat |

Rule: **gateway for infrastructure**, BFF for **client-specific composition** — don't shove business aggregation into gateway filters (they become untestable Perl-like scripts).

---

### 5. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Blocking call in a gateway filter | Event loop throttled for ALL routes (Q221) | Reactive only; wrap blocking work in `boundedElastic` |
| No `StripPrefix` / WRONG path downstream | 404s from service | Match `Path` against downstream root with `StripPrefix`/`RewritePath` |
| Load-balanced URI without LoadBalancer dependency | `lb://` unresolved → error | `spring-cloud-starter-loadbalancer` on classpath (Q233) |
| BFF returning giant full entities | Same payload bloat you tried to fix | DTOs shaped per client at the BFF |
| Aggregation with blocking sequential calls | Slow BFF (sum of latencies) | `Mono.zip`/`Flux.merge` in parallel (reactive) |
| Circuit breaker missing at the edge | One slow downstream stalls the gateway | `CircuitBreaker` filter + fallback URI (Q213) |

### Summary

- **Spring Cloud Gateway** (WebFlux-based, Q221) combines **routes** = `predicates` (Path/Method/Header) + `filters` (StripPrefix, AddRequestHeader, CircuitBreaker, RequestRateLimiter, custom) → `uri` (`lb://service-name`, Q233) — all filters are **reactive** and must never block.
- **BFF** adds a per-client service (one for mobile, one for web, one for admin) that **aggregates** downstream calls reactively (`Mono.zip`/`WebClient`, Q221) into a single client-shaped response — eliminating client-side N+1 and payload bloat.
- Keep the **gateway for infrastructure** (auth/Q231, rate limits/Q238, routing) and the **BFF for client-specific composition** — and always put a circuit breaker at the edge.

---

## 240. What is the CAP theorem and how do you choose consistency vs availability? Explain AP/CP systems, quorum, and the PACELC theorem.

The CAP theorem says a distributed data store cannot guarantee **Consistency, Availability, and Partition tolerance simultaneously** — during a network partition (P), you must choose **C** or **A**. This drives every database decision: Postgres/MySQL are CP (strongly consistent), Cassandra/Scylla are AP (highly available, eventually consistent), MongoDB and Redis trade off along a spectrum. **PACELC** extends CAP to the no-partition case (latency vs consistency), and **quorum** (R + W > N) tunes how much consistency you actually need.

---

### 1. TCPAPS — The CAP Theorem

```
CAP: Consistency, Availability, Partition tolerance — pick two of three is WRONG.
Correct statement: you must pick CP or AP when the network PARTITIONS (P is unavoidable)
  • C = every read returns the most recent write
  • A = every request gets a (non-error) response
  • P = system keeps working despite message loss between nodes

When a network partition separates replicas:
  CP system: serve reads only from the node that has the newest data (some nodes return errors)
  AP system: serve reads from any node, maybe with stale data (all nodes answer)
```

```
Partition scenario (write happens on node A, B is cut off):
  CP (e.g., single-writer with quorum fail-fast):  B refuses reads → C guaranteed, A lost
  AP (e.g., Cassandra with hinted handoff):        B answers with old data → A kept, C lost
```

| System | C | A | P | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **CP** | Strong | Sacrificed during partition | Yes | Postgres, MySQL, ZooKeeper, etcd, HBase |
| **AP** | Eventual | Kept during partition | Yes | Cassandra, ScyllaDB, Riak, DynamoDB (default) |
| **CA** | Strong | Always | No (impossible in a distributed system with partitions) | — theoretical only |

**Why "CA" doesn't exist in practice:** partitions are a fact of life (network hiccups, node restarts) — if you design for no partitions, you only work when the network never splits, which is not a real distributed deployment.

---

### 2. Quorum — Tuning C vs A With R and W

Replication factor **N** (number of copies). Reads (R) and writes (W) must cover an overlap to guarantee the newest value is read:

```
Quorum condition: R + W > N
  N=3 (three replicas):
  • W=3, R=1 — write to ALL, read from Any   → strong (but write latency high)
  • W=1, R=3 — write to any, read from ALL    → strong (read checks all)
  • W=2, R=2 — majority both sides            → strong when R+W>N (default quorum)
  • W=1, R=1 — write any, read any            → WEAK, may read stale (but max availability)
```

| Config | Consistency level | Availability | Latency |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| W=3,R=1 | Strong writes | Worst for writes (need all up) | Write slow |
| W=2,R=2 (default) | Strong (majority quorum) | Good | Moderate |
| W=1,R=1 | Eventual | Best (any node answers) | Fastest |
| W=ALL | Strong | Depends on slowest replica | Slowest |

```
Cassandra example:
  CREATE KEYSPACE orders WITH REPLICATION = {'class':'NetworkTopologyStrategy','dc1':'3'};
  INSERT ... USING CONSISTENCY QUORUM;    -- wait for 2 of 3
  SELECT ... USING CONSISTENCY QUORUM;    -- R+W=4>3 → newest value always read
  The SAME data can mix: reads at ONE (fast/ap) or QUORUM (consistent/cp) per request!
```

**Key insight:** quorum makes consistency **tunable, not all-or-nothing** — the same cluster can run critical reads at QUORUM and dashboards at ONE.

---

### 3. PACELC — The Extension Nobody Mentions

```
CAP answers only "during a partition, C or A?"
PACELC adds: "if there is NO partition (Else), choose Latency (L) or Consistency (C)?"

PACELC: if P (partition) → A or C; else (no partition) → L (latency) or C (consistency)

DynamoDB/Cassandra:  PA/EL  (partition→availability, no-partition→low latency)
Postgres/Spanner:    PC/EC  (partition→consistency, no-partition→consistency)
MongoDB (default):   PA/EC  (reads from primary during partition, W=majority otherwise)
```

| System | Partition choice | No-partition choice | Typical config |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Postgres/MySQL | **PC** | **EC** | All reads from primary, WAL replication |
| Cassandra/DynamoDB | **PA** | **EL** | Tunable consistency, fast local reads |
| MongoDB | PA (secondary reads) | EC (primary reads) | `readConcern: majority` |
| Redis (cluster) | AP (failover window) | EL | Replicas serve reads |

PACELC matters in interviews and design: **"what happens when the network is fine?"** is often the more important trade-off — e.g., eventual consistency might give lower latency the other 99.99% of the time.

---

### 4. Consistency Models in Spring/DBs

| Model | Guarantee | System example | Spring view |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Strong (linearizable)** | Read sees latest write | Postgres `READ COMMITTED` primary, ZooKeeper | `@Transactional(ISOLATION_SERIALIZABLE)` — watch locks (Q204) |
| **Linearizable with tunable** | Quorum-level | Cassandra QUORUM, Spanner | Consistency set per statement |
| **Causal** | Related events ordered | DynamoDB, Riak | Events carry sequence (Q234) |
| **Eventual** | Converges eventually | Cassandra ONE, Redis replication | Async reads, outbox (Q223) |
| **Read-your-writes** | Own write always visible | Most replicated systems | Route reads to primary (shard key, Q237) |

```java
// In practice — pick per aggregate:
//  Money/orders → strong (Postgres primary, QUORUM) — financial correctness
//  Product views → eventual (Caffeine/Redis, Q229) — stale OK for a moment
//  Analytics → eventual (ES, Q225) — projections lag
```

---

### 5. Common Pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Claiming CAP = "pick 2 of 3" | Wrong answer in interviews; wrong design intent | P is a given; decide **CP vs AP during partition** |
| Run all reads at ONE for "speed" | Stale reads on critical money paths | Per-request consistency: QUORUM for money, ONE for views |
| Over-provisioning quorum (W=ALL) | Write latency = slowest replica, availability drops | R+W>N with majority is the sweet spot |
| Ignoring PACELC | Surprised that "consistent" systems have high latency | Consider the no-partition trade-off explicitly |
| Applying one consistency to everything | Either over-strong (slow) or over-week (stale everywhere) | Per-aggregate consistency tiers (Q237 shard-key design) |
| "CA system" on the whiteboard | Theoretically impossible distributed | Any real replicated store is CP or AP |

### Summary

- **CAP**: during a network partition you choose **CP** (Postgres/MySQL/ZooKeeper — refuse some reads) or **AP** (Cassandra/DynamoDB — answer from possibly stale data); partitions are a fact, so "CA" is not a real distributed design.
- **Quorum (`R + W > N`)** makes consistency tunable: `W=2,R=2` majority gives strong reads on a 3-replica cluster, while `W=1,R=1` maximizes availability — and you can mix per statement.
- **PACELC** extends CAP: when there is **no partition**, choose **Latency or Consistency** — DynamoDB/Cassandra favor latency (EL), Postgres/Spanner favor consistency (EC).
- Apply **per-aggregate tiers**: money/orders strong (primary/QUORUM), product views eventual (cache/ES), analytics eventual (projections) — one consistency setting for everything is usually wrong.

---

## 241. How do Virtual Threads (Project Loom) work and when should you use them? Explain scheduling, platform threads vs virtual threads, and Spring Boot 3.2 integration.

Virtual threads (Project Loom, final in Java 21) let one OS platform thread run **millions of logical threads** — each blocking I/O call parks the virtual thread (freeing a carrier), and the JVM resumes it when the I/O completes. This kills the classic "thread-per-request" ceiling without rewriting your blocking code into reactive style. Spring Boot 3.2+ can run its web server and executors on virtual threads with **one property** — a huge practical win for blocking JDBC-based services.

---

### 1. Platform Threads vs Virtual Threads

```
Platform thread: 1:1 with an OS thread. Each blocked on I/O costs ~1MB stack + kernel resources.
  GByte RAM ≈ 1000–2000 threads → connection pool 200 ⇒ Tomcat thread pool saturated (Q206)

Virtual thread: many-to-few, scheduled by the JVM onto a small carrier pool (os threads).
  Each virtual thread is a heap object (~few KB). Blocking parks the VT, not the OS thread.
  Memory allows millions of concurrent VTs; throughput no longer capped by thread count.
```

```
How a blocking call behaves:
Platform thread:  Thread A → JDBC call blocks → OS thread parked → 200 threads = 200 blocked = 200/200 pool used
Virtual thread:   Virtual T1 → JDBC call blocks → carrier thread resumes Virtual T2 (no OS thread wasted)
```

| | Platform thread | Virtual thread |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| OS 1:1 | Yes | No — scheduled by JVM onto carriers |
| Memory per thread | ~1MB stack | ~few KB heap |
| Max practical | ~thousands | millions |
| Blocking I/O | Wastes OS thread | Frees carrier; VT resumed later |
| CPU-bound work | Fine | **Bad** — pins/occupies carrier, no freeing |
| `synchronized`/native I/O | Fine | Can **pin** the carrier (older JDK; mitigated JDK 21+) |

---

### 2. Creating and Using Virtual Threads

```java
// Thread.ofVirtual() — build and start:
Thread v = Thread.ofVirtual()
    .name("cache-warm-1")
    .start(() -> cacheService.warm());

// ExecutorService that is auto-closed and runs on virtual threads:
try (ExecutorService exec = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    List<Future<String>> futures = IntStream.range(0, 100_000)
        .mapToObj(i -> exec.submit(() -> slowExternalCall(i)))
        .toList();
    // 100k VTs, ~one carrier pool of OS threads — no OOM, no blocking
}

// Virtual thread factory for Spring:
@Bean
ThreadFactory virtualThreadFactory() {
    return Thread.ofVirtual().name("vt-", 0).factory();
}
```

```bash
# Note: Thread.ofVirtual(), Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()
# are FINAL in Java 21 (no preview flag needed).
```

**The key insight:** total throughput on blocking I/O often goes **up**, because no carrier is ever parked waiting for a response — the JVM multiplexes far more in-flight ops than a thread pool could.

---

### 3. Spring Boot 3.2 — One Property Turns Everything Virtual

```yaml
# application.yml — Tomcat + @Async + scheduled executors use virtual threads:
spring:
  threads:
    virtual:
      enabled: true
```

```java
// What this single property switches internally:
//  • Tomcat's request-processing executor → virtual thread per request
//  • @Async executers (Q211) → virtual threads
//  • @Scheduled scheduler → virtual threads
//  Resources on the reactor for your own pools:
//   - Any @Bean(ThreadPoolTaskExecutor) still wins if defined explicitly
//   - TaskExecutionAutoConfiguration picks virtual when the property is true + no custom executor
```

```java
// Fallback executor bean — use virtual threads if available, else platform:
@Configuration
class ExecutorConfig {
    @Bean(name = "appExecutor")
    Executor appExecutor() {
        if (VIRTUAL_THREADS_AVAILABLE) {        // JDK 21+
            return Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor();
        }
        ThreadPoolTaskExecutor exec = new ThreadPoolTaskExecutor();
        exec.setCorePoolSize(20); exec.setMaxPoolSize(100);
        exec.setQueueCapacity(500);             // JDK < 21 fallback
        exec.initialize();
        return exec;
    }
}
```

With virtual threads, the old advice "size your thread pool to avoid exhaustion (Q211/Q232)" mostly disappears for **I/O-bound blocking** services — but pool sizing and backpressure still matter for Reactor/queue-limited subsystems.

---

### 4. Where Virtual Threads Excel (and Where They Don't)

| Workload | Virtual threads | Reason |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Blocking I/O (JDBC, HTTP client, Kafka, file) | **Excellent** | Blocking parks the VT, carrier keeps running other VTs |
| High concurrency, many requests | Excellent | No thread-count ceiling, bounded memory |
| CPU-bound (parsing, crypto, math) | **Poor** | VT occupies carrier without yielding — no benefit; use `parallel()` (Q221/Q227) |
| `synchronized` blocks / native methods | **Can pin** | Pinned carrier blocks; JDK 21 removes most pinning via biased-lock-free mode — still avoid long `synchronized` |
| Very long-running tasks | Fine (if I/O) | Memory-per-VT still low, but think about timeouts |

```java
// Anti-pattern — CPU-heavy work on virtual threads: no server benefit + carrier pinning risk
//   crypto  hashing, JSON parse of huge payloads → use a fixed parallel pool instead
// Pattern — blocking JDBC becomes `@Transactional` code, no reactive rewrite needed:
@Service
public class OrderService {
    @Transactional                       // still good with VT — TX is per-virtual-thread
    public OrderDTO getWithCustomer(Long id) {
        Order o = orderRepository.findById(id).orElseThrow(...);   // blocking JDBC
        return new OrderDTO(o, customerRepository.findById(o.getCustomerId()).orElse(null));
    }
}
```

---

### 5. Observability and Pitfalls

```java
// Observability — virtual threads integrate with JFR (Q227):
//   JFR event: jdk.VirtualThreadStart / jdk.VirtualThreadEnd, ThreadPark, Mount/Unmount
//   jcmd Thread.print shows virtual threads; JFR "Virtual Threads" tab
//   Micrometer: instrument blocking calls to see *where* VTs spend time
```

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| CPU-bound tasks on VTs | No speedup + possible carrier pinning | Offload CPU work to `parallel()`/fixed pool (Q221) |
| `synchronized` around long I/O | Carrier pinned under load | Use `Lock` / avoid holding locks across I/O |
| ThreadLocal use with VTs | Thousands of VTs → large memory if TL heavy (Q211) | Use Reactor Context / scoped values (Q167 covered in 176-200) |
| Relying on a huge `thymeleaf`/blocking pool size | No longer needs giant `max-post-size` tuning | Trust VT; keep pool props default |
| Pinning via native `Socket` old libs | Some carrier blocked (rare) | Upgrade libs; disable pinned behavior notes |
| Ignoring backpressure on downstream | Even VTs don't protect QoS stampede | Keep resilience4j circuit breaker + limits (Q213/Q238) |

**Rule of thumb:** adopt virtual threads when you have **blocking JDBC + moderate-to-high concurrency** and don't want a reactive rewrite — it's the least-invasive big throughput win since Java 8. Keep resilience (Q213) and observability (Q214) unchanged.

---

### 6. Common Pitfalls Table

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Enabling `spring.threads.virtual.enabled` on JDK < 21 | Ignored or startup error | JDK 21+ required; feature-detection fallback bean (above) |
| Custom executors override the flag | `@Async` still on platform threads | Check for explicit `TaskExecutor` beans overriding auto-config (Q211) |
| VT used for infinite parallelism | Downstream overwhelmed | Combine with circuit breaker/rate limit (Q213/Q238) |
| Long `synchronized` methods | Carrier pinning clusters | Refactor to `ReentrantLock` or lock-free data |
| Assumed "no pool sizing needed at all" | Queues blow up on other resources | Pool RDBMS connection pool regardless (Q232); timeouts+bounds everywhere |

### Summary

- **Virtual threads** (Java 21, final) are lightweight logical threads scheduled by the JVM onto a small **carrier pool** — blocking I/O parks the VT and frees the carrier, removing the thread-per-request ceiling and ~1MB-stack cost of platform threads.
- Use `Thread.ofVirtual()` / `Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()`; in Spring Boot 3.2 **one property** `spring.threads.virtual.enabled: true` makes Tomcat, `@Async`, and `@Scheduled` run on virtual threads with zero code changes.
- Virtual threads excel at **blocking I/O** (JDBC, clients, Kafka) — huge cheap concurrency for existing blocking code; they are **poor for CPU-bound** work and long `synchronized` blocks (carrier pinning).
- Keep resilience (Q213), connection pooling (Q232), rate limits (Q238), and observability (Q214) — virtual threads change the threading model, not the need for bounds; adopt as the least-invasive throughput fix for blocking-JDBC services.

---

## 242. How do you implement idempotency and exactly-once semantics across distributed systems? Explain message deduplication, idempotent consumers, and the outbox pattern.

In distributed systems, retries are inevitable — network failures, crashes, or broker redeliveries mean a message or API call may be processed more than once. **Exactly-once** is often impossible without coordination, so the practical goal is **at-least-once delivery + idempotent processing**. Combined with **transactional outbox** (Q223), **consumer deduplication**, and producer **idempotence** (Kafka, Q212), you can achieve **effectively exactly-once semantics** end-to-end.

---

### 1. Delivery Semantics — At-Most / At-Least / Exactly-Once

| Semantics | Guarantee | When it fails | Implementation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **At-most-once** | Message delivered ≤ 1 time | Lost on crash after ack before processing | Auto-ack before processing |
| **At-least-once** | Message delivered ≥ 1 time | Duplicates on retry/redelivery | Ack after processing (Kafka manual, RabbitMQ ack after success) — default practical |
| **Exactly-once** | Delivered exactly 1 time | Hard across boundaries | Kafka EOS (transactions, Q212), or idempotent + dedup + outbox |

The pragmatic standard: **design for at-least-once delivery and make every operation idempotent**. Kafka offers true transactional exactly-once for consume-transform-produce (Q212), but across external systems (DB+S3+payments) you still need idempotency keys.

---

### 2. Producer-Side Idempotence

```yaml
# Kafka — producer idempotence (Q212):
spring:
  kafka:
    producer:
      properties:
        enable.idempotence: true   # deduplicates retries
        acks: all                  # wait for ISR
        retries: 3
```

- `enable.idempotence=true` gives **exactly-once per partition** within a producer session (no duplicate records from internal retries). Requires `acks=all`, `max.in.flight.requests.per.connection <= 5`, `retries > 0`.
- For **cross-border exactly-once** (consume→transform→produce across topics + DB), combine with Kafka transactions (`transactional.id`) and `isolation.level=read_committed` (Q212).

---

### 3. Consumer-Side Idempotency — Deduplication by a Key

```java
// Idempotent consumer pattern — dedup by a natural or event key:
@KafkaListener(topics = "order-events")
void onOrderEvent(OrderEvent evt) {
    String dedupKey = evt.getEventId();              // unique per logical event
    if (processedRepo.existsByDedupKey(dedupKey)) {
        log.debug("Skipping duplicate event: {}", dedupKey);
        return;                                       // already processed
    }
    // Process the business logic
    orderService.apply(evt);
    // Record processed key in the SAME transaction
    processedRepo.save(new ProcessedEvent(dedupKey, Instant.now()));
}
```

```java
// Ensure the dedup check + processing + save happen atomically:
@Service
class OrderEventHandler {
    @Transactional
    public void handle(OrderEvent evt) {
        String k = evt.getEventId();
```
